\mathrm{RL}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\mathrm{LRL}^{\mathrm{op}}
, and adopting the notation to
suppress mention of the involved spaces (instead of the involved sheaves
of rings), the universal property of
\Spec(A)
reads as follows: For any
local ring
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
over any locale
Y
,

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{LRL}^{\mathrm{op}}}(\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)},\mathcal{O}_{Y})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{RL}^{\mathrm{op}}}(A,\mathcal{O}_{Y}).

The morphism
A\to\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}
in
\mathrm{RL}^{\mathrm{op}}
is therefore the

universal localization
of
A
.

Proposition 12.7
.

Let
A
be a ring. The locale given by the space of prime ideals
of
A
with the flat topology is the classifying locale of prime ideals of
A
.
Equipped with
\text{\textul{$A$}}/\mathcal{P}
as structure sheaf, where
\mathcal{P}
is the generic prime
ideal, it is the universal way of mapping
A
to an integral domain in the weak
sense (as defined in Section
3.5
; with morphisms of
weak integral domains taken to be injective ring homomorphisms).

See
[
71
, Proposition 4.5]
.∎

Proposition 12.8
.

Let
A
be a ring. The locale given by the space of prime ideals
of
A
with the constructible topology is the classifying locale of detachable
prime ideals (or equivalently detachable filters) of
A
. Equipped
with
\text{\textul{$A$}}/\mathcal{P}
as structure sheaf, where
\mathcal{P}
is the generic prime ideal, it
is the universal way of mapping
A
to an integral domain in the strong sense.
Equipped with
\text{\textul{$A$}}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]
, where
\mathcal{F}
is the generic filter, it is the
universal way of mapping
A
to a local ring in which invertibility is
decidable.

This is mostly covered
in
[
71
, p. 253]
.∎

In constructive mathematics, a subset
U\subseteq A
is
detachable
if
and only if for every element
a\,{:}\,A
, either
a\in U
or
a\not\in U
. While
intuitionistically the complement of a filter might fail to be a prime ideal
and the complement of a prime ideal might fail to be a filter, the complement
of a detachable filter is a detachable prime ideal, and vice versa.

12.4.
The relative spectrum as an ordinary spectrum
\except
toc from the internal
point of view

Let
X
be a scheme and
\mathcal{A}
be a quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra.
Since
\mathcal{A}
looks like a plain algebra from the internal perspective
of
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
, we can consider its internally defined spectrum. This is a locale
internal to
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
; we might hope that its externalization is precisely the
relative spectrum of
\mathcal{A}
(considered as a locale):

E(\Spec\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{\cong}}\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}{\mathcal{A}}.

However, this turns out to be too naive. The locale
E(\Spec(\mathcal{A}))
is equipped
with a map to
X
, being an externalization of a locale internal to
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
,
and it is equipped with a sheaf of rings (because we can transport the
internally defined structure sheaf along the
equivalence
\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}(\Spec(\mathcal{A}))\simeq\mathrm{Sh}(E(\Spec(A)))
. Furthermore,
this sheaf of rings is local, since we know

\mathrm{Sh}(X)\models\ulcorner\text{{$\mathrm{Sh}(\Spec(\mathcal{A}))\models\ulcorner\text{{$\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(\mathcal{A})}$ is a local ring}}\urcorner$}}\urcorner

which by idempotency of the internal language is equivalent to

\mathrm{Sh}(E(\Spec(\mathcal{A})))\models\ulcorner\text{{$\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(\mathcal{A})}$ is a local ring}}\urcorner.

However, the map
E(\Spec(\mathcal{A}))\to X
is only part of a morphism of ringed
locales, not of locally ringed locales (even though domain and codomain happen
to be locally ringed): Internally, the morphism
(\Spec(\mathcal{A}),\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(\mathcal{A})})\to(\mathrm{pt},\mathcal{O}_{X})
of ringed locales, which is defined using the
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra
structure of
\mathcal{A}
, is not a morphism of locally ringed locales (even though
domain and codomain happen
to be locally ringed).

In contrast, the true relative spectrum
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})
is equipped with a
morphism of locally ringed locales to
X
.

It’s illuminating to compare the different universal properties
of
E(\Spec(\mathcal{A}))
and
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})
. There is a canonical
morphism
E(\Spec(\mathcal{A}))\to E(\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{X}))
of locally ringed locales (the
externalization of the canonical morphism
\Spec(\mathcal{A})\to\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{X})
given by
the
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra structure of
\mathcal{A}
), but in general, the
locales
E(\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{X}))
and
X
are not isomorphic.

As we justify below, the externalization of the internally
defined spectrum has the universal property

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{LRL}/E(\Spec\mathcal{O}_{X})}(Y,E(\Spec\mathcal{A}))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{A},\mu_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y})

for all locally ringed locales
Y
over
E(\Spec\mathcal{O}_{X})
. Here,
\mu
is the
structure morphism
Y\to\Spec\mathcal{O}_{X}
,
E(\Spec\mathcal{O}_{X})
is the locally ringed
locale associated to the internally defined spectrum of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
,
and
\mathrm{LRL}_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}
is the category of locally ringed locales internal
to
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
. In contrast, the relative spectrum has the different universal property

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{LRL}/X}(Y,\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}{\mathcal{A}})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{A},\mu_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y})

for all locally ringed locales
Y
over
X
.
23
23

23

If
X
is a scheme
and
\mathcal{A}
is quasicoherent, this universal property is well-known, even though
it’s usually only stated for schemes
Y
over
X
instead of general locally
ringed locales over
X
. In any case, we take this universal property as the
definition of what the relative spectrum should be.
The crucial
difference is that in general, the internally defined locally ringed
locale
\Spec\mathcal{O}_{X}
does
not
coincide with the internal locally ringed
locale
(\mathrm{pt},\mathcal{O}_{X})
(which is simply
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
from the external point of
view). More succinctly, the functor
E\circ\Spec
is an adjoint to the
pushforward-of-sheaf-of-functions functor
\mathrm{LRL}/E(\Spec\mathcal{O}_{X})\to\mathrm{Alg}(\mathcal{O}_{X})^{\mathrm{op}}
, while the
relative spectrum functor is an adjoint to the analogous functor
\mathrm{LRL}/X\to\mathrm{Alg}(\mathcal{O}_{X})^{\mathrm{op}}
.

The universal property of
E(\Spec(\mathcal{A}))
can be determined as follows.
From the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
, the locally ringed
locale
E(\Spec(\mathcal{A}))
looks like the ordinary locale-theoretic
spectrum
\Spec(\mathcal{A})
and therefore has the universal property

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{LRL}}(Y,\Spec(\mathcal{A}))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Ring}}(\mathcal{A},\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y}))

for any locally ringed locale
Y
.
24
24

24

Externally, this implies that for any
locally ringed locale over the underlying locale of
X
(that is, for any
locale
Y
equipped with a morphism
\mu:Y\to X
and a local sheaf of
rings), we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{LR}(\mathrm{L}/X)}(Y,E(\Spec(\mathcal{A})))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Ring}_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}}(\mathcal{A},\mu_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}).

If we restrict the right-hand side to the
set of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra homomorphisms, the left-hand side restricts to the set of
morphisms
Y\to\Spec(\mathcal{A})
of locally ringed locales over the locally ringed
locale
\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{X})
. So we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{LRL}/\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{X})}(Y,\Spec(\mathcal{A}))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}(\mathcal{O}_{X})}(\mathcal{A},\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y})).

This discussion took place in the internal universe of
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
. Externally,
the displayed universal property implies that for any locally ringed
locale
\mu:Y\to X
over
E(\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{X}))
,

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{LRL}/E(\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{X}))}(Y,E(\Spec(\mathcal{A})))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}(\mathcal{O}_{X})}(\mathcal{A},\mu_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}),

as claimed above.

Definition 12.9
.

Let
R
be a ring. Let
A
be an
R
-algebra. The
local
spectrum
of
A
over
R
is the locale
\Spec(A|R)
with frame of opens
given by

\quad\qquad\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A|R))\vcentcolon=\{\mathfrak{a}\subseteq A\,|\,\text{$\mathfrak{a}$ is a radical ideal such that}\\
\text{$\forall f\,{:}\,R\mathpunct{.}\,\forall s\,{:}\,A\mathpunct{.}\,(\ulcorner\text{{$f$ inv.{}}}\urcorner\Rightarrow s\in\mathfrak{a})\Rightarrow fs\in\mathfrak{a}$}\}.\qquad\quad

We’ll equip the local spectrum with the structure of a locally ringed locale
below. It is this refined construction which correctly internalizes the
relative spectrum:

Theorem 12.10
.

Let
X
be a scheme (or a locally ringed locale). Let
\mathcal{A}
be
an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra. Then the externalization
E(\Spec(\mathcal{A}|\mathcal{O}_{X}))
coincides
with
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})
as locally ringed locales over
X
.

Before giving the proof, we want to clarify some details of the
construction.

Firstly, the base ring
R
directly enters the construction. This is in
contrast to the usual spectrum: If
A
is an
R
-algebra, the construction
of
\Spec(A)
does not depend on the
R
-algebra structure of
A
. The algebra
structure only enters in the construction of a morphism
\Spec(A)\to\Spec(R)
.

Secondly, in the case that
X
is a scheme and
\mathcal{A}
is a
quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra, we can compare the externalization
of
\Spec(\mathcal{A}|\mathcal{O}_{X})
with the result of the construction of
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})

by gluing spectra:

Proposition 12.11
.

Let
X
be a scheme. Let
\mathcal{A}
be a quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra.
Then
E(\Spec(\mathcal{A}|\mathcal{O}_{X}))
coincides with
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})
as locales over
X
.

The condition

\forall f\,{:}\,\mathcal{O}_{X}\mathpunct{.}\,\forall s\,{:}\,\mathcal{A}\mathpunct{.}\,(\ulcorner\text{{$f$ inv.{}}}\urcorner\Rightarrow s\in\mathfrak{a})\Longrightarrow fs\in\mathfrak{a}

appearing in Definition
12.9
is precisely the internal
quasicoherence condition of Corollary
8.5
(slightly
simplified in view that
\mathfrak{a}
is a radical ideal). The sections of the
sheaf
{\llbracket{\mathcal{T}(\Spec(\mathcal{A}|\mathcal{O}_{X}))}\rrbracket}
on an open subset
U\subseteq X
are
therefore precisely the quasicoherent sheaves of radical ideals
\mathfrak{a}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}|_{U}
. Let
\pi:\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})\to X
be the canonical
morphism. If
U
is affine, then

\pi^{-1}U\cong\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})\times_{X}U\cong\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{U}(\mathcal{A}|_{U})\cong\Spec(\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}))

is affine as well and

\displaystyle\Gamma(U,\mathcal{T}(I(\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A}))))

\displaystyle=\Gamma(U,\pi_{*}\Omega_{\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})})=\Omega_{\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})}(\pi^{-1}U)

\displaystyle\cong\text{set of open subsets of\penalty\ $\pi^{-1}U$}

\displaystyle\cong\text{set of open subsets of\penalty\ $\Spec(\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}))$}

\displaystyle\cong\text{set of radical ideals of\penalty\ $\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A})$}

\displaystyle\cong\text{set of quasicoherent sheaves of radical ideals of\penalty\ $\mathcal{A}|_{U}$}

\displaystyle\cong\Gamma(U,{\llbracket{\mathcal{T}(\Spec(\mathcal{A}|\mathcal{O}_{X}))}\rrbracket}).

Therefore
I(\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A}))
is canonically isomorphic to
\Spec(\mathcal{A}|\mathcal{O}_{X})
as
locales internal to
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
. Expressed externally: The relative
spectrum
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})
coincides with the externalization
of
\Spec(\mathcal{A}|\mathcal{O}_{X})
as locales over
X
, as claimed.
∎

Thirdly, the partial order
\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A|R))
is indeed a frame. A quick
way to verify this is to recognize that it is related to the frame of opens
of
\Spec(A)
by the formula

\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A|R))=\{\mathfrak{a}\,{:}\,\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A))\,|\,\mathfrak{a}=\overline{\mathfrak{a}}\},

where
(\mathfrak{a}\mapsto\overline{\mathfrak{a}})
is the quasicoherator described in
Remark
8.16
. Since the quasicoherator
satisfies the axioms for a nucleus, this formula exhibits
\Spec(A|R)
as a
sublocale of
\Spec(A)
. In particular, suprema are computed
in
\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A|R))
by applying the quasicoherator to the suprema computed
in
\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A))
. We denote the inclusion
\Spec(A|R)\hookrightarrow\Spec(A)
by “
i
”.

Lastly, it’s interesting to know what the points of
\Spec(A|R)
are, even though these
don’t determine
\Spec(A)
.

Definition 12.12
.

Let
R
be a ring. Let
\varphi:R\to A
be an algebra. A
filter
F\subseteq A

lies over the filter of units
if and only if

\varphi^{-1}F\subseteq R^{\times}
, that is if

\varphi(r)\in F\Longrightarrow\text{$r$ is invertible in $R$}

for all
r\,{:}\,R
. (The reverse inclusion “
\varphi^{-1}F\supseteq R^{\times}
”
holds automatically.)

This definition will mostly be used in situations where the ring
R
is local,
in which case the subset
R^{\times}
is actually a filter and the phrase
“filter of units” is therefore justified.

It’s illuminating to consider Definition
12.12
in
a classical context, even though the use case we have in mind is to apply it in
the internal language of the little Zariski topos of a base scheme.
Classically, a filter
F
lies over the filter of units if and only
if
\varphi^{-1}\mathfrak{p}\supseteq R\setminus R^{\times}
, where
\mathfrak{p}\vcentcolon=F^{c}=A\setminus F
is the prime ideal associated to
F
. If
R
is local, the
set
R\setminus R^{\times}
is the unique maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
R
. Thus
F

lies over the filter of units if and only if
\mathfrak{p}
lies over the maximal
ideal.

Proposition 12.13
.

Let
R
be a ring. Let
\varphi:R\to A
be an
R
-algebra. Then
the points of
\Spec(A|R)
are intuitionistically in canonical one-to-one
correspondence with those filters of
A
which lie over the filter of units.

The correspondence outlined in
Proposition
12.3
can be adapted to the situation at hand.
A completely prime filter
K\subseteq\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A|R))
corresponds to the
ring-theoretic filter

F\vcentcolon=\{s\,{:}\,A\,|\,\overline{\sqrt{(s)}}\in K\}

and a ring-theoretic filter
F
corresponds to the completely prime filter

K\vcentcolon=\{\mathfrak{a}\,{:}\,\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A|R))\,|\,\text{$\mathfrak{a}\cap F$ is inhabited}\}.

It’s instructive to perform some of the necessary verifications, to see how
the quasicoherator is used, even though
Proposition
12.14
will subsume this
correspondence.

The filter
F
corresponding to
K
has the displayed property for the
following reason. Let
\varphi(r)\in F
. We want to verify that
r
is
invertible in
R
. Under the assumption that
r
is invertible in
R
,
it’s trivial that
1
is an element of

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\sup\{\sqrt{(1)}\,|\,\text{$r$ is invertible in $R$}\}

\displaystyle\phantom{\vcentcolon}=\{s\,{:}\,A\,|\,\text{$s$ is nilpotent or $r$ is invertible in $R$}\}\in\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A)).

Therefore, without any assumption on
r
, we have that
r\cdot 1=\varphi(r)
is an
element of
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
and therefore
\overline{\sqrt{(\varphi(r))}}\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
. Since
K
is upward-closed, it follows that
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}\in K
. Since
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
is the supremum of the set
\{\sqrt{(1)}\,|\,\text{$r$ is invertible}\}
in
\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A|R))
and
K
is completely prime, it
follows that this set is inhabited. Thus
r
is invertible in
R
.

The set
K
corresponding to a ring-theoretic filter
F
is completely prime
for the following reason. Let
\sup_{i}\mathfrak{a}_{i}=\overline{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\mathfrak{a}_{i}}}\in K
. Then
\overline{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\mathfrak{a}_{i}}}\cap F
is inhabited. By the
special assumption on
F
, the intersection
\sqrt{\sum_{i}\mathfrak{a}_{i}}\cap F
is inhabited
as well: In the case that
X
is a scheme, this follows easily using the
description of the quasicoherator given in
Proposition
8.13
. In the general case,
we use the proof scheme outlined in
Remark
8.16
– using the notation of that
remark, if
P(\mathfrak{b})\cap F
is inhabited, then
\mathfrak{b}\cap F
is as well.

A short calculation using the filter axioms then shows that there
exists an index
i
such that
\mathfrak{a}_{i}\cap F
is inhabited.
∎

Proposition 12.14
.

Let
R
be a ring. Let
\varphi:R\to A
be an algebra. Then
\Spec(A|R)
is
the classifying locale of the theory of filters of
A
which lie over the
filter of units, that is of the geometric theory with atomic propositions “
s\in F
” for
s\,{:}\,A
and axioms given by the following axiom schemes:

(1)

\top\vdash 1\in F

(2)

st\in F\dashv\vdash s\in F\wedge t\in F
(two axioms for each
s,t\,{:}\,A
)

(3)

0\in F\vdash\bot

(4)

s+t\in F\vdash s\in F\vee t\in F
(one axiom for each
s,t\,{:}\,A
)

(5)

\varphi(r)\in F\vdash\bigvee\{\top\,|\,\textnormal{$r$ is invertible in
$R$}\}
(one axiom for each
r\,{:}\,R
)

The frame of the classifying locale of the given theory
T
is the
free frame on generators “
s\in F
” for
s\,{:}\,A
subject to the relations
given by the axioms of the theory. More explicitly, it’s the Lindenbaum
algebra
L(T)
of the theory, so its elements are the formulas of the theory up
to provable equivalence and the ordering is defined by
[\varphi]\preceq[\psi]\vcentcolon\Leftrightarrow(\varphi\vdash\psi)
. We want to verify that this frame is
isomorphic to
\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A|R))
.

We define a frame homomorphism
L(T)\to\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A|R))
by sending the
generators
[s\in F]
to the radical ideal
\overline{\sqrt{(s)}}
. This
respects the relations and therefore gives a well-defined map. The map is
surjective, since a preimage to
\mathfrak{a}\,{:}\,\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A|R))

is
[\bigvee_{s\in\mathfrak{a}}(s\in F)]
. To verify that it is an isomorphism of
frames, we therefore only have to verify that it reflects the ordering.

By the axiom schemes (1) and (2), any formula of
T
is provably equivalent to
a formula of the form
\bigvee_{i}(s_{i}\in F)
. It therefore suffices to verify
that, for any families
(s_{i})_{i}
and
(t_{j})_{j}
such that

\overline{\sqrt{(s_{i})_{i}}}\subseteq\overline{\sqrt{(t_{j})_{j}}}
, the sequent

\bigvee_{i}(s_{i}\in F)\vdash\bigvee_{j}(t_{j}\in F)
is derivable. We’ll show
more generally: If
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{b}
are radical ideals such
that
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{b}}
,
then
\bigvee_{s\in\mathfrak{a}}(s\in F)\vdash\bigvee_{t\in\mathfrak{b}}(t\in F)
. This
follows from the following chain of deductions:

\bigvee_{s\in\mathfrak{a}}(s\in F)\vdash\bigvee_{s\in\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}(s\in F)\vdash\bigvee_{s\in\overline{\mathfrak{b}}}(s\in F)\vdash\bigvee_{s\in\mathfrak{b}}(s\in F).

All but the final step are trivial. The final step is an application of the
general proof scheme outlined in
Remark
8.16
. In the notation of that remark,
we set
\alpha(\mathcal{J})\vcentcolon=[\bigvee_{s\in\mathcal{J}}(s\in F)]
and exploit that, if
s\in P(\mathcal{J})
, then
s\in F\vdash\bigvee_{t\in\mathcal{J}}(t\in F)
. This is
because
s
can be written as
s^{n}=\sum_{j}a_{j}f_{j}u_{j}
such that, for each
j
,
if
f_{j}
is invertible in
R
then
u_{j}\in\mathcal{J}
, and we have the
following chain of deductions.

\displaystyle s\in F

\displaystyle\vdash s^{n}\in F

\displaystyle\vdash\bigvee_{j}(t_{j}f_{j}u_{j}\in F)

\displaystyle\vdash\bigvee_{j}(\varphi(f_{j})\in F\wedge u_{j}\in F)

\displaystyle\vdash\bigvee_{j}\bigl(\bigvee\{\top\,|\,\text{$f_{j}$ invertible in $R$}\}\wedge u_{j}\in F\bigr)

\displaystyle\vdash\bigvee_{j}\bigvee\{(u_{j}\in F)\,|\,\text{$f_{j}$ invertible in $R$}\}

\displaystyle\vdash\bigvee_{t\in\mathcal{J}}(t\in F).\qed

Lemma 12.15
.

Let
R
be a local ring. Let
\varphi:R\to A
be an
R
-algebra.
Then, intuitionistically, the locale
\Spec(A|R)
carries a canonical structure
as a locally ringed locale over
(\mathrm{pt},R)
and has the following universal
property: For any locally ringed locale
(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y})
over
(\mathrm{pt},R)
,

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{LRL}/(\mathrm{pt},R)}(Y,\Spec(A|R))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}(R)}(A,\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y})).

Since
\Spec(A|R)
is a sublocale of
\Spec(A)
, we can
equip
\Spec(A|R)
with the restriction of
\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}
to
\Spec(A|R)
as
the structure sheaf:

\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R)}\vcentcolon=i^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}=i^{-1}(\text{\textul{$A$}}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}])\cong(i^{-1}\text{\textul{$A$}})[(i^{-1}\mathcal{F})^{-1}]\cong\text{\textul{$A$}}[(i^{-1}\mathcal{F})^{-1}].

The generic filter
\mathcal{F}
was described in Section
11.2
.
The penultimate isomorphism is because localizing is a geometric construction.
Since locality of a ring is a geometric implication, this structure sheaf is
indeed a local sheaf of rings. Thus
\Spec(A|R)
is a locally ringed locale.

Next, we have to describe a morphism
(\Spec(A|R),\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R)})\to(\mathrm{pt},R)
. Locale-theoretically, this morphism is given by the unique map
!:\Spec(A|R)\to\mathrm{pt}
. The ring-theoretic part is given by the composition

!^{-1}R=\text{\textul{$R$}}\longrightarrow\text{\textul{$A$}}\longrightarrow\text{\textul{$A$}}[(i^{-1}\mathcal{F})^{-1}]=\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R)}.

This homomorphism of rings which happen to be local is indeed a local
homomorphism, that is, it reflects invertibility. More precisely,

\Spec(A|R)\models\forall f\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$R$}}\mathpunct{.}\,\ulcorner\text{{$\text{\textul{$\varphi$}}(f)$ is inv.{}{} in\penalty\ $\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R)}$}}\urcorner\Rightarrow\ulcorner\text{{$f$ is inv.{}{} in\penalty\ $\text{\textul{$R$}}$}}\urcorner.

Denoting the modal operator associated to the sublocale inclusion
\Spec(A|R)\hookrightarrow\Spec(A)
by “
\Box
”, this statement is equivalent to

\Spec(A)\models(\forall f\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$R$}}\mathpunct{.}\,\text{\textul{$\varphi$}}(f)\in\mathcal{F}\Rightarrow\ulcorner\text{{$f$ is inv.{}{} in\penalty\ $\text{\textul{$R$}}$}}\urcorner)^{\Box}

by Theorem
6.31
and
Lemma
6.23
. To verify this, let
s\,{:}\,A
and
f\,{:}\,R

be given such that
\sqrt{(s)}\models\varphi(f)\in\mathcal{F}
, that is,
s\in\sqrt{(\varphi(f))}
. We are to show that
\sqrt{(s)}\models\Box(\ulcorner\text{{$f$
is invertible in\penalty\ $\text{\textul{$R$}}$}}\urcorner)
.

The largest open in
\Spec(A)
on which
\ulcorner\text{{$f$ is invertible in\penalty\ $\text{\textul{$R$}}$}}\urcorner

holds is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\sup\{\sqrt{(1)}\,|\,\text{$f$ is invertible in\penalty\ $R$}\}

\displaystyle\phantom{\vcentcolon}=\{t\,{:}\,A\,|\,\text{$t$ is nilpotent or $f$ is invertible in $R$}\}\in\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A)),

by Lemma
11.1
. Under the assumption
that
f
is invertible in
R
, trivially
1\in\mathfrak{a}
. Therefore, without any
assumptions on
f
, we have that
\varphi(f)\in\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
.
Thus
\sqrt{(\varphi(f))}\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
and
therefore
\sqrt{(\varphi(f))}\models\Box(\ulcorner\text{{$f$ is invertible
in\penalty\ $\text{\textul{$R$}}$}}\urcorner)
. Since
\sqrt{(s)}\subseteq\sqrt{(\varphi(f))}
, the
monotonicity of the internal language implies
\sqrt{(s)}\models\Box(\ulcorner\text{{$f$ is invertible
in\penalty\ $\text{\textul{$R$}}$}}\urcorner)
.

Finally, we verify the universal property. Let
Y
be a locally ringed locale
over
(\mathrm{pt},R)
and let a morphism
A\to\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y})
of
R
-algebras be
given. We like this data to uniquely induce a morphism
Y\to\Spec(A|R)
of
locally ringed locales over
(\mathrm{pt},R)
.

To obtain a locale-theoretic map
f:Y\to\Spec(A|R)
, by
Proposition
12.14
we need to specify a
filter of
A
in
\mathrm{Sh}(Y)
which lies over the filter of units.
The given morphism
A\to\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y})
induces a morphism
\alpha:\text{\textul{$A$}}\to\mathcal{O}_{Y}
in
\mathrm{Sh}(Y)
. Since
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
is a local ring, the subsheaf
\mathcal{O}_{Y}^{\times}

is a filter. Its preimage
F\vcentcolon=\alpha^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}^{\times}
is the sought filter of
A
.
It lies over the filter of units because the composition
\text{\textul{$R$}}\to\text{\textul{$A$}}\to\mathcal{O}_{Y}
is local. By the general theory, the pullback of the generic filter
in
\mathrm{Sh}(\Spec(A|R))
to
\mathrm{Sh}(Y)
along
f
is
F
.

The ring-theoretic part of the sought morphism
Y\to\Spec(A|R)
of locally ringed
locales over
(\mathrm{pt},R)
is the canonical homomorphism

f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R)}=f^{-1}(\text{\textul{$A$}}[(i^{-1}\mathcal{F})^{-1}])=\text{\textul{$A$}}[F^{-1}]\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{Y}

of local rings.

This finishes the description of the construction. We omit further
verifications that the construction works as claimed.
∎

Remark 12.16
.

The modal operator
\Box
associated to the inclusion
\Spec(A|R)\hookrightarrow\Spec(A)
can be defined in the internal language
of
\mathrm{Sh}(\Spec(A))
. Namely, it’s the smallest operator such that
the
\Box
-translated statement

(\ulcorner\text{{the morphism $\text{\textul{$R$}}\to\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}$ is local}}\urcorner)^{\Box}

holds. It is thus the smallest operator such that for
any
f\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$R$}}
with
\text{\textul{$\varphi$}}(f)\in\mathcal{F}
,
\Box(\ulcorner\text{{$f$ is invertible
in $\text{\textul{$R$}}$}}\urcorner)
. The sublocale
\Spec(A|R)
is therefore the largest
sublocale of
\Spec(A)
on which the morphism
\text{\textul{$R$}}\to\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}
is
local.

Remark 12.17
.

In classical logic, the sublocale
\Spec(A|R)
is closed
in
\Spec(A)
, coinciding with
V(\mathfrak{m}_{R}A)
(see
Remark
12.24
). But we don’t think that
this property can be verified intuitionistically.
25
25

25

The nucleus
corresponding to the sublocale
\Spec(A|R)
is the quasicoherator
\mathfrak{a}\mapsto\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
. Assume that the sublocale is closed. Then there is a radial
ideal
\mathfrak{b}
such that
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}=\sqrt{\mathfrak{a}+\mathfrak{b}}
for all radical
ideals
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq A
. This radical ideal
\mathfrak{b}
is uniquely determined
by
\mathfrak{b}=\overline{\sqrt{(0)}}
. Thus we obtain the simple
description
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}=\sqrt{\mathfrak{a}+\overline{\sqrt{(0)}}}
for the
quasicoherator. We don’t believe that this is right in general.
For technical
reasons, it would be nice to know that
\Spec(A|R)
is an essential sublocale,
since the pullback functor admits a simpler description for essential
sublocales. It is an intersection of open, hence essential, sublocales, but the
intersection of essential sublocales needn’t be
essential
[
77
]
. We are grateful
to Guilherme Frederico Lima de Carvalho e Silva for valuable discussions and
references on this matter.

Follows immediately by interpreting the intuitionistic proof of
Lemma
12.15
in the internal language
of
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
, applied to
R\vcentcolon=\mathcal{O}_{X}
and
A\vcentcolon=\mathcal{A}
.
Then “
(\mathrm{pt},\mathcal{O}_{X})
” actually refers to the locally ringed locale
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})

and “
\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y})
” refers to
\mu_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}
, where
\mu:(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y})\to(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
is a locally ringed locale over
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
.
∎

Theorem
12.10
settles the question
how the little Zariski topos of
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})
looks like from the
internal point of view of
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
. A related question is how the big Zariski
topos looks like. We give the answer in
Theorem
16.9
.

A basic fact about the ordinary spectrum is that the ring of global sections
of
\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}
is canonically isomorphic to
A
. This is not true for the
local spectrum: A trivial example is given by any algebra over a
ring
R
which is strongly not local over
R
in the sense that there are some nonunits
of
R
which generate the unit ideal in
A
.
In this case the theory which
\Spec(A|R)
classifies is
inconsistent. Thus
\Spec(A|R)
is the empty locale and the ring of global
sections of
\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R)}
is the zero ring.
26
26

26

This failure is not
entirely unexpected, since Coste’s general result on sheaf
representations
[
45
, Theorem 5.1.1]
, which would
immediately guarantee that the global sections of
\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R)}
are in
canonical one-to-one correspondence with the elements of
A
, is not
applicable: The theory which
\Spec(A|R)
classifies is not a coherent
theory. The set-indexed disjunction appearing in axiom scheme (5) of the
description given in Proposition
12.14
can’t be
rewritten as a finite disjunction.

Proposition 12.18
.

Let
R
be a local ring. Let
A
be an
R
-algebra. The canonical
homomorphism
A\to\Gamma(\Spec(A|R),\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R)})
is an isomorphism in
any of the following situations:

(1)

The algebra
A
satisfies the quasicoherence condition given in
Theorem
8.3
.

(2)

The algebra
A
is local and the structure morphism
R\to A
is local.

Furthermore, the sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}
is a sheaf
for the modal operator associated to the sublocale
\Spec(A|R)\hookrightarrow\Spec(A)
if and only if
A
satisfies the quasicoherence condition.

We only verify the claim in the second situation. In this
case
1\in\overline{\mathfrak{a}}
implies
1\in\mathfrak{a}
for any radical ideal of
A
,
as can be checked using the proof scheme given in
Remark
8.16
.
Hence
\Spec(A|R)
is a local locale, meaning that for any covering
\sqrt{(1)}=\bigvee_{i}\mathfrak{a}_{i}=\overline{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\mathfrak{a}_{i}}}
of the top element
of
\mathcal{T}(\Spec(A|R))
, there is an index
i
such that
\mathfrak{a}_{i}=\sqrt{(1)}
.

The locale
\Spec(A|R)
thus has an initial (locale-theoretic) point. This
focal point can be explicitly described: it is the filter
A^{\times}
(which
lies over the filter of units because
R\to A
is local). As is generally the
case for local locales, taking global sections is the same as taking the stalk
at the focal point. Therefore we can conclude by the following string of
isomorphisms.

\Gamma(\Spec(A|R),\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R)})\cong\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R),A^{\times}}\cong A[(A^{\times})^{-1}]\cong A.\qed

Corollary 12.19
.

Let
X
be a scheme. Let
\mathcal{A}
be an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra.
Let
f:\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})\to X
be the canonical projection morphism.
The canonical morphism
\mathcal{A}\to f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})}
of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebras is an
isomorphism in any of the following situations:

(1)

The algebra
\mathcal{A}
is quasicoherent.

(2)

From the point of view of
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
, the algebra
\mathcal{A}
is local and the
homomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\mathcal{A}
is local. (This means that, for every point
x\in X
, the stalk
\mathcal{A}_{x}
is local and the homomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\to\mathcal{A}_{x}
is
local.)

This is just the interpretation of
Proposition
12.18
internal to
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
.
∎

Remark 12.20
.

Naively one might think that the canonical morphism
\mathcal{A}\to f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})}
of Corollary
12.19
is
the canonical morphism from
\mathcal{A}
to the quasicoherization of
\mathcal{A}
. This is not
the case. Firstly, the canonical morphism obtained by quasicoherization goes in
the other direction. Secondly, as stated in
Corollary
12.19
, the canonical morphism can
be an isomorphism even if
\mathcal{A}
is not quasicoherent.

12.5.
Comparing the different spectrum constructions

For rings and algebras, there are at least the following spectrum
constructions.

•

The ordinary spectrum of a ring, possibly realized as a locale instead of
a topological space in order to work in an intuitionistic setting:
\mathrm{Ring}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{LRS}
or
\mathrm{Ring}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{LRL}

•

The local spectrum of an algebra:
\mathrm{Alg}(R)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{LRL}/(\mathrm{pt},R)

•

(A special case of) Gillam’s spectrum of a sheaf of algebras
[
57
]
:

\mathrm{Alg}(\mathcal{O}_{X})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{LRS}/(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})

•

Hakim’s spectrum of a ringed topos
[
63
]
,
yielding a locally ringed topos:
\mathrm{RT}\to\mathrm{LRT}
.

•

Cole’s general framework for spectrum constructions
[
35
]

(also reported on at
[
75
, Theorem 6.58]
)

These are related as follows.

As described in Section
12.4
, the ordinary
spectrum construction cannot only be applied to rings, but also to sheaves of
rings and indeed ring objects internal to arbitrary elementary toposes equipped
with a natural numbers object, by employing the internal language. Applied to a
ring
\mathcal{O}
internal to such a topos
\mathcal{E}
, it yields a locally ringed locale
internal to
\mathcal{E}
, or equivalently a locally ringed localic topos internal
to
\mathcal{E}
. Externally, this corresponds to a locally ringed topos which is
equipped with a localic geometric morphism to
\mathcal{E}
.

The ordinary spectrum construction can therefore be used to turn a ringed
topos
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{O})
(with a natural numbers object) into a locally ringed topos
(which will be equipped with a morphism of ringed toposes to
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{O})
, but
which will, even if
\mathcal{O}
happens to be a local ring, not be equipped with a
morphism of locally ringed toposes to
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{O})
).

By comparing the universal properties one sees that this kind of internal
application of the ordinary spectrum construction coincides with the result of
Hakim’s spectrum construction. In fact, it can be interpreted as a simultaneous
simplification and generalization of Hakim’s construction: It’s simpler, since
it’s just the familiar spectrum construction and no explicit site calculations
are required; and it’s more general, since Hakim’s construction only applies to
ringed Grothendieck toposes whereas the internally-performed construction of
the ordinary spectrum applies to ringed elementary toposes with natural numbers
object.

Gillam’s spectrum coincides with internally performing the construction of the
local spectrum, with the caveat that Gillam’s construction starts with and
yields a locally ringed space, whereas ours starts with and yields a locally
ringed locale.
27
27

27

More generally, the local spectrum construction can be
applied to any algebra over a local ring
\mathcal{O}
internal to an elementary
topos
\mathcal{E}
with a natural numbers objects and yields a locally ringed topos
equipped with a morphism of locally ringed toposes to
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{O})
.
More
precisely:

For a locale
Y
, let
Y_{P}
be the topological space of points of
Y
, and for
a topological space
T
, let
T_{L}
be the induced locale. Let
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
be a
sober locally ringed topological space. Let
\mathcal{A}
be an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra. Then we
have a morphism
E(\Spec(\mathcal{A}|\mathcal{O}_{X}))\to X_{L}
of locally ringed locales.
Since
X\cong(X_{L})_{P}
, there is an induced morphism
E(\Spec(\mathcal{A}|\mathcal{O}_{X}))_{P}\to X
of
locally ringed spaces. The adjunction
(\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt})_{L}\dashv(\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt})_{P}
relating locales and topological spaces then yields, for any
locally ringed space
\mu:Y\to X
over
X
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{LRS}/X}(Y,E(\Spec(\mathcal{A}|\mathcal{O}_{X}))_{P})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{LRL}/X_{L}}(Y_{L},E(\Spec(\mathcal{A}|\mathcal{O}_{X})))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}(\mathcal{O}_{X})}(\mathcal{A},\mu_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}).

This is precisely the universal property which Gillam’s spectrum enjoys.

Cole’s framework for spectrum constructions is sufficiently general to
encompass both the ordinary spectrum and the local spectrum, and by extension
Hakim’s spectrum and Gillam’s spectrum. As is well-known, the ordinary spectrum
can be obtained from Cole’s framework by applying it to the geometric
theory
\mathbb{S}
of rings, its quotient theory
\mathbb{T}
of local rings,
and the admissible class
\mathbb{A}
of local homomorphisms (notation as
in
[
75
, Theorem 6.58]
). The local spectrum can be
obtained by applying it to the geometric theory
\mathbb{S}

of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebras, its quotient theory
\mathbb{T}
of local
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebras
which are local over
\mathcal{O}_{X}
, and the admissible class of local homomorphisms.
For this to make sense, one has to interpret Cole’s framework in the internal
language of
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
, since there are no external geometric theories of
(local)
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebras.

In general, the local spectrum doesn’t coincide with the usual spectrum and
Gillam’s spectrum doesn’t coincide with Hakim’s spectrum. (This was partly
observed before, for instance in
[
34
, Remark A.1.2]
.) However, if the base
space is a scheme of dimension
\leq 0
, they do coincide.

Proposition 12.21
.

Let
X
be a scheme. Then
E(\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{X}))\cong X
as locales
over
X
if and only if
\dim X\leq 0
.

The externalization of
\Spec\mathcal{O}_{X}
coincides with
X
if and only
if from the internal point of view, the locale
\Spec\mathcal{O}_{X}
coincides with the
one-point locale. By interpreting Lemma
12.5
in the
internal language of
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
, it follows that this is the case if and only if

\mathrm{Sh}(X)\models\forall f\,{:}\,\mathcal{O}_{X}\mathpunct{.}\,\ulcorner\text{{$f$ nilpotent}}\urcorner\vee\ulcorner\text{{$f$
invertible}}\urcorner.

(Internally, it always holds that
\neg(1=0)
in
\mathcal{O}_{X}
, even if
X
happens
to be the empty scheme. Therefore the lemma is indeed applicable.) By
Corollary
3.14
, this condition is equivalent to the
dimension of
X
being less than or equal to zero (i. e. to
X
being empty or
having dimension exactly zero).
∎

Corollary 12.22
.

Let
X
be a scheme. Then the relative spectrum of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebras
can be computed by the internal spectrum (instead of the internal local
spectrum) if and only if
\dim X\leq 0
.

The externalization of the internal spectrum of
arbitrary
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebras
\mathcal{A}
coincides with the relative spectrum if and
only if it coincides in the special case
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
. This is apparent by the
universal properties of both constructions. Thus the claim follows from
Proposition
12.21
.
∎

Which construction is more fundamental, the ordinary spectrum of a ring or the
local spectrum of an algebra? The ordinary spectrum
\Spec(A)
can be expressed
as the local spectrum
\Spec(A^{\sim}|\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(\mathbb{Z})})
, where
A^{\sim}
is the
induced quasicoherent algebra on
\mathrm{Sh}(\Spec(\mathbb{Z}))
. This fact is well-known
in the alternate form “
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{\Spec(\mathbb{Z})}(A^{\sim})\cong\Spec(A)
”.

Fast and loose reasoning as follows could lead one to believe that it’s
similarly possible to express the local spectrum as an ordinary spectrum.
Let
R
be a local ring. Let
\varphi:R\to A
be an algebra. The points
of
\Spec(A|R)
are those filters
F\subseteq A
such that
\varphi^{-1}F=R^{\times}
. Illicitly assuming classical logic, the points of
\Spec(A|R)
are in
canonical one-to-one correspondence with those prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}\subseteq A

such that
\varphi^{-1}\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}_{R}
. The points of
\Spec(A|R)
are therefore
in canonical one-to-one correspondence with the points of
\Spec(A\otimes_{R}k)
, where
k=R/\mathfrak{m}_{R}
is the residue field of
R
. Therefore
\Spec(A|R)

and
\Spec(A\otimes_{R}k)
might coincide.

However, we have the following negative result.
28
28

28

Intuitionistically,
it’s still true that the prime ideals of a quotient ring
A/\mathfrak{p}
are in
one-to-one correspondence with those prime ideals of
A
which contain
\mathfrak{p}
.
However, the analogous statement “filters of
A/F
correspond to those filters
of
A
which are contained in
F
” can’t be shown intuitionistically, if
A/F

is defined as
A/F^{c}
. However, informally speaking, this failure is not the
fault of the statement, but of the definition of
A/F
. The definition
raises red flags from an intuitionistic point of view, since not
F
, but only
its complement
F^{c}
enters the construction.
The statement can be salvaged by defining “
A/F
” to mean the set
A

equipped with a new
apartness relation
defined by
a\mathrel{\#}b\vcentcolon\Leftrightarrow a-b\in F
. (A basic example for a
ring-with-apartness-relation is the field of real numbers equipped with
x\mathrel{\#}y\vcentcolon\Leftrightarrow\exists q\in\mathbb{Q}\mathpunct{.}\,|x-y|\geq q>0
.) A filter
G
of this
ring-with-apartness-relation
A
is by definition a subset
G\subseteq A
which
verifies the filter axioms and which is
open with respect to the
apartness relation
in that for any elements
a,b\,{:}\,A
, the implication
a\in G\Rightarrow((b\in G)\vee(a\mathrel{\#}b))
holds.
This construction provides one of several motivations for developing the theory
of rings using apartness relations and anti-ideals; one can even define the
spectrum of a ring-with-apartness-relation. However, we’ll not pursue these
ideas further here.

Proposition 12.23
.

In general, the local spectrum of an algebra can’t be expressed as
an ordinary spectrum.

It is well-known that the ordinary spectrum is always quasicompact. The local spectrum,
however, can fail to be quasicompact. A quick way to see this is to notice
that, if that was the case, the locale-theoretic part of the projection
morphism
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})\to X
would always be a proper map of
locales
[
139
]
.

There’s also a more direct way of seeing this, which in fact proves a slightly
stronger statement. Let
X
be a scheme. Let
f\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
.
From the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
, the local
spectrum
\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{X}[f^{-1}]|\mathcal{O}_{X})\hookrightarrow\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{X}|\mathcal{O}_{X})\cong\mathrm{pt}

is the open sublocale of
\mathrm{pt}
corresponding to the truth value of “
f
is
invertible”. Explicitly, the frame of opens of
\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{X}[f^{-1}]|\mathcal{O}_{X})
is
isomorphic to
\{\psi\,{:}\,\Omega\,|\,\psi\Rightarrow\text{$f$ is
invertible}\}
.

The ordinary spectrum always has the Frobenius reciprocity property, being
quasicompact. In contrast, the locale
\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{X}[f^{-1}]|\mathcal{O}_{X})
has this
property if and only if
f
is nilpotent or invertible.
∎

Remark 12.24
.

Even in classical logic, where the local spectrum
\Spec(A|R)

and the ordinary spectrum
\Spec(A\otimes_{R}R/\mathfrak{m}_{R})
coincide as locales,
they do not coincide as locally ringed locales. The structure sheaf
of
\Spec(A|R)
, regarded as a sheaf on
\Spec(A\otimes_{R}R/\mathfrak{m}_{R})
,
is
i^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}
, where
i:\Spec(A\otimes_{R}R/\mathfrak{m}_{R})\hookrightarrow\Spec(A)
is the closed immersion corresponding to the
inclusion
\Spec(A|R)\hookrightarrow\Spec(A)
. It’s in general
not
\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A\otimes_{R}R/\mathfrak{m}_{R})}
.

Finally, we want to restate the universal properties of the ordinary spectrum
and the local spectrum in ring-theoretic language, employing the dual
categories
\mathrm{RL}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\mathrm{LRL}^{\mathrm{op}}
, as in
Section
12.3
.

Let
A
be a ring. The morphism
A\to\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}
in
\mathrm{RL}^{\mathrm{op}}
(the
ring-theoretic part of the canonical morphism
(\Spec(A),\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)})\to(\mathrm{Set},A)
) is the
universal localization
of
A
: The
ring
\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}
is local, and for any morphism
A\to\mathcal{B}
into a local
ring
\mathcal{B}
(over any locale), there is a unique local morphism
\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}\to\mathcal{B}
rendering the diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{local}\\
\text{\normalsize$\mathcal{B}$}\\
\phantom{\text{local}}\end{subarray}}}
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\normalsize$\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}$}\\
\text{local}\end{subarray}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
local

commutative. In contrast, the universal property of the local spectrum is as
follows. Let
R
be a ring. Let
A
be an
R
-algebra. The morphism
A\to\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R)}
is the universal way of turning
A
into a local ring

which is local over
R
: The ring
\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R)}
is local, the
composition
R\to A\to\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R)}
is local, and for any morphism
A\to\mathcal{B}
into a local ring (over any locale) such that the composition
R\to A\to\mathcal{B}
is local, there is a unique local morphism
\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R)}\to\mathcal{B}

such that the diagram

\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
local
local
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{local}\\
\text{\normalsize$\mathcal{B}$}\\
\phantom{\text{local}}\end{subarray}}}
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\normalsize$\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A|R)}$}\\
\text{local}\end{subarray}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
local

commutes.

Remark 12.25
.

It’s possible to state the universal property of the structure sheaf
of the big Zariski topos of a ring
A
, more precisely of the canonical
morphism
(\mathrm{Zar}(A),{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{\Spec A}})\to(\mathrm{Set},A)
of ringed toposes, in a similar manner,
employing the dual categories
\mathrm{RT}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\mathrm{LRT}^{\mathrm{op}}
of the categories of
(locally) ringed toposes. However, unlike the universal property of the
spectrum, this universal property looks slightly odd from an algebraic point of
view: For any morphism
A\to\mathcal{B}
into a local ring (over any topos
\mathcal{E}
), there is
a unique bijective homomorphism
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{\Spec A}}\to\mathcal{B}
rendering the diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{local}\\
\text{\normalsize$\mathcal{B}$}\\
\phantom{\text{local}}\end{subarray}}}
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\normalsize${\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{\Spec A}}$}\\
\text{local}\end{subarray}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
bijective

commutative. By “bijective” we mean that the ring-theoretic part
f^{\sharp}:f^{-1}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{\Spec A}}\to\mathcal{B}
of the morphism
f:(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{B})\to(\mathrm{Zar}(A),{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{\Spec A}})
is
bijective as seen from the internal point of view of
\mathcal{E}
.

12.6.
The spectrum of the generic ring

Let
\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]
be the classifying topos of the theory of
rings; explicitly, it’s the topos of presheaves on
\mathrm{Ring}_{\mathrm{fp}}^{\mathrm{op}}
, the dual of
the category of finitely presented rings. This topos contains the
generic
ring

U
(explicitly the presheaf
R\mapsto R
): any ring in any topos is the
pullback of
U
along a suitable geometric morphism.

Let
\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{LocRing}]
be the classifying topos of the theory of local rings.
Explicitly, it’s the big Zariski topos
\mathrm{Zar}(\Spec(\mathbb{Z}))
(built using one of
the
parsimonious sites
, as described in
Section
15
). This topos contains the

generic local ring

U^{\prime}
: any local ring in any topos is the pullback
of
U^{\prime}
along a suitable geometric morphism.

Let
A
be a ring. By the universal property of
\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]
, there is a
geometric morphism
g:\mathrm{Set}\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]
such that
g^{-1}U\cong A
.
Since
U^{\prime}
is in particular a ring, again by the universal property
of
\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]
, there is a geometric morphism
f:\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{LocRing}]\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]
such that
f^{-1}U\cong U^{\prime}
.
By the universal property of
\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{LocRing}]
, the topos of
sheaves over the spectrum of
A
admits a geometric morphism
g^{\prime}

to
\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{LocRing}]
such that
(g^{\prime})^{-1}U^{\prime}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}
.

The resulting solid diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Sh}(\Spec(A))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{LocRing}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Set}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]}

commutes up to a non-invertible natural transformation
\eta
; under the
equivalence

\qquad\text{category of geometric morphisms\penalty\ $\mathrm{Sh}(\Spec(A))\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]$}\simeq\\
\text{category of ring objects in\penalty\ $\mathrm{Sh}(\Spec(A))$}\qquad

this transformation corresponds to the non-invertible localization homomorphism
\text{\textul{$A$}}\to\text{\textul{$A$}}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]=\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}
. It is folklore that this square is a lax
pullback square in the 2-category of Grothendieck toposes (for instance, this
is reported on at
[
6
]
); however, this is not true.

Given a topos
\mathcal{E}
together with geometric morphisms
\tilde{f}:\mathcal{E}\to\mathrm{Set}

and
\tilde{g}:\mathcal{E}\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{LocRing}]
and a natural transformation
\tilde{\eta}:\tilde{f}^{-1}\circ g^{-1}\Rightarrow\tilde{g}^{-1}\circ f^{-1}

(these data correspond to a local ring
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}
in
\mathcal{E}
together with a ring
homomorphism
\varphi:\text{\textul{$A$}}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}
), there is a canonical geometric morphism
h:\mathcal{E}\to\mathrm{Sh}(\Spec(A))
(determined by requiring that
h^{-1}\mathcal{F}\cong\mathcal{F}_{0}\vcentcolon=\varphi^{-1}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}^{\times}]
), and this morphism renders the lower left triangle
commutative up to a natural isomorphism, but it renders the upper right
triangle commutative only up to a non-invertible natural transformation
(corresponding to the non-invertible ring homomorphism
\text{\textul{$A$}}[\mathcal{F}_{0}^{-1}]\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}
).

The observation that the square is not a lax pullback is joint with Peter Arndt
and Matthias Hutzler. The observation raises two questions: What is the lax
pullback (which exists by general theory), if it’s not
\mathrm{Sh}(\Spec(A))
?
And how can
\mathrm{Sh}(\Spec(A))
be described as a pullback? The following two
propositions answer these questions. The geometric morphism
\mathrm{Set}\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]
which they implicitly refer to is the morphism
g
mentioned
above.

Proposition 12.26
.

Let
A
be a ring. The lax pullback
(\mathrm{Set}\Rightarrow_{\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]}\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{LocRing}])
is the big Zariski topos of
\Spec(A)
(built using one of the
parsimonious sites, as described in Section
15
).

The claim can be checked by hand, but it’s more instructive to
employ the general theory of classifying toposes. In the situation

\textstyle{(\mathrm{Set}[T]\Rightarrow_{\mathrm{Set}[T_{0}]}\mathrm{Set}[T^{\prime}])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Set}[T^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Set}[T]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Set}[T_{0}],}

where
T_{0}
,
T
, and
T^{\prime}
are arbitrary geometric theories, the lax pullback
classifies the geometric theory whose models consist of a model
M
of
T
, a
model
N
of
T^{\prime}
, and a homomorphism
G(M)\to F(N)
of
T_{0}
-models. The
constructions
G
and
F
are given by the geometric morphisms
g
and
f
as
follows:

Any object of
\mathrm{Set}[T]
can be obtained by geometric constructions from
U_{T}
,
the universal model of
T
in
\mathrm{Set}[T]
. In particular, the pullback
g^{-1}U_{T_{0}}
, which is a model of
T_{0}
, can be obtained by geometric constructions
from
U_{T}
. Therefore the geometric morphism
g
displays a way to turn the
generic model of
T
into a model of
T_{0}
using only geometric constructions.
The same constructions can be applied to any model
M
of
T
, yielding a
model
G(M)
of
T_{0}
.

In the concrete situation at hand, the theory
T
is the empty theory
(admitting in any topos a unique model
M
), the theory
T^{\prime}
is the theory of local
rings, and
T_{0}
is the theory of rings. The
T_{0}
-model
G(M)
is the
ring
A
. The
T_{0}
-model
F(N)
of a local ring
N
is the underlying ring
of
N
.

Therefore the lax pullback
(\mathrm{Set}\Rightarrow_{\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]}\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{LocRing}])

classifies ring homomorphisms
A\to R
where
R
is a local ring, that is,
local
A
-algebras. It’s well-known that
\mathrm{Zar}(\Spec(A))
classifies these as
well.
∎

Remark 12.27
.

The pseudo pullback of the geometric morphism
\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{LocRing}]\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]
along
\mathrm{Set}\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]
is not very interesting: It is the
largest subtopos of
\mathrm{Set}
where the given ring
A
is local. Assuming
classical logic, this subtopos is either the trivial topos (if
A
is not
local) or
\mathrm{Set}
(if
A
is local).

Proposition 12.28
.

Let
A
be a ring. The pullback of the spectrum of the generic ring
along
\mathrm{Set}\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]
is the spectrum of
A
.

There are two related ways of making the statement precise.
Firstly, the spectrum of the generic ring
U
can be interpreted as a (locally
ringed) locale internal to
\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]
. Locales can be pulled back along
geometric morphisms (even though the pullback of a frame along a geometric
morphism typically fails to be a frame)
[
143
]
. In this
way
\Spec(U)
pulls back to a locale internal to
\mathrm{Set}
, that is an ordinary
external locale. The claim is that this locale is canonically isomorphic
to
\Spec(A)
.

A second way to interpret the statement of the proposition is to regard the
spectrum of the generic ring as a localic geometric morphism with
codomain
\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]
. The claim is then that the diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{Sh}(\Spec(A))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]}(\Spec(U))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Set}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Set}[\mathrm{Ring}]}

is a pullback diagram in the 2-category of toposes.

Using the language of classifying locales and classifying toposes, both claims
are easy to establish. The pulled-back locale (or topos) classifies the
pulled-back geometric theory
[
143
, Corollary 5.4]
. Since
the description of the theory which
\Spec(U)
classifies – the theory of
filters of
U
– is itself geometric, the pulled-back theory is the theory of
filters of
g^{-1}U\cong A
.
29
29

29

In the notation
of
[
143
, Section 5]
, the theory of filters of
U
is
represented by a GRD system with
G=U
and
R=1\amalg U^{2}\amalg U^{2}\amalg 1\amalg U^{2}
(one summand for each axiom scheme).

∎

Proposition 12.29
.

(1)

Let
A
be an
R
-algebra. The local spectrum
\Spec(A|R)
is the
pullback of
\Spec(U^{\prime\prime}|R)
along the geometric
morphism
\mathrm{Set}\to\mathcal{E}
given by
A
,
where
U^{\prime\prime}
is the
generic
R
-algebra

contained in the classifying topos
\mathcal{E}
of
R
-algebras.

(2)

Let
X
be a scheme (or a locally ringed locale). Let
\mathcal{A}
be
an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra. The relative spectrum
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{A})
is the pullback
of
\Spec(U^{\prime\prime}|\mathcal{O}_{X})
along the geometric
morphism
\mathrm{Sh}(X)\to\mathcal{E}
given by
\mathcal{A}
,
where
U^{\prime\prime}
is the generic
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra contained in
the classifying
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
-topos
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebras.

Straightforward modification of the proof of
Proposition
12.28
.
∎

Remark 12.30
.

The big Zariski topos
\mathrm{Zar}(\Spec(A))
can be obtained as the
pullback of the big Zariski topos of the generic ring
U
, if both toposes
are understood to be defined using the parsimonious sites as described in
Section
15
.

12.7.
Limits in the category of locally ringed locales

The category of ringed locales has small limits, by the naive construction. For
instance, the fiber product
X\times_{Z}Y
of ringed locales is given by the
fiber product of the underlying locales and the
structure sheaf
\pi_{X}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}\otimes_{\pi_{Z}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Z}}\pi_{Y}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}
. More
generally, the limit of a small diagram of ringed locales is given by the limit
L

of the underlying locales and the colimit of the pulled-back structure sheaves
(computed in the category of sheaves of rings on
L
).

However, when applied to a diagram of locally ringed locales, the ringed locale
which this simple construction yields is in general not locally ringed. This
can be nicely understood from the internal point of view: Let
R
be a local
ring. Let
R\to A
and
R\to B
be local
R
-algebras which are furthermore
local over
R
. Then the tensor product
A\otimes_{R}B
is in general not a
local ring. Indeed, this fails even in the easiest case, where all rings
involved are fields: The rings
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{C}
are local, and the
inclusion
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
is local, but
\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{R}[X]/(X^{2}+1)\cong\mathbb{C}[X]/(X^{2}+1)\cong\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
is not.

The following proposition explains that the true limit in the category of
locally ringed locales is obtained by
relocalizing
the limit in the
category of ringed locales.

Proposition 12.31
.

The category of locally ringed locales has all small limits.

For notational simplicity, we describe how products in the
category of locally ringed locales can be constructed. The general case is
entirely analogous.

Let
X
and
Y
be locally ringed locales. Their product
P
as ringed locales has
two defects: Firstly, it’s not locally ringed. Secondly, the ring-theoretic
parts of the projection morphisms
\pi_{X}:P\to X
and
\pi_{Y}:P\to Y

aren’t local, that is, don’t reflect invertibility.

The first issue could be solved by constructing, internally to
\mathrm{Sh}(P)
, the
ordinary spectrum of
\mathcal{O}_{P}
. From the external point of view, this would yield
a locally ringed locale equipped with morphisms of ringed, but not of locally
ringed, locales to
X
and
Y
.

To solve both issues, we need to employ a refined spectrum construction,
similar to the modification required by the internal account of the relative
spectrum: Internally to
\mathrm{Sh}(P)
, we construct the classifying locale of the
theory of those filters of
\mathcal{O}_{P}
which simultaneously lie over the filter of
units of
\pi_{X}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}
and which lie over the filter of units
of
\pi_{Y}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}
. This locale is a sublocale of
\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{P})
, the largest
such that the morphisms to
(\mathrm{pt},\pi_{X}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X})
and to
(\mathrm{pt},\pi_{Y}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y})
are
morphisms of locally ringed locales.

The externalization of the internal locally ringed locale obtained in this way
is the sought product of
X
and
Y
in the category of locally ringed locales.
∎

Remark 12.32
.

The category of locally ringed locales embeds as a (non-full)
coreflective subcategory into the category of ringed locales; the coreflector
maps a ringed locale
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
to the externalization of
\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{X})

(constructed internally to
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
). However, as is familiar in situations where the embedding
is not full
[
4
]
, it’s in general not the case that limits in
\mathrm{LRL}
are computed by
applying the coreflector to the limit computed in
\mathrm{RL}
. Employing the language
of the proof of Proposition
12.31
, applying the coreflector only
solves the first issue, but not the second.

It’s instructive to determine the points of limits in
\mathrm{LRL}
, even though a
locale is of course not determined by its points. For instance, the construction in
Proposition
12.31
shows that the points of the product
X\times Y
of locally ringed locales in
\mathrm{LRL}
are in canonical one-to-one
correspondence with tuples
(x,y,F)
, where
x
is a point of
X
,
y
is a
point of
y
, and
F
is a filter of
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}
which lies
over the filter of units of
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
and of
\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}
. In classical logic,
those tuples are in canonical one-to-one correspondence with
tuples
(x,y,\mathfrak{p})
, where
x
and
y
are as before and
\mathfrak{p}
is a prime
ideal of
k(x)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}k(y)
.

Similarly, points of the fiber product
X\times_{Z}Y
are in canonical
one-to-one correspondence with tuples
(x,y,F)
, where
x
is a point of
X

and
y
is a point of
y
such that both map to the same point
z
of
Z
,
and
F
is a filter of
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Z,z}}\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}
lying over the
filter of units of
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
and of
\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}
(and therefore automatically
of
\mathcal{O}_{Z,z}
). In classical logic, those tuples are in canonical one-to-one
correspondence with tuples
(x,y,\mathfrak{p})
, where
x
and
y
are as before
and
\mathfrak{p}
is a prime ideal of
k(x)\otimes_{k(z)}k(y)
.

Remark 12.33
.

By the adjunction
(\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt})_{L}\dashv(\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt})_{P}

relating locales and topological spaces, limits of locally ringed spaces which
happen to be sober can be computed by regarding them as locally ringed
locales by
(\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt})_{L}
, computing their limit in
\mathrm{LRL}
, and taking
the associated topological space of the limit by
(\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt})_{P}
.

Small diagrams of arbitrary locally ringed spaces admit limits as well.
Indeed, the proof of Proposition
12.31
was adapted from
Gillam’s proof of this fact
[
57
, Corollary 5]
.

12.8.
Relative Proj construction

Similar issues as with the relative
spectrum arise with the Proj construction: The standard definition of the Proj
construction as a topological space of homogeneous prime ideals gives rise to a
space which can’t intuitionistically be shown to satisfy the expected
universal property. The construction has to be reimagined as a locale
instead of a topological space. A certain sublocale of this locale then yields
the relative Proj construction when interpreted in the internal language of
the little Zariski topos of a base scheme (or a locally ringed locale).

Definition 12.34
.

The
Proj construction
of an
\mathbb{N}
-graded ring
S
is the
locale with frame of opens given by

\qquad\mathcal{T}(\Proj(S))\vcentcolon=\{\mathfrak{a}\subseteq S\,|\,\text{$\mathfrak{a}$ is a homogeneous radical ideal such that}\\
\forall x\,{:}\,S\mathpunct{.}\,xS_{+}\subseteq\mathfrak{a}\Rightarrow x\in\mathfrak{a}\},\qquad

where
S_{+}=\bigoplus_{i>0}S_{i}
is the irrelevant ideal.

A quick way to see that the partial order
\mathcal{T}(\Proj(S))
is a frame is to
recognize that it’s the frame of opens of a sublocale of
\Spec(S)
.
The associated nucleus
j:\mathcal{T}(\Spec(S))\to\mathcal{T}(\Spec(S))
is given by

j(\mathfrak{a})\vcentcolon=(\sqrt{\mathfrak{a}^{h}}:S_{+}),

where
\mathfrak{a}^{h}
is the homogenization of
\mathfrak{a}
, the ideal of
S
generated by
all homogeneous components of the elements of
\mathfrak{a}
. Since
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq\mathfrak{a}^{h}\subseteq\sqrt{\mathfrak{a}^{h}}\subseteq j(\mathfrak{a})
, a radical ideal
\mathfrak{a}

is an element of
\mathcal{T}(\Proj(S))
if and only if
\mathfrak{a}=j(\mathfrak{a})
.

One way to derive this definition is to start, within a classical context, with
the general expression for the nucleus associated to the subspace of
\Spec(S)

consisting of those prime ideals which are homogeneous and don’t
contain
S_{+}
, and then rewrite this expression to not refer to prime ideals.

Definition 12.35
.

A filter
F\subseteq S
in an
\mathbb{N}
-graded ring
S
is

homogeneous
if and only if, for any element
a\,{:}\,S
, the filter
F

contains
a
if it contains at least one of the homogeneous components of
a
.
It
meets the irrelevant ideal
if and only if
F\cap S_{+}
is
inhabited.

In classical logic, a subset is a homogeneous filter meeting the irrelevant
ideal if and only if its complement is a homogeneous prime ideal not containing
the irrelevant ideal. Intuitionistically, neither direction can be shown.

Proposition 12.36
.

Let
S
be an
\mathbb{N}
-graded ring. Then
\Proj(S)
is the classifying
locale of any of the following geometric theories.

(1)

The theory of homogeneous filters of
S
meeting the irrelevant ideal,
that is the theory of Remark
12.4
supplemented by the
following two axiom schemes:

•

\bigvee_{i}(a_{i}\in F)\vdash a\in F
(one axiom for each
decomposition
a=\sum_{i}a_{i}
of an element of
S
into homogeneous components)

•

\top\vdash\bigvee_{a\in S_{+}}(a\in F)
(one axiom)

(2)

The theory given by one atomic proposition “
a\in F_{i}
” for each
homogeneous element
a
of degree
i
in
S
and axioms given by the following
axiom schemes:

•

\top\vdash 1\in F_{0}
(one axiom)

•

st\in F_{i+j}\dashv\vdash s\in F_{i}\wedge t\in F_{j}
(two axioms for
each
i,j\geq 0
,
s\in S_{i}
,
t\in S_{j}
)

•

0\in F_{i}\vdash\bot
(one axiom for each
i\geq 0
)

•

s+t\in F_{i}\vdash s\in F_{i}\vee t\in F_{i}
(one axiom for each
i\geq 0
,
s,t\in A_{i}
)

•

\top\vdash\bigvee_{i\geq 1}\bigvee_{a\in S_{i}}(a\in F_{i})
(one
axiom)

(3)

The same theory as in (2), but with atomic propositions only for
homogeneous elements of degree
\geq 1
and without the first axiom “
\top\vdash 1\in F_{0}
”.

That
\Proj(S)
coincides with the classifying locale of the
theory given in (1), can be verified by a direct calculation. By the general
theory, the nucleus associated to the quotient theory given in (1) maps a
radical ideal
\mathfrak{a}\,{:}\,\mathcal{T}(\Spec(S))
to the least fixed point above
of
\mathfrak{a}
of the map

\mathfrak{b}\longmapsto\mathfrak{b}\vee\bigvee_{a\,{:}\,S}\Bigl(\sqrt{(a_{i})_{i}}\cap\bigl(\sqrt{(a)}\rightarrow\mathfrak{b}\bigr)\Bigr)\vee\Bigl(\sqrt{(a)_{a\in S_{+}}}\to\mathfrak{b}\Bigr),

where
(\mathfrak{c}\to\mathfrak{b})=(\mathfrak{b}:\mathfrak{c})
is the Heyting implication
and “
\vee
” is the join in
\mathcal{T}(\Spec(S))
. We omit the intermediate steps
of the calculation.

The theories given in (1) and in (2) are bi-interpretable. The interpretation
of the atomic propositions “
a\in F_{i}
” of theory (2) using the signature of
theory (1) is “
a\in F
”. Verifying the axioms is straightforward.
Conversely, the interpretation of “
a\in F
” in the signature of theory (2)
is “
\bigvee_{i}(a_{i}\in F_{i})
”, where
a=\sum_{i}a_{i}
is the decomposition
into homogeneous components. For verifying the axioms, one needs the lemma that

\bigvee_{i}(s_{i}\in F_{i})\wedge\bigvee_{j}(t_{j}\in F_{j})\ \dashv\vdash\ \bigvee_{n}\Bigl(\sum_{i+j=n}s_{i}t_{j}\in F_{n}\Bigr)

is derivable in theory (2), for any decompositions
s=\sum_{i}s_{i}
and
t=\sum_{j}t_{j}
of elements of
S
into homogeneous components. In the
guise “
\sqrt{(s_{i})_{i}}\cap\sqrt{(t_{j})_{j}}=\sqrt{(\sum_{i+j=n}s_{i}t_{j})_{n}}
”
this is a familiar fact on the content of
polynomials
[
13
, Proposition 1]
.

Also theories (2) and (3) are bi-interpretable. The interpretation of “
a\in F_{0}
” in the signature of theory (3) is “
\bigvee_{i\geq 1}\bigvee_{h\in S_{i}}(ha\in F_{i})
”.
∎

Corollary 12.37
.

Let
S
be an
\mathbb{N}
-graded ring. The points of
\Proj(S)
are in
canonical one-to-one correspondence with the homogeneous filters
of
S
meeting the irrelevant ideal.

Points of
\Proj(S)
are given by models of the theory of
homogeneous filters of
S
meeting the irrelevant ideal in
\mathrm{Set}
.
∎

Remark 12.38
.

The same presentation as in
Proposition
12.36
(3) has been used to
construct
\Proj(S)
not as a locale, but as a distributive
lattice
[
44
]
.

Definition 12.39
.

Let
S
be an
\mathbb{N}
-graded ring. The
generic homogeneous
filter meeting the irrelevant ideal
is the subsheaf
\mathcal{F}\hookrightarrow\text{\textul{$S$}}
over
\Proj(S)
generated by the sections
a
over
D_{+}(a)\vcentcolon=j(\sqrt{(a)})
.

Equivalently, the generic homogeneous filter meeting the irrelevant ideal is
the pullback of the generic filter in
\mathrm{Sh}(\Spec(S))
to
\mathrm{Sh}(\Proj(S))
.

Definition 12.40
.

Let
S
be an
\mathbb{N}
-graded ring. The structure sheaf of
\Proj(S)
is the homogeneous
localization
\text{\textul{$S$}}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]_{0}
of the ring
S
at the generic
homogeneous filter meeting the irrelevant ideal, that is the degree-zero part
of
\text{\textul{$S$}}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]
. The
tilde construction
of a graded
S
-module
M

is
M^{\sim}\vcentcolon=\text{\textul{$M$}}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]_{0}
.

The locally ringed locale
\Proj(S)
and the tilde construction defined in this
way enjoy their familiar properties. For instance, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 12.41
.

Let
S
be an
\mathbb{N}
-graded ring.

(1)

Let
f\,{:}\,S
be homogeneous of degree
d\geq 1
. Then
D_{+}(h)\cong\Spec(S[f^{-1}]_{0})
.

(2)

Assume that
S
is generated as an
S_{0}
-algebra by
S_{1}
. Let
M

and
N
be graded
S
-modules. Then
M^{\sim}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\Proj(S)}}N^{\sim}\cong(M\otimes_{S}N)^{\sim}
.

(3)

Under the same assumption as in (2), the twisting sheaves
\mathcal{O}(m)\vcentcolon=(S(m))^{\sim}
are finite locally free of rank
1
.

For the first statement, it suffices to verify that the theories
of homogeneous filters of
S
meeting the irrelevant ideal and containing
h

and of filters of
S[f^{-1}]_{0}
are bi-interpretable. It’s slightly more
convenient to use the presentation given by
Proposition
12.36
(2) for the former theory.

The interpretation of “
q\in F
” for
q\,{:}\,S[f^{-1}]_{0}
in the signature of
the theory given by Proposition
12.36
(2) is

\bigvee\{(x\in F_{di})\,|\,\text{$q=x/f^{i}$ for some $x\,{:}\,S$, $i\geq 0$}\}.

Conversely, the interpretation of “
a\in F_{i}
” in the signature of the
theory of filters of
S[f^{-1}]_{0}
is “
x^{d}/h^{i}\in F
”.

The second statement follows from the calculation

\displaystyle M^{\sim}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\Proj(S)}}N^{\sim}

\displaystyle=\text{\textul{$M$}}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]_{0}\otimes_{\text{\textul{$S$}}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]_{0}}\text{\textul{$N$}}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]_{0}

\displaystyle\cong(\text{\textul{$M$}}\otimes_{\text{\textul{$S$}}}\text{\textul{$N$}})[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]_{0}\cong(\text{\textul{$M\otimes_{S}N$}})[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]_{0}=(M\otimes_{S}N)^{\sim}.

The first isomorphism maps
x/s\otimes y/t
to
(x\otimes y)/(st)
. By the
assumption that
S
is generated as an
S_{0}
-algebra by
S_{1}
, the generic
filter contains a homogeneous element
h
of degree
1
from the internal point
of view of
\mathrm{Sh}(\Proj(S))
. Therefore the
map has an inverse sending
(a\otimes b)/u
, where
a
and
b
are
homogeneous of degrees
i
and
j
, to
(h^{j}a)/u\otimes b/h^{j}
. The second
isomorphism is because the tensor product is a geometric construction and
therefore commutes with constructing the constant sheaf.

For the proof of the third statement, we show that
(S(m))^{\sim}
is a finite
free module of rank
1
from the internal point of view. We again use that the
generic filter contains a homogeneous element
h\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$S$}}
of degree
1
from
the internal point of view. Such an element allows to define an
isomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{\Proj(S)}=\text{\textul{$S$}}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]_{0}\to\text{\textul{$S(m)$}}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]_{0}=\mathcal{O}(m)
by
mapping
x/s
to
(h^{m}x)/s
if
m\geq 0
and to
x/(h^{-m}s)
otherwise.
∎

Definition 12.42
.

Let
R
be a ring. Let
S
be an
\mathbb{N}
-graded
R
-algebra. The

local Proj construction
of
S
over
R
is the sublocale
\Proj(S|R)

of
\Proj(S)
with frame of opens given by

\mathcal{T}(\Proj(S|R))\vcentcolon=\{\mathfrak{a}\,{:}\,\mathcal{T}(\Proj(S))\,|\,\forall f\,{:}\,R\mathpunct{.}\,\forall s\,{:}\,S\mathpunct{.}\,(\ulcorner\text{{$f$ inv.{}}}\urcorner\Rightarrow s\in\mathfrak{a})\Rightarrow fs\in\mathfrak{a}\}

and with the pullback of
\mathcal{O}_{\Proj(S)}
as the structure sheaf.

Proposition 12.43
.

Let
R
be a ring. Let
S
be an
\mathbb{N}
-graded
R
-algebra.
Then the local Proj construction
\Proj(S|R)
is the classifying locale of the theory of homogeneous
filters of
S
meeting the irrelevant ideal and lying over the filter of
units.

Direct calculation similar to the proof of
Proposition
12.36
.∎

Since pullback and localization commute, the structure sheaf of
\Proj(S|R)

can also be described as
\text{\textul{$S$}}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]_{0}
, where by abuse of notation we
mean by “
\mathcal{F}
” the pullback of the generic filter on
\Proj(S)

to
\Proj(S|R)
. This filter has the special property

\mathrm{Sh}(\Proj(S|R))\models\forall r\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$R$}}\mathpunct{.}\,r\in\mathcal{F}\Rightarrow\ulcorner\text{{$r$ inv.{}{} in $\text{\textul{$R$}}$}}\urcorner.

Theorem 12.44
.

Let
X
be a scheme (or a locally ringed locale). Let
\mathcal{S}
be
an
\mathbb{N}
-graded
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra. Then the externalization
E(\Proj(\mathcal{S}|\mathcal{O}_{X}))
coincides
with the relative Proj construction
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Proj}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{S})
as locally ringed locales
over
X
.

For simplicity, we assume that
\mathcal{S}
is generated as
an
\mathcal{S}_{0}
-algebra by
\mathcal{S}_{1}
. In this case, the expected universal property
of the relative Proj construction is that it’s a locally ringed locale over
X
such
that, for all locally ringed locales
\mu:Y\to X
over
X
, the
set
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{LRL}/X}(Y,\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Proj}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{S}))
is canonically isomorphic (by pullback
of the standard such datum on
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Proj}$}}}_{X}(\mathcal{S})
) to the set of pairs
(\mathcal{L},\psi)
such that

•

\mathcal{L}
is a line bundle on
Y
and

•

\psi:\mu^{*}\mathcal{S}\to\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{L}^{\otimes n}
is a graded
morphism of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-algebras such that the degree-
1
part of
\psi
is a
surjective morphism
\mu^{*}\mathcal{S}_{1}\to\mathcal{L}

modulo equivalence. For instance, it is known that this property is satisfied
in the case that
X
is a scheme and
\mathcal{S}
is
quasicoherent
[
126
,
Tag 01O4
]
.

We verify that
E(\Proj(\mathcal{S}|\mathcal{O}_{X}))
enjoys the same property, even if
X
is not
a scheme or
\mathcal{S}
is not quasicoherent. For the rest of the proof, we switch to
the internal universe of
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
.

The local Proj construction is a locally ringed locale over
(\mathrm{pt},\mathcal{O}_{X})
by
the unique morphism
!:\Proj(\mathcal{S}|\mathcal{O}_{X})\to\mathrm{pt}
of locales and by the canonical
morphism
!^{\sharp}:\text{\textul{$\mathcal{O}_{X}$}}\to\text{\textul{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{0}\to\text{\textul{$S$}}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{\Proj(\mathcal{S}|\mathcal{O}_{X})}

of local rings.

As the standard datum on
\Proj(\mathcal{S}|\mathcal{O}_{X})
, we choose the line bundle
\mathcal{O}(1)

(pulled back to
\Proj(\mathcal{S}|\mathcal{O}_{X})
) together with the canonical morphism
!^{*}\mathcal{S}\to\oplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{O}(1)^{\otimes n}
.

Let
Y
be a locally ringed locale over
(\mathrm{pt},\mathcal{O}_{X})
. Let a pair
(\mathcal{L},\psi)

be given. In the internal language of
\mathrm{Sh}(Y)
, we define a filter by the formula

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\vcentcolon=\{s\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$S$}}\,|\,\ulcorner\text{{there exists\penalty\ $i$ such that\penalty\ $(\psi(s_{i}\otimes 1))$ is a basis
of\penalty\ $\mathcal{L}^{\otimes i}$}}\urcorner\}\subseteq\text{\textul{$S$}},

where
s_{i}
refers to the homogeneous component of
s
of degree
i
.
Since
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes i}
is finite free of rank
1
, a one-element family
in
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes i}
is a basis if and only if it’s a generating family. This
observation can be repeatedly used to verify that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is
homogeneous, meets the irrelevant ideal, and lies over the filter of
units. Since
\Proj(\mathcal{S}|\mathcal{O}_{X})
is the classifying locale of such filters
(Proposition
12.43
), we obtain a
morphism
f:Y\to\Proj(\mathcal{S}|\mathcal{O}_{X})
of locales which is unique with the property
that
f^{-1}\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.

To obtain a morphism
Y\to\Proj(\mathcal{S}|\mathcal{O}_{X})
of locally ringed locales, it
remains to define a morphism
f^{\sharp}:f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\Proj(\mathcal{S}|\mathcal{O}_{X})}=\text{\textul{$S$}}[\mathcal{F}^{\prime-1}]_{0}\to\mathcal{O}_{Y}
. A canonical choice is

x/s\mapsto\ulcorner\text{{
the coefficient of\penalty\ $\psi(x\otimes 1)$
with respect to the basis\penalty\ $(\psi(s\otimes 1))$}}\urcorner.

We omit further verifications.
∎

12.9.
A constructive account of scheme theory

Scheme theory as classically set up heavily relies on prime ideals and
therefore only works well in a classical context, where the law of excluded
middle and (at least some forms of) the axiom of choice are available. However,
the actual mathematical ideas often do not fundamentally require classical logic; we
don’t begin the proof that the kernel of a morphism between quasicoherent
sheaves of modules is quasicoherent by supposing that it’s not. Instead,
classical logic is only needed because the usual foundations of scheme theory
involving locally ringed spaces require it.

In this section, we sketch how scheme theory can be developed in an
intuitionistic metatheory; there are several reasons why it’s desirable to have
such an account. Firstly, as is familiar from constructive treatments of other
subjects, the constraint to set up all definitions in an intuitionistically
sensible way is a useful guiding principle which can increase the perceived
elegance of the theory and result in more direct proofs.

It would be interesting to know which advanced results in algebraic geometry

actually
require classical logic (or at least classicality hypotheses on
the ground ring); to study this question, one has to use a foundation
which doesn’t itself require classical logic just for organizational
purposes.
30
30

30

For instance, some results in linear algebra can
intuitionistically only be shown for
discrete
fields – fields such that
any element is zero or not zero. Such hypotheses are computationally meaningful
and will entail similar hypotheses for some results in algebraic geometry.

McLarty and other researchers study a similar question: Which axioms of set
theory are actually needed for algebraic geometry, in particular for proving
Fermat’s Last Theorem?
[
96
]

Secondly, one might be interested in concrete computations and might therefore
leverage the fact that one can mechanically extract algorithms from
constructive proofs. For instance, an intuitionistic proof that some cohomology
is finite dimensional yields an algorithm for computing the dimension and even
a basis.

Finally, one might want to apply scheme theory in the intuitionistic internal
universe of the little Zariski topos of a base scheme, in order to generalize
results of absolute scheme theory to relative scheme theory with little effort
and no duplication of proofs. The starting point for such a transfer is that
locally ringed locales over a locally ringed locale
X
look like locally
ringed locales over the point from the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
, as
discussed in Section
12.1
.
