\mathrm{Zar}(X)\to\mathrm{Zar}(S)

which we again denote by “
f
”. Explicitly, the big Zariski toposes are
related by the adjoint triple
f_{!}\dashv f^{-1}\dashv f_{*}
with

\displaystyle f_{*}:\mathrm{Zar}(X)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Zar}(S),\ F

\displaystyle\longmapsto((T\xrightarrow{g}S)\mapsto F(T\times_{S}X)),

\displaystyle f^{-1}:\mathrm{Zar}(S)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Zar}(X),\ E

\displaystyle\longmapsto((T\xrightarrow{g}X)\mapsto E(T\xrightarrow{g}X\xrightarrow{f}S)),

\displaystyle f_{!}:\mathrm{Zar}(X)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Zar}(S),\ F

\displaystyle\longmapsto((T\xrightarrow{g}S)\mapsto\coprod_{h:T\to X}F(T\xrightarrow{h}X)).

In situation (2), the well-definedness of these functors is trivial. In
situation (1), the well-definedness rests on the lemma that an
S
-morphism
h:T\to X
is locally of
finite presentation if
T
and
X
are locally of finite presentation over
S

[
126
,
Tag 02FV
]
.

The objects of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
listed on page
14
pull back
as expected:

•

Let
Y
be an
S
-scheme. Then
f^{-1}\text{\textul{$Y$}}=\text{\textul{$Y\times_{S}X$}}
, by
the universal property of the fiber product.

•

In particular,
f^{-1}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}={\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}}
, since
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\times_{S}X=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{X}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a sheaf of sets on
S
. Then
f^{-1}\pi_{S}^{-1}\mathcal{F}=\pi_{X}^{-1}f^{-1}\mathcal{F}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a sheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules. Then
f^{-1}\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Zar}}=(f^{*}\mathcal{F})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
.

The functors
f_{!}\dashv f^{-1}
induce an equivalence

\mathrm{Zar}(X)\simeq\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\text{\textul{$X$}},

explicitly described by

\begin{array}[]{r@{}c@{}l}F&{}\longmapsto&(f_{!}F\to f_{!}1),\\
((T\xrightarrow{g}X)\mapsto\{s\in(f^{-1}E)(T)\,|\,\alpha(s)=g\})&{}\longmapsfrom&(E\xrightarrow{\alpha}\text{\textul{$X$}}).\end{array}

From the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, the big Zariski topos of
X
is
therefore simply
\mathrm{Set}/\text{\textul{$X$}}
, the category of
X
-indexed families of
sets or equivalently the category of sheaves on
X
considered as a

discrete
locale. This fits nicely with the philosophy that “
S
-schemes are
plain unstructured sets from the internal point of view
of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
’’.
40
40

40

The requirement on
f
mentioned at the beginning of
this subsection is really necessary. For instance, let
f
be the unique
morphism
\Spec(\mathbb{Q})\to\Spec(\mathbb{Z})
. This morphism is not locally of finite
presentation. By Proposition
11.19
, if the parsimonious
sites are used, the functor of
points
\text{\textul{$\mathbb{Q}$}}\in\mathrm{Zar}(\Spec(\mathbb{Z}))
coincides with the functor of points of
the empty scheme and is thus the initial
object in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. Therefore
\mathrm{Zar}(\Spec(\mathbb{Z}))/\text{\textul{$\mathbb{Q}$}}
is the trivial
topos. In contrast,
\mathrm{Zar}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))
is not.

In contrast, for the little Zariski toposes, there is no similarly simple
description of the little Zariski topos of
X
as a slice of the little Zariski
topos of
S
. From the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Sh}(S)
, the topos
\mathrm{Sh}(X)

looks like the topos of sheaves over a locale which is not discrete, and the
topos
\mathrm{Zar}(X)
doesn’t even look like a topos of sheaves over an arbitrary
locale (discrete or not).

The internal language of a slice topos
\mathcal{E}/I
admits a
simple description from the point of view of
\mathcal{E}
. Namely, for
any formula
\varphi
over
\mathcal{E}/I
,

\mathcal{E}/I\models\varphi\hskip 20.00003pt\text{iff}\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{E}\models\forall i\,{:}\,I\mathpunct{.}\,\varphi(i).

For the right hand side to make sense, it has to be interpreted in the
following way. Any object
(p:M\to I)
of
\mathcal{E}/I
which appears in
\varphi
,
for instance as a domain of quantification, has to be substituted by the
internal expression “
p^{-1}[\{i\}]
” denoting the fiber of
p

over
i\,{:}\,I
.
41
41

41

This substitution is less ad hoc as it might at first
appear. The internal language of a topos
\mathcal{E}
is
dependently typed
, meaning
that the types one can quantify over may depend on previously introduced
values. Types in the empty context, depending on no values, correspond to
objects of
\mathcal{E}
. Types in the context of a variable
i\,{:}\,I
correspond to
objects
(p:M\to I)
of
\mathcal{E}/I
. For instance, in this case one can form
formulas of the form “
\forall i\,{:}\,I\mathpunct{.}\,\forall m\,{:}\,M(i)\mathpunct{.}\,\psi(i,m)
”. If in
the translation process using the Kripke–Joyal semantics a formal variable
i

was substituted by a generalized element
i_{0}:A\to I
, the
expression “
M(i_{0})
” has to be interpreted as the pullback
i_{0}^{*}M
.

For example, if
(p:M\to I)
is such an object of
\mathcal{E}/I
,

\displaystyle\phantom{\text{iff }}\mathcal{E}/I\models\ulcorner\text{{$M$ is inhabited}}\urcorner

\displaystyle\text{iff }\phantom{/I}\mathcal{E}\models\forall i\,{:}\,I\mathpunct{.}\,\ulcorner\text{{the fiber of $p$ over $i$ is inhabited}}\urcorner

\displaystyle\text{iff }\phantom{/I}\mathcal{E}\models\forall i\,{:}\,I\mathpunct{.}\,\exists m\,{:}\,M\mathpunct{.}\,p(m)=i.

Thanks to this description of the internal language of a slice topos, the
equivalence
\mathrm{Zar}(X)\simeq\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\text{\textul{$X$}}
is useful for lifting internal
characterizations concerning properties of
S
-schemes to properties of
morphisms of
S
-schemes. For instance, we will see in
Proposition
19.37
that the structure morphism of
an
S
-scheme
f:Y\to S
is surjective if and only if

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\neg\neg(\ulcorner\text{{$\text{\textul{$Y$}}$ is inhabited}}\urcorner)
.
This automatically implies (Corollary
19.38
)
that a morphism
p:Y\to X
of
S
-schemes is surjective if and only if

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\forall x\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$X$}}\mathpunct{.}\,\neg\neg(\ulcorner\text{{the fiber of\penalty\ $\text{\textul{$p$}}$
over\penalty\ $x$ is inhabited}}\urcorner).

Many properties of morphisms in algebraic geometry, and all properties which
can be characterized using the internal language of the big Zariski topos, are
stable under base change. For those kinds of properties
P
, if a morphism
Y\to X
is
P
, then for any point
x\in X
the base change
Y_{x}\to\Spec(k(x))
along
\Spec(k(x))\to X
is
P
as well. The converse is usually
false, but the motto “a morphism is
P
if all its fibers are
P
in a
continuous fashion” is still useful for intuition. The equivalence
\mathrm{Zar}(X)\simeq\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\text{\textul{$X$}}
makes this motto precise: For any morphism
p:Y\to X

of
S
-schemes and any formula
\varphi(M)
of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
containing a free
variable
M
,

\mathrm{Zar}(X)\models\varphi(\text{\textul{$Y$}})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{iff}\hskip 20.00003pt\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\forall x\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$X$}}\mathpunct{.}\,\varphi(\text{\textul{$p$}}^{-1}[\{x\}]),

that is
Y
has property
\varphi
when regarded as an
X
-scheme if and only
if all the fibers of
Y\to X
have property
\varphi
when regarded
as
S
-schemes.

The family of geometric morphisms

\mathrm{Sh}(X)\to\mathrm{Zar}(X)\to\mathrm{Zar}(S)

with inverse image
E\mapsto E|_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}
, where
X
ranges over
all
S
-schemes contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, is jointly
surjective. That is, the restriction functors
\mathrm{Zar}(S)\to\mathrm{Sh}(X)
are jointly
conservative. This fact, together with the fact that these functors commute
with geometric constructions, is frequently useful to relate truth in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)

with truth in all of the
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
.

Lemma 16.6
.

(1)

The functor
\pi^{-1}
from
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules to
\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules commutes
with tensor product.

(2)

The functor
\pi_{*}
from
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules to
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules commutes with
tensor product.

(3)

The
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
associated to an
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{E}
(defined
on page
14
) is canonically isomorphic
to
\pi^{-1}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
.

(4)

The functor
\mathcal{E}\mapsto\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
from
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules to
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules
commutes with tensor product.

Forming the tensor product is a geometric construction. It is
therefore preserved by
\pi^{-1}
and by
\pi_{*}
, since both functors are
inverse-image-parts of geometric morphisms. Claim (3) follows from the
calculation

\displaystyle(\pi^{-1}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})|_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}

\displaystyle\cong(\pi^{-1}\mathcal{E})|_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}\otimes_{(\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S})|_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}|_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}

\displaystyle\cong(f^{-1}\mathcal{E})\otimes_{f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathcal{O}_{X}

\displaystyle\cong f^{*}\mathcal{E}

for
S
-schemes
(f:X\to S)
contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.
Finally, claim (4) follows by using a standard property for tensor products
(whose proof is intuitionistic and therefore valid in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
):

\displaystyle(\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathcal{F})^{\mathrm{Zar}}

\displaystyle\cong\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{F})\otimes_{\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}

\displaystyle\cong(\pi^{-1}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}}\pi^{-1}\mathcal{F})\otimes_{\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}

\displaystyle\cong(\pi^{-1}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})\otimes_{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}(\pi^{-1}\mathcal{F}\otimes_{\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Zar}}\otimes_{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Zar}}.\qed

Example 16.7
.

Constructing, internally in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, the
module
\Omega^{1}_{{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}|\flat{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}}
of Kähler differentials of the ring
morphism
\flat{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\to{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
yields the “universal cotangent sheaf”

X\longmapsto\Gamma(X,\Omega^{1}_{X|S}).

This is because constructing the module of Kähler differentials is a geometric
construction and the restriction functors
\mathrm{Zar}(S)\to\mathrm{Zar}(X)
commute with
geometric constructions, so

{\llbracket{\Omega^{1}_{{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}|\flat{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}}}\rrbracket}|_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}\cong\Omega^{1}_{({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}|_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)})|((\flat{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})|_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)})}\cong\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}|f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}}\cong\Omega^{1}_{X|S}

for any
S
-scheme
(f:X\to S)
contained in the site used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

This universal cotangent sheaf doesn’t enjoy any finiteness properties from the
internal point of view. For instance, it isn’t finitely generated, because else
all the individual cotangent sheaves
\Omega^{1}_{X|S}
would be of finite type
(even with a uniform bound on the number of generators, if
S
is quasicompact) by
Proposition
18.1
.

We feel that the significance of the canonical morphism
\flat{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\to{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
hasn’t been adequately explored yet.

Remark 16.8
.

Some care is needed when dealing with the modalities
\flat

and
\sharp
, since they are not compatible with change of base.
If
f:X\to S
is a morphism of schemes, then in general
f^{-1}(\flat E)\not\cong\flat(f^{-1}E)
, since

\displaystyle f^{-1}(\flat E)

\displaystyle=((T\xrightarrow{g}X)\mapsto\Gamma(T,g^{-1}f^{-1}(E|_{\mathrm{Sh}(S)}))),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{but}

\displaystyle\flat(f^{-1}E)

\displaystyle=((T\xrightarrow{g}X)\mapsto\Gamma(T,g^{-1}(E|_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}))).

A special case in which the canonical morphism
f^{-1}(\flat E)\to\flat(f^{-1}E)
is an isomorphism is when
f
is an open immersion.

A consequence of the fact that
\flat
and
\sharp
aren’t compatible with
change of base is that these modalities can’t be defined in the internal
language of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, since any construction which can be described in the
internal language is automatically compatible with change of base. However, the
modalities can still be used and their general properties can even be
elementarily axiomatized
[
9
]
.

16.4.
The big Zariski topos of a relative spectrum

Theorem 16.9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra. In the situation that the
parsimonious sites are used for constructing big Zariski toposes, the big
Zariski topos of
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}(\mathcal{A})
is, from the internal point of view
of
\mathrm{Sh}(S)
, the classifying topos of the theory of
local
\mathcal{A}
-algebras which are local over
\mathcal{O}_{S}
.

The proof is similar to the proof of
Theorem
16.1
. Let
X=\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}(\mathcal{A})
and
f:X\to S
be the
canonical morphism. The big Zariski topos of
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}(\mathcal{A})
is
a
\mathrm{Sh}(S)
-topos by the composition
\mathrm{Zar}(\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}(\mathcal{A}))\to\mathrm{Zar}(S)\to\mathrm{Sh}(S)
. The pullback of
\mathcal{O}_{S}
along this geometric morphism
is
f^{-1}(\flat{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})
. A canonical
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra in
\mathrm{Zar}(\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}(\mathcal{A}))

is therefore

f^{-1}(\flat{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})\longrightarrow\flat{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}}\longrightarrow{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}}.

This algebra is indeed local and local over
f^{-1}(\flat{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})
.

For verifying the universal property, it suffices to restrict to the case
that
S=\Spec(R)
is affine, as in the proof of
Theorem
16.1
, and consider a geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{E}\to\mathrm{Sh}(S)
. In this case
\mathcal{A}=A^{\sim}

and
X=\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}(\mathcal{A})=\Spec(A)
. Let
\alpha:R\to A
be the structure
morphism of
A
. We then have the chain of equivalences

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{\simeq}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Sh}(S)}(\mathcal{E},\mathrm{Zar}(X))

\displaystyle\simeq\text{cat.{} of local algebras\penalty\ $\varphi:\text{\textul{$A$}}\to\mathcal{B}$
in\penalty\ $\mathrm{Zar}(X)$ such that\penalty\ $\text{\textul{$\alpha$}}^{-1}\varphi^{-1}\mathcal{B}^{\times}=f^{-1}\mathcal{F}$}

\displaystyle\simeq\text{cat.{} of local algebras\penalty\ $\psi:f^{-1}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}$ such that
$f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}\to f^{-1}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}$ is local}.

The first equivalence maps a geometric morphism
g:\mathcal{E}\to\mathrm{Zar}(X)
to
\text{\textul{$A$}}\to g^{-1}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}}
. The second equivalence acts as follows. Given an
algebra
\varphi:\text{\textul{$A$}}\to\mathcal{B}
such that
\text{\textul{$\alpha$}}^{-1}\varphi^{-1}\mathcal{B}^{\times}=f^{-1}\mathcal{F}
, we can factor
\text{\textul{$R$}}\to\text{\textul{$A$}}\to\mathcal{B}
uniquely as a
localization
\text{\textul{$R$}}\to C
followed by a local homomorphism
C\to\mathcal{B}
. By the
condition on filters, the localization
C
is isomorphic to
f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}
. From
the description
\mathcal{A}=\text{\textul{$A$}}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}]
it is apparent that
\text{\textul{$A$}}\to\mathcal{B}

factors over
\text{\textul{$A$}}\to f^{-1}\mathcal{A}
. In this way, we obtain
morphisms
f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}\to f^{-1}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
.
∎

The only reason why we have supposed that
\mathcal{A}
is quasicoherent in the
statement of Theorem
16.9
is
because else
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}(\mathcal{A})
might fail to be a scheme, whereby the notion
“big Zariski topos of
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}(\mathcal{A})
” is not defined.

In fact, we propose the following definition: If
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
is an arbitrary
locally ringed locale (or even a locally ringed topos), then the big Zariski
topos of
X
should be the classifying
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
-topos of the theory (internal
to
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
) of local
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebras which are local over
\mathcal{O}_{X}
.
The following proposition shows that this definition is consistent with
Theorem
16.1
and
with Theorem
16.9
.

Proposition 16.10
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra. The following constructions,
performed internally to
\mathrm{Sh}(S)
, yield canonically equivalent toposes:

(1)

Constructing first the local spectrum
X\vcentcolon=\Spec(\mathcal{A}|\mathcal{O}_{S})
and then,
internally to
\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Sh}(S)}(X)
, the classifying topos of the theory
of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebras which are local over
\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

(2)

Constructing the classifying topos of the theory of
\mathcal{A}
-algebras which
are local over
\mathcal{O}_{S}
.

If furthermore
\mathcal{A}
is finitely presented as an
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra from the
internal point of view of
\mathrm{Sh}(S)
, then the following construction yields the
same result as well:

(3)

Constructing first the big Zariski topos of
S
as the classifying topos
of local
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebras which are local over
\mathcal{O}_{S}
and then constructing,
internally to that topos, the slice topos over
[\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{Zar}},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]_{\mathrm{Alg}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}
.

If
S
is indeed a scheme, as is supposed throughout Part
III
, and
\mathcal{A}
is
quasicoherent, then all three constructions yield the big
Zariski topos of
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}(\mathcal{A})
(defined using one of the parsimonious
sites). For the first construction, this is by
Theorem
12.10
and
Theorem
16.1
; for the second construction, this is by
Theorem
16.9
; and for the third
construction, this is by Theorem
16.1
,
Proposition
18.8
, and the description of
the slice topos in Section
16.3
. However, the claim also
holds if
\mathcal{A}
is not quasicoherent or if
S
is an arbitrary locally ringed
locale, and it’s instructive to see the proof in this more general situation.

We work in the internal universe of
\mathrm{Sh}(S)
. Let
\mathcal{E}
be an arbitrary
(Grothendieck) topos. Then
\mathcal{E}
-valued points of the three toposes are given
by:

(1)

a filter
F\subseteq\mathcal{A}
lying over the filter of units of
\mathcal{O}_{S}

together with a local
\mathcal{A}_{F}
-algebra
R
which is local over
\mathcal{A}_{F}

(2)

a local
\mathcal{A}
-algebra which is local over
\mathcal{O}_{S}

(3)

a local
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra
R
which is local over
\mathcal{O}_{S}
together with an
element of the stalk of
[\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{Zar}},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]_{\mathrm{Alg}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}
at the point
corresponding to
R

In the case that
\mathcal{A}
is finitely presented, the stalk appearing in
description (3) is canonically isomorphic to the set of
R
-algebra
homomorphisms
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}R\to R
, as discussed in
Lemma
6.45
.

With these descriptions, the equivalence is immediate. For instance, a
datum
(F\subseteq\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}_{F}\to R)
as in description (1) gives rise to the
datum
(\mathcal{O}_{S}\to\mathcal{A}_{F}\to R)
as in description (2). Conversely, the structure
morphism of a datum as in description (2) can be factored as a localization
followed by a local homomorphism to yield a datum as in (1).
∎

16.5.
Constructing the big Zariski topos without recourse to classical
scheme theory

Given a scheme
S
, is it possible to construct the big Zariski topos of
S

without recourse to classical scheme theory? Without employing a site which
refers to schemes as classically conceived?

Taken literally, this question is ill-posed, since the datum
S
is given as a
classical scheme. A better question is: Is it possible to setup the basics of
the theory of schemes using only big Zariski toposes, preferably even in an
intuitionistic fashion?

This is indeed possible, and we wish to sketch how this can be done. Given a
base ring
A
, the big Zariski topos of
\Spec(A)
can be defined as the topos
of sheaves over the parsimonious site
\mathrm{Alg}(A)_{\mathrm{fp}}^{\mathrm{op}}
consisting of (formal
duals) of finitely presented
A
-algebras. We can then declare an
A
-scheme to
be an object of
\mathrm{Zar}(\Spec(A))
having certain properties, for instance being
a
finitely presented synthetic scheme

(Definition
19.49
).

The big Zariski topos of such an
A
-scheme
X
can then simply be defined as
the slice topos
\mathrm{Zar}(\Spec(A))/X
, in accordance with the equivalence noted in
Section
16.3
. This slice topos can serve as the base over
which further schemes and their big Zariski toposes can be constructed.

Inaccessible to this approach to scheme theory are schemes which are not
locally of finite presentation over the base ring. If one wants to account for
such schemes, one has to substitute the parsimonious site for a larger one;
however, some problems remain, as indicated in
Section
19.10
.

17.
The double negation modality

Proposition 17.1
.

Let
\varphi
be a formula over
S
. Consider the following
statements:

(1)

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\neg\neg\varphi
.

(2)

For all points
s\in S
, there is a field extension
K\mathrel{|}k(s)
such
that
\Spec(K)\to\Spec(k(s))\to S
is contained in the site used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
and such that
\Spec(K)\models\varphi
.

(3)

For all closed points
s\in S
, there is a finite field extension
K\mathrel{|}k(s)
such that
\Spec(K)\models\varphi
.

Then:

•

Condition (2) implies condition (1). The converse holds if the site used
to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is closed under taking spectra of residue fields (this
is satisfied for all sites listed in Section
15

except for the parsimonious sites).

•

If one of the parsimonious sites is used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
and
S
is
locally Noetherian, condition (1) implies condition (3). The converse holds if
additionally
S
is locally of finite type over a field.

We begin with showing that condition (2) implies condition (1). By
the Kripke–Joyal translation, we need to verify that

\forall(X\to S)\mathpunct{.}\,\Bigl(\forall(T\to X)\mathpunct{.}\,(T\models\varphi)\Rightarrow T=\emptyset\Bigr)\Longrightarrow X=\emptyset,

where the universal quantifiers range over all schemes contained in the site
used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. So let such an
S
-scheme
f:X\to S
be given. We
show that the fiber over any point
s\in S
is empty. By assumption, there is
a field extension
K\mathrel{|}k(s)
such that
\Spec(K)\to\Spec(k(s))\to S

is contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
and such that
\Spec(K)\models\varphi
. The base change
T
of the fiber
X_{s}
to
\Spec(K)
as
indicated in the diagram

\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spec(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spec(k(s))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S}

is contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
as well, therefore
saying “
T\models\varphi
” is meaningful. And indeed
T\models\varphi
,
since
\Spec(K)\models\varphi
. Therefore
T=\emptyset
. Thus
X_{s}=\emptyset
as claimed.

For the direction “(1)
\Rightarrow
(2)”, let a point
s\in S
be given.
Since we assume that the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
contains
the
S
-scheme
X\vcentcolon=\Spec(k(s))
and since
X\neq\emptyset
, the
assumption implies that there exists a nonempty
X
-scheme
T
such that
T\models\varphi
. Since
T
is nonempty, there exists a point
t\in T
. By
the morphism
\Spec(k(t))\to T\to X
, the field
K\vcentcolon=k(t)
is an
extension of
k(s)
, and since
\Spec(K)\to T\to X\to S
is contained in the
site, we have
\Spec(K)\models\varphi
.

The proof that condition (1) implies condition (3) in the case that one of the
parsimonious sites is used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
and that
S
is locally
Noetherian is similar. For a closed point
s\in S
, the residue field
k(s)

can be computed as
A/\mathfrak{m}
, where
A
is the ring of functions of an open
affine neighborhood of
s
and
\mathfrak{m}
is a maximal ideal in
A
. Since
A
is
Noetherian, the ideal
\mathfrak{m}
is finitely generated and therefore
A/\mathfrak{m}
is
finitely presented as an
A
-algebra. Thus the canonical morphism
\Spec(k(s))\to\Spec(A)\to S
is locally of finite presentation and thereby contained in
the parsimonious site. The hypothesis is therefore applicable to
X\vcentcolon=\Spec(k(s))
and yields a nonempty
X
-scheme
T
which is locally of finite
presentation over
X
such that
T\models\varphi
.

Since the structure morphism
T\to X
is locally of finite presentation, the
scheme
T
inherits the property to be locally Noetherian from
X
. Let
U\subseteq T
be a nonempty open affine subset and let
t\in U
be a point
which is closed in
U
. With the same reasoning as above, the canonical
morphism
\Spec(k(t))\to U\to T
is therefore contained in the parsimonious
site. Thus
\Spec(k(t))\models\varphi
. The field
K\vcentcolon=k(t)
is finitely
presented as a
k(s)
-algebra. By Noether normalization, it is also of finite
dimension as a
k(s)
-vector space.

Finally, we verify that condition (3) implies condition (1) if one of the
parsimonious sites is used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
and if
S
is locally
of finite type over a field (and therefore in particular Noetherian). We adopt
the notation of the proof of “(2)
\Rightarrow
(1)”. The argument there
shows that all fibers of
f
over closed points are empty. If
X
is not empty,
it contains a closed point
x
(since
X
is locally of finite type over a field,
any point which is closed in an open affine neighborhood will do). Since
X

is locally of finite type over a field, the point
f(x)
is closed in
S
.
Therefore
x
is contained in the fiber over a closed point; a contradiction.
∎

Remark 17.2
.

The proof of Proposition
17.1
uses
classical logic in a substantial way, since repeatedly the lemma that a scheme
is trivial if it doesn’t have any points was used. Even if scheme theory is
set up in an intuitionistically sensible way (for instance by defining a scheme to be
a locally ringed locale which is locally isomorphic to the locale-theoretic
spectra of rings as discussed in Section
12.2
), one
should therefore not expect the proposition to admit an intuitionistic proof
without additional hypotheses.

Lemma 17.3
.

Let
f:X\to S
and
g:Y\to S
be
S
-schemes which are locally contained
in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. In the case that the site is one of the
parsimonious sites, further assume that
f
and
g
are quasicompact and quasiseparated.
The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The image of
f
coincides with the image of
g
topologically.

(2)

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\neg\neg(\ulcorner\text{{$\text{\textul{$X$}}$ inhabited}}\urcorner)\Leftrightarrow\neg\neg(\ulcorner\text{{$\text{\textul{$Y$}}$ inhabited}}\urcorner)
.

By Proposition
19.37
, which we’ll
prove below, statement (2) is equivalent to:

For any
S
-scheme
h:T\to S
contained in the site used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, the morphism
X\times_{S}T\to T
is surjective if and only
if
Y\times_{S}T\to T
is.

We verify that this statement implies statement (1). Let
s\in\im(f)
. Then the canonical
morphism
X_{s}\to\Spec(k(s))
is surjective. Therefore there exists
an
S
-scheme
h:T\to S
which is contained in the site used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
such that
X\times_{S}T\to T
is surjective and such that
s\in\im(h)
: If
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is defined using a Grothendieck or partial universe,
this claim is trivial, since we can take
T\vcentcolon=\Spec(k(s))
. If
\mathrm{Zar}(S)

is defined using one of the parsimonious sites, we employ the technique of
relative approximation.
42
42

42

More specifically, we may assume that
S
is
affine. Then the lemma on relative approximation
[
126
,
Tag 09MV
]
can be
applied to write
\Spec(k(s))
as a directed limit of an inverse system of
finitely presented
S
-schemes
T_{i}
with affine transition maps. Let
U\subseteq X
be an open affine subset containing a preimage of
s
. The
property that
U\hookrightarrow X\to\Spec(k(s))
is surjective descends to
one of the morphisms
U\times_{S}T_{i}\to T_{i}

[
126
,
Tag 07RR
]
. In
particular, the morphism
X\times_{S}T_{i}\to T_{i}
is surjective. We can
therefore take
T\vcentcolon=T_{i}
. The image of
T_{i}\to S
contains
s

since
\Spec(k(s))\to S
factors over
T_{i}\to S
.

The assumption yields that the induced morphism
Y\times_{S}T\to T
is
surjective. Since
s\in\im(h)
, also
s\in\im(g)
.

The proof of the converse containment relation is analogous.

The direction “(1)
\Rightarrow
(2)” is immediate, since

\im(X\times_{S}T\to T)=h^{-1}\im(f)=h^{-1}\im(g)=\im(Y\times_{S}T\to T).\qed

Remark 17.4
.

Let
f:X\to S
be contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.
In the case that the site is one of the parsimonious sites, further assume
that
f
is quasicompact and quasiseparated.
The expression “
\neg\neg(\ulcorner\text{{$\text{\textul{$X$}}$ is inhabited}}\urcorner)
” of the internal
language of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
denotes the subfunctor of the terminal
functor
\text{\textul{$S$}}=1\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
given by

(h:T\to S)\longmapsto\{\star\,|\,\im(h)\subseteq\im(f)\}.

If
f
is an open immersion, then this functor coincides with the functor of
points of
X
, since the set-theoretic image of a morphism of schemes is
contained in an open subset
U\subseteq S
if and only if it factors over the
open immersion
U\hookrightarrow S
.

If
f
is a closed immersion, this functor is the functor of points of the
formal completion of
S
along
X
. More generally, for an
arbitrary
S
-scheme
X
and a closed subscheme
Z\hookrightarrow X
(such
that both
X
and
S
are locally contained in the site used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
), the internal expression “
\{x\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$X$}}\,|\,\neg\neg(x\in\text{\textul{$Z$}})\}
” denotes the functor of points of the formal completion of
X

along
Z
. For instance, the expression

\{f\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\,|\,\neg\neg(f=0)\}=\{f\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\,|\,\ulcorner\text{{$f$ is nilpotent}}\urcorner\}

denotes the formal neighborhood of the origin in the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
.
(The equivalence
\neg\neg(f=0)\Leftrightarrow\ulcorner\text{{$f$ is nilpotent}}\urcorner
is
by Proposition
18.29
.)

18.
Sheaves of rings, algebras, and modules

Proposition 18.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module. Properties of
\mathcal{E}
and of the
induced
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
are related as follows:

•

\mathcal{E}
is finite locally free if and only if
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
is finite free as
an
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module from the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

•

\mathcal{E}
is of finite type if and only if
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
is finitely generated as
an
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module from the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

•

\mathcal{E}
is of finite presentation if and only if
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
is finitely
presented as an
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module from the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

The “if” directions follow just as in
Proposition
4.3
. The proofs of the “only if”
directions can too proceed by hand, further exploiting only that generators and
relations are stable under base change. But there’s also a more conceptual
proof: From the point of view of
\mathrm{Sh}(S)
, the
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
admits
the description
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Zar}}=\text{\textul{$\mathcal{E}$}}\otimes_{\text{\textul{$\mathcal{O}_{S}$}}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
, where the
underline denotes the constant sheaf construction (which externally is
interpreted by the functor
\pi^{-1}
described in
Section
16
). By
Lemma
12.49
(generalized from
locales to toposes), the
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{E}
inherits any property
from
\mathcal{E}
which is of a certain logical form, and the properties under
discussion are stable under tensoring.
∎

An analogous result holds for sheaves of algebras and their finiteness
conditions.

Coherence is missing from the list in
Proposition
18.1
since coherence is not stable
under pullback (for instance, the pullback of
\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(\mathbb{Z})}
is not stable
under any morphism
X\to\Spec(\mathbb{Z})
for which
\mathcal{O}_{X}
is not coherent) and can
therefore not be characterized by any formula in the internal language of a
big Zariski topos whose site is sufficiently encompassing.

However, coherence is stable under pullback along locally finitely presented
morphisms. Therefore we do have the following result.

Proposition 18.2
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module. If the induced
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
is
coherent from the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, then
\mathcal{E}
is coherent.
The converse holds if
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is defined using one of the parsimonious sites.

Proposition 18.3
.

The inclusion functor

\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{Sh}(S)}(\mathcal{O}_{S})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{Zar}(S)}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}),\ \mathcal{E}\longmapsto\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Zar}}

is fully faithful.

This follows from the following string of isomorphisms, employing
the adjunction
\pi^{-1}\dashv\pi_{*}
and the fact that
\pi_{*}
commutes with
tensor product by Lemma
16.6
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Mod}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}(\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Zar}},\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{Zar}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Mod}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}(\pi^{-1}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}},\pi^{-1}\mathcal{F}\otimes_{\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S})}(\pi^{-1}\mathcal{E},\pi^{-1}\mathcal{F}\otimes_{\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathcal{O}_{S})}(\mathcal{E},\pi_{*}(\pi^{-1}\mathcal{F}\otimes_{\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathcal{O}_{S})}(\mathcal{E},\pi_{*}\pi^{-1}\mathcal{F}\otimes_{\pi_{*}\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S}}\pi_{*}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathcal{O}_{S})}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathcal{O}_{S})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathcal{O}_{S})}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}).\qed

Caveat 18.4
.

The inclusion functor from the category of
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules to the
category of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules is right exact, and preserves even arbitrary
colimits, but is not left exact.

18.1.
Relative spectrum

Definition 18.5
.

In the context of a specified local ring
R
, the
synthetic spectrum
of an
R
-algebra
A
is

\Spec(A)\vcentcolon=[A,R]_{\mathrm{Alg}(R)},

the set of
R
-algebra homomorphisms from
A
to
R
.

Example 18.6
.

The synthetic spectrum of
R
is the one-element set.
More generally, the synthetic spectrum of the algebra
R[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})

is the solution set
\{x\,{:}\,R^{n}\,|\,f_{1}(x)=\cdots=f_{n}(x)=0\}
.

Example 18.7
.

The synthetic spectrum of
R/(f)
is
{\llbracket{f=0}\rrbracket}
, the truth value
of the formula “
f=0
”, the subsingleton set
\{\star\,|\,f=0\}
.
If classical logic is available, then this set contains
\star
or is empty,
depending on whether
f
is zero or not. Similarly, the synthetic spectrum
of
R[f^{-1}]
is
{\llbracket{\ulcorner\text{{$f$ inv.{}}}\urcorner}\rrbracket}
.

Proposition 18.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{0}
be an
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra (not necessarily quasicoherent).
Then the synthetic spectrum of the
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
(\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
, as constructed
in the internal language of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, is the functor of points of
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

The Hom set occurring in the definition of the synthetic spectrum is
interpreted by the internal Hom when using the internal language. For
any
S
-scheme
f:T\to S
contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, we
have the following chain of isomorphisms.

\displaystyle{\llbracket{\Spec(A)}\rrbracket}(T)

\displaystyle=[(\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]_{\mathrm{Alg}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}(T)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Zar}(S)}(\text{\textul{$T$}},[(\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]_{\mathrm{Alg}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Zar}(S)}(\text{\textul{$T$}}\times(\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})_{\ldots}

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\text{\textul{$T$}}}(\text{\textul{$T$}}\times(\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}},\text{\textul{$T$}}\times{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})_{\ldots}

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathrm{Zar}(T)}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{T}})}((f^{*}\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{T}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathrm{Zar}(T)}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{T}})}(\pi^{-1}(f^{*}\mathcal{A}_{0})\otimes_{\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{T}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{T}},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{T}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathrm{Zar}(T)}(\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{T})}(\pi^{-1}(f^{*}\mathcal{A}_{0}),{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{T}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathrm{Sh}(T)}(\mathcal{O}_{T})}(f^{*}\mathcal{A}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{T})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathrm{Sh}(S)}(\mathcal{O}_{S})}(\mathcal{A}_{0},f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{T})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{S}(T,\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}\mathcal{A}_{0}).

The omitted subscripts “
\ldots
” should denote that we’re only taking the
subset of the Hom set where, for each fixed first argument, the morphisms are
morphisms of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebras.
∎

If
X\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is an arbitrary object, there is a canonical morphism
X\to\Spec([X,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}])
. In the internal language of
\mathrm{Zar}(X)
it looks like the
“inclusion into the double dual”:

x\longmapsto\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt}(x),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where $\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt}(x):[X,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]\to{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}},\ \varphi\mapsto\varphi(x)$.}

The following proposition shows that bijectivity of this map is related to
X

being the functor of points of an affine
S
-scheme (an
S
-scheme whose
structure morphism to
S
is affine).

Proposition 18.9
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be a Zariski sheaf. Consider the following statements:

(1)

The sheaf
X
is isomorphic to the functor of points of an
affine
S
-scheme.

(2)

In the internal language of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
,
the
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
[X,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]
is synthetically quasicoherent
(Definition
18.18
) and the canonical map
X\to\Spec([X,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}])
is bijective.

If one of the parsimonious sites is used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
or if, internally,
the algebra
[X,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]
is finitely presented, then “(2)
\Rightarrow
(1)”.
The converse holds in any of the following
situations:

•

The affine
S
-scheme which
X
represents is of finite presentation
over
S
.

•

The site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is defined using a partial universe
and the affine
S
-scheme which
X
represents is of finite type over
S
.

•

The affine
S
-scheme which
X
represents is contained in the site used
to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. (This situation subsumes the previous ones.)

The direction “(2)
\Rightarrow
(1)” is straightforward, since the assumption
expresses
X
as the functor of points of the relative spectrum of a
quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra. Theorem
18.19
, which is needed
to obtain the sought quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra, is applicable.

For the converse direction, we abuse notation and denote the given
affine
S
-scheme whose functor of points is
X
by “
f:X\to S
”.
Then
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is quasicoherent and the canonical morphism
X\to\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}{f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}}
is an isomorphism. In any of the listed situations, the
internal Hom
[X,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]
is canonically isomorphic to
(f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
, since
for any object
T\xrightarrow{g}S
of the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
we have that

\displaystyle[X,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}](T)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Zar}(S)}(\underline{T},[X,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}])\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Zar}(S)}(\underline{T}\times X,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Zar}(S)}(\underline{T}\times\underline{X},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Zar}(S)}(\underline{T\times_{S}X},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})

\displaystyle\cong{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}(T\times_{S}X)\cong(g^{*}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})(T)=(f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{\mathrm{Zar}}(T).

Therefore
[X,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]
is quasicoherent. The map induced by the isomorphism
X\to\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}{f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}}
on the level of functors of points is precisely the
canonical map
X\to\Spec([X,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}])
as defined in the internal language;
therefore this map is bijective from the internal point of view.
∎

Remark 18.10
.

The condition in Proposition
18.9
that
\Spec(A)
is
representable by an object of the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is slightly
unnatural from a topos-theoretic point of view, since the conclusion of the
Scholium depends only on the topos over the site and not the site itself.
In fact, the condition can be weakened and made more natural at the
same time: It suffices to require that
\Spec(A)
is
locally

representable by an object of the site.

The following corollary answers a question by
Madore
[
93
, entry 2002-07-07:044]
.

Corollary 18.11
.

A morphism
f:X\to S
of schemes is finite if, from
the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, the canonical map
\text{\textul{$X$}}\to\Spec([\text{\textul{$X$}},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}])
is bijective and the
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
[\text{\textul{$X$}},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]

is synthetically quasicoherent and finitely generated. The converse holds
if
X
is locally contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

Immediate using Proposition
18.9
and
Proposition
18.1
.
∎

Remark 18.12
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{0}
be an
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra. Then one can form, internally
to
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, two locales related to
\mathcal{A}_{0}
: the discrete locale on the synthetic
spectrum of
(\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
, and the local spectrum of
(\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
over
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
as described
in Definition
12.4
. These locales don’t coincide. In fact,
the pullback of a discrete locale is discrete, whereas the pullback of
the local spectrum to any of the little Zariski toposes
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
, where
f:X\to S
is an
S
-scheme contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, is the
relative spectrum
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(f^{*}\mathcal{A}_{0})
, which is typically not discrete as
an
X
-locale. (This is because the local spectrum construction is geometric,
by Proposition
12.29
.)

There is, however, a comparison morphism from the discrete locale on the
synthetic spectrum to the local spectrum. On points, it sends
an
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra homomorphism
\varphi:(\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}\to{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
to the
filter
\varphi^{-1}[({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{\times}]
.

One can also form, internally to
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, the classifying topos
of
(\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
-algebras which are local over
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
. This topos doesn’t
coincide with the (toposes of sheaves over) the mentioned two locales, either.
The pullback of that classifying topos to any of the
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
is the big
Zariski topos of
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}(f^{*}\mathcal{A}_{0})
(built using one of the parsimonious
sites).

18.2.
Relative Proj construction

Definition 18.13
.

In the context of a specified local ring
R
, the
synthetic
Proj
of an
\mathbb{N}
-graded
R
-algebra
A
which is generated as an
A_{0}
-algebra
by
A_{1}
is the set

\Proj(A)\vcentcolon=(\text{set of all surj.{} graded $R$-algebra homomorphisms\penalty\ $A\to R[T]$})/R^{\times}.

Example 18.14
.

The synthetic Proj of
R[X_{0},\ldots,X_{n}]
is canonically isomorphic
to the set of points
[x_{0}\mathbin{:}\cdots\mathbin{:}x_{n}]
with at
least one invertible coordinate.

Proposition 18.15
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an
\mathbb{N}
-graded
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra (not necessarily quasicoherent).
Assume that
\mathcal{A}
is generated as an
\mathcal{A}_{0}
-algebra by
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.
Then the synthetic Proj of the
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
, as constructed
in the internal language of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, is the functor of points of
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Proj}$}}}_{S}\mathcal{A}
.

We omit the somewhat tedious verification.
∎

The following corollary was prompted by a question on
MathOverflow
[
37
]
. We are grateful to Yuhao Huang for the impulse.

Corollary 18.16
.

The big Zariski topos of the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, when constructed using a parsimonious site, classifies the theory of “a local
ring together with a point
[a\mathbin{:}b]
” (that is a pair
(a,b)
of ring elements,
where at least one coordinate is invertible, up to multiplication by units).
Explicitly, this theory is given by:

(1)

A sort
A
together with function symbols, constants, and axioms expressing
that
A
is a local ring.

(2)

A sort
P
(to be thought of as the set of
[a\mathbin{:}b]
with
a,b\,{:}\,A
where at
least one coordinate is invertible) together with a
relation
\langle\cdot,\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
A\times A\times P
and the
following axioms:

•

\ulcorner\text{{$a$ inv.{}}}\urcorner\vee\ulcorner\text{{$b$ inv.{}}}\urcorner\dashv\vdash_{a,b{:}A}\exists p\,{:}\,P\mathpunct{.}\,\langle a,b,p\rangle

•

\langle a,b,p\rangle\wedge\langle a,b,p^{\prime}\rangle\vdash_{a,b{:}A,\,p,p^{\prime}{:}P}p=p^{\prime}

•

\top\vdash_{p{:}P}\exists a,b\,{:}\,A\mathpunct{.}\,\langle a,b,p\rangle

•

\langle a,b,p\rangle\wedge\langle a^{\prime},b^{\prime},p\rangle\dashv\vdash_{a,a^{\prime},b,b^{\prime}{:}A,\,p{:}P}\exists s\,{:}\,A\mathpunct{.}\,\ulcorner\text{{$s$ inv.{}}}\urcorner\wedge a^{\prime}=sa\wedge b^{\prime}=sb

(3)

A constant of sort
P
.

The big Zariski topos of
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is a topos over the big
Zariski topos of
\Spec(\mathbb{Z})
; from the point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(\Spec(\mathbb{Z}))
, it
is the classifying topos of a point
[a\mathbin{:}b]
where
a,b\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{\Spec Z}}
,
since
\mathrm{Zar}(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}})\simeq\mathrm{Zar}(\Spec(\mathbb{Z}))/\text{\textul{$\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}$}}

as discussed in Section
16.3
and
\text{\textul{$\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}$}}\cong\{[a\mathbin{:}b]\,|\,a,b\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{\Spec Z}}\}
by Proposition
18.15
and
Example
18.14
. The big Zariski topos of
\Spec(\mathbb{Z})

classifies local rings. Therefore the claim follows by considering the
combined geometric theory.

An alternative proof builds upon Remark
16.4
and
the description of the theory which the little Zariski topos of
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}

classifies (Proposition
12.36
). Combining these,
we see that
\mathrm{Zar}(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}})
classifies the theory of a homogeneous filter
F

of
\mathbb{Z}[X,Y]
meeting the irrelevant ideal together with a local
homomorphism
\alpha:\mathbb{Z}[X,Y][F^{-1}]_{0}\to A
into a local ring
A
. Such data
gives rise to a point
[\alpha(X/u):\alpha(Y/u)]
, where
u
is a
homogeneous element of degree 1 contained in
F
; and conversely any
point
[a\mathbin{:}b]
gives rise to a filter

F\vcentcolon=\{f\in\mathbb{Z}[X,Y]\,|\,\text{$f_{n}(a,b)$ is invertible in\penalty\ $A$
for some\penalty\ $n\geq 0$}\},

where
f_{n}
is the homogeneous component of degree
n
of
f
, and a local
homomorphism
\alpha:\mathbb{Z}[X,Y][F^{-1}]_{0}\to A
mapping
f/g

to
f(a,b)/g(a,b)
.
∎

18.3.
Quasicoherence

The goal of this section is to give an internal
characterization of quasicoherence. We’ll build several notions of synthetic
algebraic geometry on quasicoherence; it is therefore central to the theory.

Lemma 18.17
.

Let
E
be an
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module. Let
S=\bigcup_{i}U_{i}
be an open covering such
that the restrictions
E|_{\mathrm{Zar}(U_{i})}
are quasicoherent, that is of the
form
(\mathcal{E}_{i})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
for quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{U_{i}}
-modules
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. Then
E
is
quasicoherent, that is of the form
(\mathcal{E}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
for a
quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{E}_{0}
.

The given modules
\mathcal{E}_{i}
glue to a
quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{E}_{0}
, and the sheaf condition ensures that
E
is
isomorphic to
(\mathcal{E}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
. Details are given
in
[
126
,
Tag 03DN
]
.
∎

Definition 18.18
.

An
R
-module
E
is
synthetically quasicoherent
if and only if,
for any finitely presented
R
-algebra
A
, the canonical
R
-module
homomorphism

E\otimes_{R}A\longrightarrow[\Spec(A),E]=[[A,R]_{\mathrm{Alg}(R)},E]

which maps a pure tensor
x\otimes f
to the function
(\varphi\mapsto\varphi(f)x)
is
bijective. Here and in the following, the set
[\Spec(A),E]
is the set of all
maps
\Spec(A)\to E
, and
[A,R]_{\mathrm{Alg}(R)}
is the set of all
R
-algebra
homomorphisms
A\to R
.

This definition has the following interpretation. The codomain of the displayed
canonical map is the set of all
E
-valued functions on
\Spec(A)
. Elements
of
E\otimes_{R}A
induce such functions; these induced functions can
reasonably be called “algebraic”. In a synthetic context, there should be no
other
E
-valued functions as these algebraic ones, and different algebraic
expressions should yield different functions. This is precisely what the
postulated bijectivity expresses.

The notion of synthetic quasicoherence is only meaningful in an intuitionistic
context. For instance, even
R
itself can’t be synthetically quasicoherent in
the presence of the law of excluded middle, since it forces the canonical evaluation
morphism
R[T]\to[R,R]
(obtained by setting
A\vcentcolon=R[T]
in the
definition of synthetic quasicoherence) to never be bijective: If
R
is
finite, then the evaluation morphism isn’t injective, since
\prod_{x\in R}(T-x)

is mapped to the same function as the zero polynomial is. If
R
is
infinite, then
R[T]
has cardinality
|R|
while the set of functions
R\to R
has strictly greater cardinality.

Theorem 18.19
.

Let
E\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be an
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module.
If
E
is quasicoherent, that is of
the form
(\mathcal{E}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
for some quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{E}_{0}
,
then
E
is synthetically quasicoherent from the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.
The converse holds in any of the following situations:

(1)

The site used to construct
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is one of the parsimonious sites.

(2)

The functor
E
maps directed limits of inverse systems of
S
-schemes with
affine transition morphisms to colimits in
\mathrm{Set}
.

(3)

From the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, the module
E
is even
finitely presented.

Let
E=(\mathcal{E}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
for a quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{E}_{0}
.
To verify that
E
is synthetically quasicoherent, we have to
verify a condition for
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebras
A
in any
slice
\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\text{\textul{$T$}}
. If such an algebra is finitely presented from
the internal point of view, then there is a covering
T=\bigcup_{i}T_{i}
such
that each of the restrictions of the algebra to the schemes
T_{i}
is of the
form
(\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
for some finitely presented
\mathcal{O}_{T_{i}}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.
Without loss of generality, we will just assume that
A
itself is of the
form
(\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
for a finitely presented
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

By Proposition
18.8
, the interpretation
{\llbracket{\Spec(A)}\rrbracket}
of the internal spectrum is the functor of
points of
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}\mathcal{A}_{0}
. For any
S
-scheme
f:T\to S
contained in the site
used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, we consider the fiber product

\textstyle{\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{T}(f^{*}\mathcal{A}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{p^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}\mathcal{A}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{S.}

Since
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{T}(f^{*}\mathcal{A}_{0})\to S
is contained in the site (for any of our
admissible sites), we may conclude using the following chain of isomorphisms:

\displaystyle[\Spec(A),E](T)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Zar}(S)}(\text{\textul{$T$}},[\Spec(A),E])\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Zar}(S)}(\text{\textul{$T$}}\times\Spec(A),E)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Zar}(S)}(\underline{T\times_{S}\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}\mathcal{A}_{0}},E)\cong E(\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{T}(f^{*}\mathcal{A}_{0}))

\displaystyle\cong\Gamma(\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{T}(f^{*}\mathcal{A}_{0}),(p^{\prime})^{*}f^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0})\cong\Gamma(T,(p^{\prime})_{*}(p^{\prime})^{*}f^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0})

\displaystyle\cong\Gamma(T,f^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{T}}f^{*}\mathcal{A}_{0})\cong(\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}})(T)

\displaystyle\cong((\mathcal{E}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}\otimes_{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}(\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}})(T)\cong(E\otimes_{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}A)(T).

The antepenultimate isomorphism is because pullback of modules in
\mathrm{Sh}(S)
to
modules in
\mathrm{Sh}(T)
commutes with tensor product. The penultimate isomorphism
is because pullback of a sheaf in
\mathrm{Sh}(S)
to a sheaf in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
commutes
with tensor product (Lemma
16.6
).

For the converse direction, we first verify that the restriction
E|_{\mathrm{Sh}(T)}

to the little Zariski topos of any
S
-scheme
T
contained in the site used
to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is a quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{T}
-module. For this, we employ the
quasicoherence criterion of Theorem
8.3
: For any open
affine subset
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
and any function
h\in\Gamma(T^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{T})
we
verify that the canonical morphism

(
\dagger
)

E|_{\mathrm{Sh}(T)}[h^{-1}]\longrightarrow j_{*}(E|_{\mathrm{Sh}(D(h))})

is an isomorphism, where
j:D(h)\hookrightarrow T^{\prime}
denotes the inclusion.
This follows from the assumption of synthetic quasicoherence by considering
the
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
A\vcentcolon={\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[h^{-1}]
(in the slice
\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\text{\textul{$T^{\prime}$}}
):
This expresses that the canonical morphism

(
\ddagger
)

E\otimes_{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[h^{-1}]\longrightarrow[\Spec(A),E]

is an isomorphism (of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\text{\textul{$T^{\prime}$}}
). Restricting the
domain to
\mathrm{Sh}(T^{\prime})
yields the sheaf
E|_{\mathrm{Sh}(T^{\prime})}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{T^{\prime}}}\mathcal{O}_{T^{\prime}}[h^{-1}]
, since restricting commutes with the geometric constructions
“forming the tensor product” and “localizing away from
h
”.
Since
\Spec(A)
is the functor of points of
D(h)
, restricting
the codomain to
\mathrm{Sh}(T^{\prime})
yields the sheaf
j_{*}(E|_{\mathrm{Sh}(D(h))})
.
The canonical morphism (

†

‣
18.3
) which we want to recognize as an
isomorphism is therefore the restriction of the canonical
morphism (

‡

‣
18.3
) which we know to be an isomorphism.

A natural candidate for a quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{E}_{0}
with
E\cong(\mathcal{E}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
is
\mathcal{E}_{0}\vcentcolon=E|_{\mathrm{Sh}(S)}
. We’ll show that this candidate indeed fits.
Let
f:T\to S
be any
S
-scheme contained in the site used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. We assume for the time being that
f
is of finite
presentation and affine, so
T\cong\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}\mathcal{A}_{0}
for some finitely
presented
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{0}
. We want to verify that the canonical morphism

(§)

f^{*}(E|_{\mathrm{Sh}(S)})\longrightarrow E|_{\mathrm{Sh}(T)}

is an isomorphism. Since the functor
f_{*}
from quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{T}
-modules to
quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules reflects isomorphisms (the morphism
f
being affine)
and the domain and codomain of morphism (
§
‣
18.3
) are quasicoherent, it suffices to
verify that its image under
f_{*}
is an isomorphism. This image is the
canonical morphism

E|_{\mathrm{Sh}(S)}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathcal{A}_{0}\longrightarrow f_{*}(E|_{\mathrm{Sh}(T)}).

The assumption of synthetic quasicoherence, applied to the finitely presented
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
A\vcentcolon=(\mathcal{A}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
, shows that this morphism is an isomorphism.

In situation (1), the only step left to do is to generalize the argument in the
previous paragraph to morphisms
f:T\to S
which are locally of finite
presentation. This works out because there are open covers of
S
and
T
such
that the appropriate restrictions of
f
are of finite presentation and affine.
The assumption of synthetic quasicoherence then needs to be applied
to
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebras in suitable slices of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, showing that the canonical
morphism (
§
‣
18.3
) is locally an isomorphism and therefore globally
as well.

In situation (2), we may by Lemma
18.17
assume without loss of generality that
S
is affine.
We then employ the technique of approximating general
S
-schemes
by
S
-schemes of finite presentation. Specifically, let
f:T\to S
be an
arbitrary
S
-scheme contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. Without
loss of generality, we may assume that
T
is an affine scheme. Thus
T
is
quasicompact and quasiseparated, and
S
is quasiseparated since it is affine. We may therefore
apply the lemma of relative approximation
[
126
,
Tag 09MV
]
to deduce
that
T
is a directed limit of an inverse system of
S
-schemes
f_{i}:T_{i}\to S
of finite presentation with affine transition maps. These
S
-schemes
are contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. Furthermore, they inherit
quasicompactness and quasiseparatedness from
S
. Therefore we can apply a
comparison result on the categories of quasicoherent
modules
[
126
,
Tag 01Z0
]
:

E(T)=E(\lim_{i}T_{i})\cong\colim_{i}E(T_{i})\cong\colim_{i}\Gamma(T_{i},f_{i}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0})\cong\Gamma(T,f^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}).

In situation (3), we may assume by Lemma
18.17
that
E
is
the cokernel of a morphism
\alpha:({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{m}\to({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{n}
of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules.
This morphism induces a morphism
\alpha|_{\mathrm{Sh}(S)}:\mathcal{O}_{S}^{m}\to\mathcal{O}_{S}^{n}

of
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules. One can then check that
E
is canonically isomorphic
to
(\cok(\alpha|_{\mathrm{Sh}(S)}))^{\mathrm{Zar}}
, by using that the restriction
functors
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{Zar}(S)}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})\to\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
are jointly
conservative and right exact.
∎

Scholium 18.20
.

Let
E\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be a quasicoherent
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module.
Let
A\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be a quasicoherent
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra such that
{\llbracket{\Spec(A)}\rrbracket}\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is representable by an object of the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.
Then the canonical morphism

E\otimes_{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}A\longrightarrow[\Spec(A),E]

is an isomorphism.

The second paragraph of the proof of
Theorem
18.19
applies.∎

Remark 18.21
.

As noted in Remark
18.10
in a slightly
different context, the condition in Scholium
18.20
that
\Spec(A)
is
representable by an object of the site is unnatural from a topos-theoretic
point of view and should be weakened to require only local representability.

However, the condition can’t be dropped completely. For instance, if we employ the
parsimonious sites and consider
S=\Spec\mathbb{Z}
,
E={\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
, and
A=(\mathcal{K}_{S})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
(where
\mathcal{K}_{S}
is the sheaf of rational functions on
S
, which in
this case is the constant sheaf
\mathbb{Q}
), then
{\llbracket{\Spec(A)}\rrbracket}
is the functor of
points of the
\mathbb{Z}
-scheme
\Spec(\mathbb{Q})
. By Proposition
11.19
, this functor coincides with the
functor of points of the empty
\mathbb{Z}
-scheme on the parsimonious sites;
therefore
\Spec(A)=\emptyset
from the internal point of view. Thus the
codomain of the canonical morphism is the zero algebra, but the domain is not.

The internal quasicoherence condition for the little Zariski topos
(Theorem
8.3
) is related to the notion of synthetic
quasicoherence as follows. Recall that an
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is quasicoherent
if and only if, from the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Sh}(S)
, the localized
module
\mathcal{E}_{0}[f^{-1}]
is a sheaf with respect to the modal operator
(\ulcorner\text{{$f$
inv.{}}}\urcorner\Rightarrow\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt})
for any function
f\,{:}\,\mathcal{O}_{S}
. The sublocale
associated with this modal operator is the open sublocale
j:\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{S}[f^{-1}]|\mathcal{O}_{S})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{pt}
. The condition can therefore also
be put in the form

\mathrm{Sh}(S)\models\forall f\,{:}\,\mathcal{O}_{S}\mathpunct{.}\,\ulcorner\text{{$\mathcal{E}_{0}[f^{-1}]\longrightarrow j_{*}j^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{0}[f^{-1}])$ is bijective}}\urcorner.

One can verify that the functor
j_{*}\circ j^{-1}
is canonically isomorphic to the functor
[{\llbracket{\ulcorner\text{{$f$ inv.{}}}\urcorner}\rrbracket},\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt}]
, hence to the functor
[\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{S}[f^{-1}]),\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt}]
.
Since
\mathcal{E}_{0}[f^{-1}]\cong\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathcal{O}_{S}[f^{-1}]
, the condition can
therefore also be put in the form

\mathrm{Sh}(S)\models\forall f\,{:}\,\mathcal{O}_{S}\mathpunct{.}\,\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathcal{O}_{S}[f^{-1}]\longrightarrow[\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{S}[f^{-1}]),\mathcal{E}_{0}].

The synthetic quasicoherence condition therefore implies the condition which
characterizes quasicoherence in the little Zariski topos as a special case.

Lemma 18.22
.

Let
J\hookrightarrow{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
be an ideal such that
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J
is
of the form
(\mathcal{E}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
for an
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{E}_{0}
. Let
\mathcal{I}
be the kernel
of the epimorphism
\mathcal{O}_{S}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{0}
induced by the quotient
morphism
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\to{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J
. Then, for any
S
-scheme
(f:X\to S)

contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, there is a canonical
isomorphism

\im(f^{*}\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{O}_{X})\cong J|_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}.

The short exact sequence
0\to J\to{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\to{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J\to 0

of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
remains exact when restricted to
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
, since
restricting to
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
is taking the inverse image along a geometric morphism.
Hence the sequence
0\to J|_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}\to f^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\to 0
is exact.
On the other hand, the short exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{O}_{S}\to\mathcal{E}_{0}\to 0

yields the short exact sequence
0\to\im(f^{*}\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{O}_{X})\to\mathcal{O}_{X}\to f^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\to 0
.
∎

Remark 18.23
.

The quotient
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/\sqrt{(0)}
in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is an example
for a sheaf of modules which is not quasicoherent even though all of its
restrictions to the little Zariski toposes
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
for morphisms
f:X\to S

are:
Since taking the quotient and taking the radical of an ideal are geometric
constructions, we have
({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/\sqrt{(0)})|_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}/\sqrt{(0)}
.
These sheaves of modules are quasicoherent (Example
8.7
).
However, in general,
f^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{S}/\sqrt{(0)})\not\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}/\sqrt{(0)}
.
A specific counterexample is
S=\Spec(k)
and
X=\Spec(k[T]/(T^{2}))
.
In this case
f^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{S}/\sqrt{(0)})\cong f^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{S})\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}\not\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}/\sqrt{(0)}
.

Caveat 18.24
.

The kernel of a morphism of quasicoherent
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules,
computed in the category of all
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules, is in general not
quasicoherent. This fact is evident from Lemma
18.22
. In the
notation of that lemma, the
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module
J
is in general not quasicoherent,
since if it was, the restriction
J|_{\mathrm{Sh}(X)}
would be canonically isomorphic
to
f^{*}\mathcal{I}
.

The category of quasicoherent
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules possesses kernels, since it is
equivalent to the category of quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules by
Proposition
18.3
, but the inclusion from
quasicoherent
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules to arbitrary
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules does not preserve
them.

Lemma 18.25
.

Let
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be defined using one of the parsimonious sites. Then tensor
products of synthetically quasicoherent modules and cokernels of morphisms
between synthetically quasicoherent modules are synthetically quasicoherent
from the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

By Theorem
18.19
and the description of the
slice toposes of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
given in Section
16.3
, the
first claim reduces to the fact that the tensor product of
quasicoherent
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules is quasicoherent. Indeed, the tensor product
of
(\mathcal{E}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
with
(\mathcal{F}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
is
(\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathcal{F}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
by
Lemma
16.6
and the tensor product of
quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules is quasicoherent.

The second claim reduces to the statement that the cokernel of a morphism
\alpha:(\mathcal{E}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}\to(\mathcal{F}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
, calculated in the category of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules,
coincides with
(\cok(\alpha|_{\mathrm{Sh}(S)}))^{\sim}
and that the cokernel of a
morphism of quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules is quasicoherent.
∎

It’s somewhat embarassing that we didn’t give an internal proof of
Lemma
18.25
. Also we don’t know whether the result
holds when using one of the larger sites (the given proof doesn’t generalize to
this situation since Theorem
18.19
can’t be applied). We
expand on this in Section
22
.

Lemma 18.26
.

Let
A
and
B
be
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebras. The canonical
map

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}(A,B)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Zar}(S)}({\llbracket{\Spec(B)}\rrbracket},{\llbracket{\Spec(A)}\rrbracket})

is bijective in the following
situations:

(1)

The algebra
B
is finitely presented.

(2)

The algebra
B
is quasicoherent and the functor
{\llbracket{\Spec(B)}\rrbracket}\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is locally representable by an object of the site used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. (This situation subsumes the previous one.)

By Scholium
18.20
, the canonical morphism
B\to[\Spec(B),{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]
is an isomorphism. Hence the claim follows by the following
entirely formal calculation:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}\mathrm{Hom}({\llbracket{\Spec(B)}\rrbracket},{\llbracket{\Spec(A)}\rrbracket})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Hom}([B,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]_{\mathrm{Alg}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})},[A,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]_{\mathrm{Alg}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}([B,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]_{\mathrm{Alg}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}\times A,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})_{\text{${\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}$-homomorphism in
the second argument}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}(A,[[B,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]_{\mathrm{Alg}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}])

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}(A,[{\llbracket{\Spec(B)}\rrbracket},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}])

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}(A,B).\qed

It’s a basic fact that for an
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
, the canonical map

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}(\mathcal{O}_{S})}(\mathcal{A}_{0},f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{LRL}/S}(X,\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}(\mathcal{A}_{0}))

is bijective for any
S
-scheme
(f:X\to S)
. One should not expect that the
similar statement that for an
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
A
, the canonical map

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}(A,[X,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}])\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Zar}(S)}(X,{\llbracket{\Spec(A)}\rrbracket})

is bijective for arbitrary functors
X\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is bijective, holds. This
is because the objects of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
are quite a bit more general than locally
ringed spaces (or locales) over
S
.

18.4.
Properties of the affine line

The ring object
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
in the big Zariski topos enjoys several special
properties, some of which are unique in that they’re only possible in an
intuitionistic context. We compile here a short list of such
properties. As was already mentioned, at least one of them, the field
property, was already noticed in the 1970s by Kock
[
82
]
.

The statements and proofs in this subsection are formulated in the internal
language. The proofs only use the fact that
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is a synthetically
quasicoherent local ring. This supports the meta-claim that synthetic
quasicoherence is a strong and meaningful condition.

Proposition 18.27
.

{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is a field in the sense that any element which is not zero is
invertible:
\forall x\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\mathpunct{.}\,\neg(x=0)\Rightarrow\ulcorner\text{{$x$ inv.{}}}\urcorner
. More generally,
for any number
n\geq 0
,

\qquad\qquad\forall x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\mathpunct{.}\,\neg(x_{1}=0\wedge\cdots\wedge x_{n}=0)\Longrightarrow\\
(\ulcorner\text{{$x_{1}$ inv.{}}}\urcorner\vee\cdots\vee\ulcorner\text{{$x_{n}$ inv.{}}}\urcorner).\qquad\qquad

Let
x\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
be such that
\neg(x=0)
. We consider the quasicoherence
condition for the finitely presented
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
A\vcentcolon={\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/(x)
.
Since
\Spec(A)\cong{\llbracket{x=0}\rrbracket}={\llbracket{\bot}\rrbracket}=\emptyset
, the condition posits
that the canonical homomorphism

{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/(x)\longrightarrow[\emptyset,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]

is an isomorphism. Since its codomain is the zero algebra, so is
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/(x)
.
Therefore
1\in(x)
, that is,
x
is invertible.

The more general statement follows in the same way, by using the quasicoherence
condition for
A\vcentcolon={\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. This yields
1\in(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. Since
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is a local ring, one of the
x_{i}
is
invertible.∎

Proposition 18.28
.

{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is not a reduced ring:

\neg\bigl(\forall x\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\mathpunct{.}\,\bigl(\bigvee_{n\geq 0}x^{n}=0\bigr)\Rightarrow x=0\bigr).

Assume that
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is reduced. Then the set
\Delta\vcentcolon=\{\varepsilon\in{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\,|\,\varepsilon^{2}=0\}
is equal to
\{0\}
.
By the quasicoherence criterion applied to the finitely
presented
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
A\vcentcolon={\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T]/(T^{2})
, the canonical map

{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T]/(T^{2})\longrightarrow[\Spec({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T]/(T^{2})),{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]\cong[\Delta,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]

is an isomorphism. It maps
[T]
to zero (the value of
T
at
0\in\Delta
).
Thus
T\in(T^{2})
and therefore
1=0
in
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
. This is a contradiction.
∎

In classical logic, Proposition
18.27
and
Proposition
18.28
would directly contradict each
other; only an intuitionistic context allows for fields which are not reduced.

That
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is not reduced, irrespective of the reducedness of the base
scheme
S
, should not come as a surprise: Reducedness is not stable under base
change, but all statements of the internal language of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
are.
If
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
was reduced, then all
S
-schemes (at least those contained in the
site used to construct
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
) would be reduced as well. In contrast, the
structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{S}
is reduced from the point of view of the little Zariski
topos if and only if
S
is reduced (Proposition
3.3
).

Proposition 18.29
.

The following statements about an element
x\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
are
equivalent:

(1)

x
is not invertible.

(2)

x
is nilpotent.

(3)

x
is
not not
zero.

Let
x\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
be not invertible. We consider the quasicoherence
condition for the finitely presented
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
A\vcentcolon={\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[x^{-1}]
.
Since
\Spec(A)\cong{\llbracket{\ulcorner\text{{$x$ inv.{}}}\urcorner}\rrbracket}=\emptyset
, it follows
that
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[x^{-1}]=0
, similarly to the proof of
Proposition
18.27
. Thus
x

is nilpotent.

Let
x\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
be a nilpotent element. Thus
x^{n}=0
for some number
n\geq 0
. If
x
was nonzero, then
x
and therefore
x^{n}
would be invertible, in
contradiction to
0\neq 1
since
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is a local ring.

Let
x\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
be
not not
zero. Then
x
is not invertible, since if
x
was
invertible, then
x
would be nonzero.
∎

Summarizing, the following facts about nilpotents hold in the internal
universe of the big Zariski topos. Firstly, it’s not true that
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is
reduced. But this doesn’t mean that there actually exist nilpotent elements
which are not zero. In fact, any nilpotent is
not not
zero.

Proposition 18.30
.

Any function
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\to{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is given by a unique polynomial
in
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T]
.

Immediate by considering the quasicoherence condition for the finitely
presented
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
A\vcentcolon={\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T]
and noticing that
\Spec(A)\cong{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
.∎

This statement too cannot be satisfied in classical logic: for infinite
fields the existence part fails and for finite fields the uniqueness part
fails.

In synthetic differential geometry, the
axiom of microaffinity
is
central to the theory. It is fulfilled by the image of
\mathbb{R}^{1}
in any
well-adapted model of synthetic differential geometry, and also by
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. This fact is well-known; we include the proof only to show that it
is a consequence of synthetic quasicoherence.

Proposition 18.31
.

{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
fulfills the axiom of microaffinity: Let
\Delta=\{\varepsilon\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\,|\,\varepsilon^{2}=0\}
. Let
f:\Delta\to{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
be an arbitrary
function. Then there are unique elements
a,b\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
such
that
f(\varepsilon)=a+b\varepsilon
for all
\varepsilon\,{:}\,\Delta
.

Immediate from the definition of synthetic quasicoherence,
considering the finitely presented
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T]/(T^{2})
.
∎

Proposition 18.32
.

{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is
anonymously algebraically closed
, in the following sense:
Any monic polynomial
p\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T]
of degree at least one does
not not
have
a zero.

Let
p\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T]
be a monic polynomial of degree at least one. Assume
that
p
doesn’t have a zero in
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
. Then the spectrum of
A\vcentcolon={\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T]/(p)
is empty. The quasicoherence condition for
A
therefore implies
that
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T]/(p)
is zero. This means that
p
is invertible in
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T]
.
A basic lemma in commutative algebra (whose standard proof is constructive)
then implies that with the exception of the constant term in
p
, all
coefficients are nilpotent. This contradicts the assumption that
p
is monic
of degree at least one.∎

Proposition 18.33
.

{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is infinite in the following sense: For any number
n\geq 0

and any given elements
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
, there is
not not
an element
y

which is distinct from all of the
x_{i}
.

The polynomial
f(T)\vcentcolon=(T-x_{1})\cdots(T-x_{n})+1

does
not not
have a zero
y\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
, since
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is anonymously algebraically
closed. This element cannot equal any of the elements
x_{i}
, since
