f(x_{i})=1
is not zero.
∎

Proposition 18.34
.

{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
fulfills the following version of the Nullstellensatz:
Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
be polynomials without a common
zero in
({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{n}
. Then there are polynomials
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\in{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
such that
\sum_{i}g_{i}f_{i}=1
.

We consider the quasicoherence condition for the finitely
presented
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
A\vcentcolon={\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})
.
Since
\Spec(A)\cong\{x\in({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{n}\,|\,f_{1}(x)=\ldots=f_{m}(x)=0\}=\emptyset
, the condition implies that
A
is the zero algebra just as in the
verification of Proposition
18.27
.
∎

Remark 18.35
.

The Krull dimension of the ring
\mathcal{O}_{S}
of the little Zariski topos
coincides with the dimension of
S

(Proposition
3.13
). The analogous statement
for
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
in the big Zariski topos is false. Unless
S
is the empty scheme,
the internal statement

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\ulcorner\text{{${\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}$ is of Krull dimension\penalty\ $\leq n$}}\urcorner

is false for any natural number
n\geq 0
: Since the property of having Krull
dimension
\leq n
is a geometric implication, this statement would imply that
for any
S
-scheme
X
(contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
) the
ring
\mathcal{O}_{X}
in
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
is of Krull dimension
\leq n
.

The ring
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is therefore an example for a ring of infinite
Krull dimension which nevertheless fulfills a field condition. A ring in the
big Zariski topos which does reflect the dimension of
S
is
\flat{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
. The
scheme
S
is of dimension
\leq n
if and only if
\flat{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is of Krull
dimension
\leq n
from the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

19.
Basic constructions of relative scheme theory

With
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
at hand, we can perform many of the usual constructions of
(relative) scheme theory internally.

19.1.
Group schemes

The functors associated to the standard group schemes
\mathbb{G}_{\text{a}}
,
\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}
,

\mathrm{GL}_{n}
, and
\mu_{n}
are given by the internal expressions

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{\text{a}}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon={\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\text{ (as an additive group)},

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{x\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\,|\,\ulcorner\text{{$x$ inv.{}}}\urcorner\},

\displaystyle\mathrm{GL}_{n}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{M\,{:}\,({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{n\times n}\,|\,\ulcorner\text{{$M$ inv.{}}}\urcorner\},

\displaystyle\mu_{n}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{x\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\,|\,x^{n}=1\}.

19.2.
Affine and projective space

Affine
n
-space over
S
is given by
({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{n}
, i. e. internally the set
of
n
-tuples of elements of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
. The functor of points of
projective
n
-space over
X
, with all its nontrivial topological and
ring-theoretical structure, is described by the naive expression

\mathbb{P}^{n}\vcentcolon=\{(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n})\,{:}\,({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{n+1}\,|\,x_{0}\neq 0\vee\cdots\vee x_{n}\neq 0\}/{\sim},

where the equivalence relation is the usual rescaling relation from the
internal point of view. This example was suggested by Zhen Lin Low (private
communication).

More generally, for an
S
-scheme
X
, affine and projective
n
-space
over
X
are given by
\text{\textul{$X$}}\times({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{n}
and
\text{\textul{$X$}}\times\mathbb{P}^{n}
,
respectively.

19.3.
Tangent bundle

For an
S
-scheme
X
, the internal Hom
[\Delta,\text{\textul{$X$}}]\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
describes the
tangent bundle of
X
, i. e. the
S
-scheme
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}{\operatorname{Sym}(\Omega^{1}_{X/S})}\to X\to S
, as can be seen by
chasing the definitions
[
28
, Lemma 5.12.1]
.
Intuitively, a map
f:\Delta\to\text{\textul{$X$}}
from the internal point of view is
given by slightly more data than merely the point
f(0)
; one also has to
specify first-order information.

This description of the (not necessarily
locally trivial) tangent bundle fits nicely with the intuition of tangent
vectors as infinitesimal curves, and in fact is precisely the definition of the
tangent bundle in synthetic differential geometry
[
81
, Def. 7.1]
.

19.4.
The tilde construction

Let
A
be an
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra.
If the functor
{\llbracket{\Spec(A)}\rrbracket}
is representable by a scheme contained in the
site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, then Section
16.3
described
the big Zariski topos of that scheme as the slice topos
\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\Spec(A)
.
From the point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, this topos looks like “
\mathrm{Set}/\Spec(A)
”,
the topos of
\Spec(A)
-indexed families of sets.

In this picture, the three functors
f_{!}\dashv f^{-1}\dashv f_{*}

relating
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
and
\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\Spec(A)
look as follows. We set
X\vcentcolon=\Spec(A)
.

(1)

The functor
f_{!}
maps a family
(M_{x})_{x{:}X}
to its
dependent
sum

\coprod_{x{:}X}M_{x}
, the “total space” of the family.

(2)

The functor
f^{-1}
maps a set
N
to the constant family
(N)_{x{:}X}
.

(3)

The functor
f_{*}
maps a family
(M_{x})_{x\,{:}\,X}
to its
dependent
product
, the set
\prod_{x{:}X}M_{x}
of sections of the
projection
(\coprod_{x{:}X}M_{x})\to X
.

The affine line over
X
is the constant family
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}}=({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})_{x{:}X}
.

We can canonically associate an
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}}
-module
E^{\sim}
to a
given
A
-module
E
. This module lives in the topos
\mathrm{Set}/X
, so
is an
X
-indexed family of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules. Explicitly, it is the family

E^{\sim}\vcentcolon=(E\otimes_{A}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})_{x{:}X},

where for the tensor product in the component
x\,{:}\,X
the ring
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is
regarded as an
A
-algebra by the
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-homomorphism
x:A\to{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
.
To render the dependence of the tensor product on
x
explicit, we use the
familiar notation
E(x)\vcentcolon=E\otimes_{A}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
to denote the fiber of
E

over
x
.

Conversely, given an
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}}
-module
F=(F_{x})_{x{:}X}
, there is a canonically
associated
A
-module given by calculating the dependent product. In analogy
with the classical situation, we write

\Gamma(X,F)\vcentcolon=\prod_{x{:}X}F_{x}

for this module.

A morphism
F\to F^{\prime}
of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}}
-modules is an
X
-indexed family
(F_{x}\to F^{\prime}_{x})_{x{:}X}
of morphism of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules.

Proposition 19.1
.

The functor
(\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt})^{\sim}:\mathrm{Mod}(A)\to\mathrm{Mod}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}})
is
left adjoint to the functor
\Gamma(X,\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt}):\mathrm{Mod}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}})\to\mathrm{Mod}(A)
.

For an
A
-module
E
and an
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}}
-module
F
, the required
bijection
\mathrm{Hom}_{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}}(E^{\sim},F)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(E,\Gamma(X,\mathcal{F}))
is given by

(\alpha_{x}:E(x)\to F_{x})_{x{:}X}\longmapsto(v\mapsto(\alpha_{x}([v\otimes 1]))_{x{:}X})

with inverse given by

(v\otimes f\mapsto f\cdot\beta(v)_{x})\longmapsfrom\beta.\qed

It’s fruitful to study the unit of the adjunction
(\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt})^{\sim}\dashv\Gamma(X,\underline{\hskip 10.00002pt})
in more detail.

Definition 19.2
.

An
A
-module
E
has
cohesive fibers
if and only if the
canonical linear map
E\to\prod_{x{:}X}E(x)
is a bijection.

Ordinary modules in classical logic almost never have cohesive fibers.
Intuitively, the law of excluded middle allows to define “discontinuous”
elements of
\prod_{x{:}X}E(x)
. These can’t be induced from elements
v\,{:}\,E
,
whose values
[v\otimes 1]\,{:}\,E(x)
always “vary continuously” in
x\,{:}\,X
.
In particular, the trivial
A
-module
A
won’t have cohesive fibers in a
classical context, since
A
might not be a product of copies of the base ring.

However, in the intuitionistic context of the internal language of the big
Zariski topos, the law of excluded middle is not available to define
discontinuous families of values. And indeed, we have the following
proposition.

Proposition 19.3
.

Assume that there is a quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{0}
such
that
A=\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
. Further assume that
{\llbracket{\Spec(A)}\rrbracket}
is locally
representable by an object of the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{0}
be a quasicoherent
\mathcal{A}_{0}
-module. Then
(\mathcal{E}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
has cohesive
fibers from the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

This just reflects the fact that the canonical morphism
\mathcal{E}_{0}\to f_{*}((\mathcal{E}_{0})^{\sim})
, where
f:\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\to S
is the structure morphism
of the relative spectrum and
(\mathcal{E}_{0})^{\sim}
is the result of applying the
(ordinary) relative tilde construction to
\mathcal{E}_{0}
, is an
isomorphism
[
126
,
Tag 01SB
]
.
∎

Corollary 19.4
.

Let
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be defined using one of the parsimonious sites. Assume that
A

is a finitely presented
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra. Then the following statements hold
from the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

(1)

The algebra
A
satisfies the following “higher-typed” version of the
synthetic quasicoherence condition: Any
A
-module which is synthetically
quasicoherent as an
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module (for instance because it is finitely
presented) has cohesive fibers.

(2)

For any
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}}
-module
F=(F_{x})_{x{:}X}
such that
F_{x}
is a synthetically
quasicoherent
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module for all
x\,{:}\,X
, the canonical
map
\Gamma(X,F)^{\sim}\to F
is an isomorphism.

The first claim is immediate from
Proposition
19.3
and Theorem
18.19
. The
second claim is an internal rendition of
[
126
,
Tag 01SB
]
.
∎

Remark 19.5
.

Since the internal language of
\mathrm{Set}/\Spec(A)
is just ordinary
language applied to all fibers, the following properties of
an
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}}
-module
F=(F_{x})_{x{:}X}
are equivalent:

(1)

For any
x\,{:}\,X
, the
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module
F_{x}
is synthetically quasicoherent.

(2)

From the point of view of
\mathrm{Set}/\Spec(A)
, the
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}}
-module
F
is
synthetically quasicoherent.

We believe that the condition that
A
is synthetically quasicoherent
is not strong enough to allow an internal proof of
Corollary
19.4
. The conclusion of
Corollary
19.4
seems quite natural from a
synthetic point of view; we therefore propose to adopt it as an axiom for
synthetic algebraic geometry.

19.5.
Open immersions

A basic concept in the functor-of-points approach to algebraic geometry is the
concept of an
open subfunctor
. It is used to delimit schemes from more
general kinds of spaces: A functor is deemed to be a scheme if and only if it
admits a covering by open subfunctors which are representable.

The following definition is phrased in such a way as to apply to any of the
several ways to define the big Zariski topos
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. In particular, it
applies to the definition using the site consisting of affine schemes which are
locally of finite presentation over
S
. If
S
is affine, the definition only
refers to affine schemes and open subschemes of affine schemes and is therefore
suitable if one wants to found the theory of schemes using the functorial
approach.

Definition 19.6

(
[
47
, Définition I.1.3.6 on page 10]
,

[
126
, Tag 01JI]
)
.

A subfunctor
U\hookrightarrow X
in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is an
open subfunctor
if
and only if for any object
(T\to S)
of the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)

and any
x\in X(T)
there exists an open subscheme
T_{0}\subseteq T

such that for any object
(T^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}T\to S)
of the site used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
the map
T^{\prime}\to T
factors over
T_{0}
if and only if
X(f)(x)\in U(T^{\prime})
.

The open subschemes
T_{0}\subseteq T
appearing in this definition are uniquely
determined by their universal property. The relation of open subfunctors to
open immersions is as follows.

Proposition 19.7
.

Let
X
be an
S
-scheme.

(1)

Let
U\subseteq X
be an open subscheme. Then the subfunctor
\text{\textul{$U$}}\hookrightarrow\text{\textul{$X$}}
is open.

(2)

If
X
is locally representable by an object of the site used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, any open subfunctor
U\hookrightarrow\text{\textul{$X$}}
is isomorphic
to the open subfunctor associated to an open subscheme of
X
.

For the first claim, let
(T\to S)
be an object of the site used
to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
and let
x\in\text{\textul{$X$}}(T)
. The open
subscheme
T_{0}\subseteq T
required by the definition of an open subfunctor can
then be chosen as
x^{-1}[U]
.

For the second claim, assuming for notational simplicity that
X
is
directly representable without having to pass to a cover, the desired open
subscheme of
X
can be obtained as the witnessing subscheme “
T_{0}
” as it
appears in the definition of an open subfunctor in the special case
(T\to S)\vcentcolon=(X\to S)
.
∎

From the point of view of the internal language of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, a subfunctor
U\hookrightarrow X
looks like the inclusion of a subset. The natural question
how one can characterize those inclusions which externally correspond to open
subfunctors is answered as follows.

Definition 19.8
.

In the context of a specified local ring
R
, as for instance
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}

of the big Zariski topos of a scheme, a truth value
\varphi
is

open
if and only if there exists an ideal
J\subseteq R
such
that
R/J
is synthetically quasicoherent
(Definition
18.18
) and such that
\varphi
holds if and only if
1\in J
. (Section
6.1
contains generalities on truth
values.)

Example 19.9
.

Let
f\,{:}\,R
. Then “
f
is invertible” is an open truth value with
witnessing ideal
J=(f)
. The quotient
R/J
is indeed synthetically
quasicoherent, since it is finitely presented. More generally,
let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\,{:}\,R
. Then “one of the
f_{i}
is invertible”
is an open truth value with witnessing ideal
J=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
.

In case that
R
fulfills the same field condition as
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
does,
one can write this truth value also as “
f_{1}\neq 0\vee\cdots\vee f_{n}\neq 0
”.

Definition 19.10
.

In the context of a specified local ring, a map
U\to X
of sets is a

synthetic open immersion
if and only if it is injective and for any
x\,{:}\,X

the truth value of “the fiber of
x
is inhabited” is open.

Example 19.11
.

The inclusion
R^{\times}\hookrightarrow R
of the
invertible elements is a synthetic open immersion, since for
x\,{:}\,R
the
truth value of “the fiber of
x
is inhabited” equals the truth value of “
x

is invertible”.

Example 19.12
.

Let
X
be a set. Let
f:X\to R
be a function.
The inclusion
\{x\,{:}\,X\,|\,\text{$f(x)$ is invertible}\}\hookrightarrow X
is a
synthetic open immersion.

Proposition 19.13
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be a Zariski sheaf. If a subfunctor
U\hookrightarrow X
is open, then the map
U\hookrightarrow X

is a synthetic open immersion from the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
,
that is

\qquad\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\forall x\,{:}\,X\mathpunct{.}\,\exists J\subseteq{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\mathpunct{.}\,\\
\ulcorner\text{{$J$ is an ideal}}\urcorner\wedge\ulcorner\text{{${\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J$ is synth.{} quasicoherent}}\urcorner\wedge(x\in U\Leftrightarrow 1\in J).

The converse holds if one of the parsimonious sites is used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
or if the ideals
J
are required to be finitely generated
from the internal point of view.

We postpone the proof of this proposition in order to give a bit of context
first.

Firstly, the displayed condition is only meaningful in an intuitionistic context as
provided by the big Zariski topos. In classical logic, the condition would be
trivially satisfied for any subfunctor
U\hookrightarrow X
: Classically, we
have
(x\in U)\vee(x\not\in U)
. If
x\in U
, we can pick
J=(1)
, and
if
x\not\in U
, we can pick
J=(0)
(whereby the quotient
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J
is
isomorphic to
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
, thus finitely presented and therefore in particular
synthetically quasicoherent).
43
43

43

Strictly speaking, incompatibility with classical
logic surfaces even earlier: in our synthetic quasicoherence condition. The
map
E\otimes_{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}A\to[\Spec(A),E]
which the condition demands to be
bijective has hardly any chance to be surjective if the law of excluded middle
is available to define maps
\Spec(A)\to E
by case distinction.

Proposition
19.13
is often used in the
following form, which is weaker because it
only gives one direction, but which is applicable for any of our choices for
the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

Corollary 19.14
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be a Zariski sheaf. Let
U\hookrightarrow X
be
a subfunctor. If

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\forall x\,{:}\,X\mathpunct{.}\,\bigvee_{n\geq 0}\exists f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\mathpunct{.}\,(x\in U\Leftrightarrow\bigvee_{i}\ulcorner\text{{$f_{i}$ inv.{}}}\urcorner),

then the subfunctor is open.

We show that the assumption implies the displayed condition of
Proposition
19.13
in the internal
language. Given elements
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
as in the assumption, we
construct the ideal
J\vcentcolon=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})\subseteq{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
. The
quotient
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J
is finitely presented, hence synthetically quasicoherent, and
the statement that
1\in J
is equivalent to one of
the
f_{i}
being invertible by locality of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
.
∎

The internal condition appearing in
Corollary
19.14
reflects basic
intuition about openness in algebraic geometry: Intuitively, a subset is open
if it is given by inequations, so that to decide whether a point belongs to the
subset one has to check that at least one of some numbers is not zero.

Of course, in classical scheme theory, one would put some condition on these
numbers in order not to trivialize the notion. For instance, one would require
that they depend continuously on the point in some sense or, more
specifically, that these numbers are given by evaluating certain locally
defined regular functions at the point.

On first sight, such a condition seems to be lacking in
Corollary
19.14
. However, it’s
implicitly built into the language, since by the Kripke–Joyal semantics the
external meaning of “
\exists f\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
” is that there exist, locally on an
open cover, suitable elements of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}(T)
, that is regular functions on
T
.

It’s useful to give a name for the kind of the subfunctors appearing in
Corollary
19.14
.

Definition 19.15
.

•

A subfunctor
U\hookrightarrow X
in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is a
quasicompact open subfunctor
if
and only if for any object
(T\to S)
of the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)

and any
x\in X(T)
there exists an open subscheme
T_{0}\subseteq T

such that the open immersion
T_{0}\hookrightarrow T
is quasicompact and such
that for any object
(T^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}T\to S)
of the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)

the map
T^{\prime}\to T
factors over
T_{0}
if and only if
X(f)(x)\in U(T^{\prime})
.

•

In the context of a specified local ring, a truth value
\varphi
is

quasicompact open
if and only if there exists a finitely generated
ideal
J\subseteq R
such that
\varphi
holds if and only if
1\in J
.

•

In the context of a specified local ring, a map
U\to X
of sets is a

synthetic quasicompact open immersion
if and only if it is injective and for any
x\,{:}\,X

the truth value of “the fiber of
x
is inhabited” is quasicompact open.

We begin with the “only if” direction. Let
T
be an
S
-scheme contained in
the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
and let
x\in X(T)
. By assumption there is
an open subscheme
T_{0}\subseteq T
such that, for any object
(T^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}T\to S)
of the site the morphism
f
factors over
T_{0}
if and only if
X(f)(x)\in U(T^{\prime})
.

There is a quasicoherent sheaf
\mathcal{I}
of ideals on
T
such that
T_{0}=D(\mathcal{I})
.
We set
J\vcentcolon=\im(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{Zar}}\to{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{T}})
. Then the quotient module
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{T}}/J

coincides with
(\mathcal{O}_{T}/\mathcal{I})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
and is therefore quasicoherent, hence
synthetically quasicoherent from the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(T)
by
Theorem
18.19
.

To verify that
T\models(x\in U\Leftrightarrow 1\in J)
, let an
object
(T^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}T\to S)
of the site be given. Then we have the
chain of equivalences

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{\Longleftrightarrow}}X(f)(x)\in U(T^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\text{$T^{\prime}\to T$ factors over\penalty\ $T_{0}$}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow T^{\prime}\models 1\in\im(f^{*}\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{O}_{T^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow T^{\prime}\models 1\in J,

where the last step follows by Lemma
18.22
.

For the converse direction, let
T
be an
S
-scheme contained in the site and
let
x\in X(T)
. By assumption there is an open covering
T=\bigcup_{i}V_{i}

and ideals
J_{i}\in\mathrm{Zar}(V_{i})
such that
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{V_{i}}}/J_{i}
is synthetically
quasicoherent and such that
V_{i}\models(x\in U\Leftrightarrow 1\in J_{i})
.

By Theorem
18.19
, there are quasicoherent modules
\mathcal{E}_{i}

over
V_{i}
such that
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{V_{i}}}/J_{i}\cong(\mathcal{E}_{i})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
. We set
\mathcal{I}_{i}\vcentcolon=\Kernel(\mathcal{O}_{V_{i}}\to\mathcal{E}_{i})
. One can check that
D(\mathcal{I}_{i})\cap V_{j}=D(\mathcal{I}_{j})\cap V_{i}
for all
i
and
j
. We set
T_{0}\vcentcolon=\bigcup_{i}D(\mathcal{I}_{i})
.

To verify that this construction satisfies what is expected of it, let
(T^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}T\to S)
be an object of the site. We then have the following chain of
equivalences:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{\Longleftrightarrow}}\text{$T^{\prime}\to T$ factors over\penalty\ $T_{0}$}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\text{for all\penalty\ $i$, $f_{i}:f^{-1}V_{i}\to V_{i}$ factors over\penalty\ $D(\mathcal{I}_{i})$}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\text{for all\penalty\ $i$, $f^{-1}V_{i}\models 1\in\im(f_{i}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{i}\to\mathcal{O}_{f^{-1}V_{i}})$}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\text{for all\penalty\ $i$, $f^{-1}V_{i}\models 1\in J_{i}$}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\text{for all\penalty\ $i$, $f^{-1}V_{i}\models x\in U$}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\text{$T^{\prime}\models x\in U$}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\text{$X(f)(x)\in U(T^{\prime})$}

For this calculation, it’s not important that the ideals
\mathcal{I}_{i}
are
quasicoherent. The assumption that the quotient modules
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{V_{i}}}/J_{i}
are
quasicoherent is only needed to ensure that Lemma
18.22

can be applied in the marked step.
∎

Proposition 19.16
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be a Zariski sheaf. A subfunctor
U\hookrightarrow X
is a
quasicompact open subfunctor if and only if
U\hookrightarrow X
is a
synthetic quasicompact open immersion from the internal point of view
of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

The proof of
Proposition
19.13
can be adapted.
∎

Lemma 19.17
.

Finite conjunctions and finite disjunctions of quasicompact open
truth values are quasicompact open. If
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is defined using one of the
parsimonious sites, then furthermore finite conjunctions and finite
disjunctions of open truth values are open from the internal point of view
of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

Let
\varphi
and
\psi
be quasicompact open truth values with
witnessing finitely generated ideals
I
and
J
. Because the ring is
local,
\varphi\wedge\psi
is equivalent to
1\in I\cdot J
, and
\varphi\vee\psi
is equivalent to
1\in I+J
. The ideals
I\cdot J
and
I+J
are
finitely generated. Therefore
\varphi\wedge\psi
and
\varphi\vee\psi
are
quasicompact open truth values.

For the case of arbitrary open truth values, we need to verify that
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/(I\cdot J)
and
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/(I+J)
are synthetically quasicoherent if
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/I

and
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J
are. This second claim from Lemma
18.25
,
since
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/(I+J)\cong{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/I\otimes_{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J
. The first claim
follows from a similar calculation.
∎

The notion of open truth values is not unique to our account of synthetic
algebraic geometry. Rather, it’s a concept in the established and more general
framework of synthetic topology
[
49
,
88
]
which aims to do topology in a
synthetic fashion: Any set should have an intrinsic topology and any map should
be automatically continuous with respect to this intrinsic topology.

This automatic continuity reflects as stability of open subfunctors under
pullbacks:

Lemma 19.18
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\penalty\ \mathrm{Zar}(S)
. Let
U\hookrightarrow Y
be an open subfunctor. Then its pullback along
f
,
denoted “
f^{-1}U\hookrightarrow X
”, is too an open subfunctor.

From the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, the
subfunctor
f^{-1}U\hookrightarrow X
looks like the inclusion of the
preimage
f^{-1}[U]\subseteq X
.

So, to verify the claim, let internally an element
x\,{:}\,X
be given. We are to
show that the truth value of “
x\in f^{-1}[U]
” is open. This truth value
equals the truth value of “
f(x)\in U
” which is open by assumption, and is
therefore open.
∎

Remark 19.19
.

In the internal language of toposes used to carry out synthetic
differential geometry, there is the concept of an
Penon-open

subset
[
104
, Chapitre III]
: A subset
U\subseteq X
is Penon-open if
and only if

\forall x\in U\mathpunct{.}\,\forall y\,{:}\,X\mathpunct{.}\,(x\neq y)\vee(y\in U).

This notion is not useful in synthetic algebraic geometry, since it is much too
weak: Any subset of the one-element set
1
is Penon-open. However, not every
subfunctor of the terminal functor in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is an open subfunctor.

In many flavors of synthetic topology, open truth values
\varphi

are
\neg\neg
-stable in that
\neg\neg\varphi
implies
\varphi
. With a
small caveat, this is true for open truth values in the big Zariski topos as
well.

Proposition 19.20
.

Let
U\hookrightarrow 1
be a subfunctor in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
such
that
\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\neg\neg U
. Then in any of the following
situations it follows that
\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models U
:

(1)

U
is a quasicompact open truth value.

(2)

U
is an arbitrary open truth value and the site used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is closed under domains of closed immersions. (This is for
instance satisfied for the sites built using a Grothendieck or a partial
universe. It is satisfied for the parsimonious sites if
S
is locally
Noetherian.)

We give two proofs, an internal one and an external one, since
they employ different ideas.

Internal proof.
Since
U
is an open truth value, there exists an ideal
J\subseteq{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
such that
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J
is synthetically quasicoherent and such that
U
holds
if and only if
1\in J
. By assumption, the element 1 is
not not
an element
of
J
; we want to verify that it’s actually an element of
J
.

By Scholium
18.20
, the canonical homomorphism

{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J\longrightarrow[\Spec({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J),{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]

is bijective; the assumptions of that scholium are satisfied in either of
the two situations. The set
\Spec({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J)
is isomorphic to
{\llbracket{J=(0)}\rrbracket}
.
Since
\neg\neg(1\in J)
, we also have
\neg(J=(0))
.
Therefore
\Spec({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J)
is empty and the codomain of the displayed
isomorphism is the zero algebra. Thus
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J
is trivial as well, showing
1\in J
.

External proof.
Since
U\hookrightarrow 1
is an open subfunctor, there
is an open subscheme
S_{0}\subseteq S
such that a morphism
f:T\to S

factors over
S_{0}
if and only if
U(T)
is inhabited. In both situations it’s
possible to endow
X\vcentcolon=S\subseteq S_{0}
with the structure of a closed
subscheme such that
X
is contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.
By the universal property of
S_{0}
, we have
X\models\neg U
. Since
\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\neg\neg U
, it follows that
X
is empty. Therefore
S_{0}=S
and
U

is globally inhabited.
∎

Corollary 19.21
.

Let
\gamma:\Delta\to X
be a morphism in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.
Let
U\hookrightarrow X
be an open subfunctor such that
\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\gamma(0)\in U
. Then, in any of the situations in
Proposition
19.20
, the morphism
\gamma
factors
over
U
.

We give an internal proof. Let
\varepsilon\in\Delta
.
Then
\neg\neg(\varepsilon=0)
. Therefore
\neg\neg(\gamma(\varepsilon)\in U)
. Since being an element of
U
is
\neg\neg
-stable, it follows
that
\gamma(\varepsilon)\in U
.
∎

Remark 19.22
.

Subobjects
U\hookrightarrow X
for which any morphism
\gamma:\Delta\to X
with
\gamma(0)\in U
factors over
U
are called “
D_{1}
-open”
in the literature on synthetic differential
geometry
[
110
, p. 60]
.
Corollary
19.21
shows that open subfunctors
are
D_{1}
-open.

In ordinary scheme theory, an inclusion of a standard open subset
D(f)\hookrightarrow X
is isomorphic to the structure morphism of the relative
spectrum
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{X}\mathcal{O}_{X}[f^{-1}]
. Inclusions of more general open subsets
can typically not be described using the relative spectrum construction, the
standard example being the inclusion
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}\setminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
whose domain is not affine.

An interesting feature of the internal universe of the big Zariski topos is
that it’s flexible enough to express
any
open subset as a spectrum.
The contradiction is only apparent since the algebra used for constructing
such a spectrum is not in general quasicoherent.

Proposition 19.23
.

Let
U\hookrightarrow 1
be an open truth value. In any of the
situations of Proposition
19.20
, there is a
(not necessarily quasicoherent)
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
A
such that the inclusion is
isomorphic to the morphism
\Spec(A)\to 1
.

The open truth value
U
is given by an ideal
J\subseteq{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}

such that
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J
is synthetically quasicoherent and such that
U
holds if
and only if
1\in J
. We set
A\vcentcolon={\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[M^{-1}]
, where
M
is the
multiplicatively closed subset

M\vcentcolon=\{f\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\,|\,1\in J\Rightarrow\ulcorner\text{{$f$ inv.{}}}\urcorner\}\subseteq{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}.

The spectrum of
A
is inhabited if and only if
M\subseteq({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{\times}
,
in which case the unique element of
\Spec(A)
is the inverse of the
localization morphism
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\to{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[M^{-1}]
. Thus
\Spec(A)
is isomorphic
to
{\llbracket{M\subseteq({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{\times}}\rrbracket}
. Therefore we have to verify that
U

holds if and only if
M\subseteq({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{\times}
.

The “only if” direction is trivial.

For the “if” direction, we exploit the
\neg\neg
-stability of
U
. If
\neg U
, then
\neg(1\in J)
, so
M={\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
, and since
M\subseteq({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{\times}
by
assumption, it follows that zero is invertible. This is a contradiction.
Thus
\neg\neg U
.
∎

Remark 19.24
.

The radical
\sqrt{J}
of the ideal
J
appearing in
Proposition
19.13
is unique:
It is equal to the radical ideal

K\vcentcolon=\{f\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\,|\,\ulcorner\text{{$f$ inv.{}}}\urcorner\Rightarrow(x\in U)\}\subseteq{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}.

It’s obvious that
J\subseteq K
and therefore
\sqrt{J}\subseteq K
.
For the converse direction, let
f\in K
be given. Since
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J
is
synthetically quasicoherent, the canonical map

({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J)[f^{-1}]\longrightarrow[\Spec({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[f^{-1}]),{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J]

is bijective. Since
\Spec({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[f^{-1}])\cong{\llbracket{\ulcorner\text{{$f$ inv.{}}}\urcorner}\rrbracket}
, the image
of
1
is zero: If
\Spec({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[f^{-1}])
is inhabited, the element
f
is invertible and
therefore
x
is an element of
U
. This implies that
1\in J
.
Thereby
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J=0
. By injectivity of the canonical map, the
algebra
({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J)[f^{-1}]
is zero. Therefore
f^{n}\in J
for some natural
number
n
.

Remark 19.25
.

In view of the previous remark, one might hope to be able to simplify the
condition in Proposition
19.13
as
follows: “For any
x\,{:}\,X
, the quotient
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/K
modulo the ideal
K=\{f\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\,|\,\ulcorner\text{{$f$ inv.{}}}\urcorner\Rightarrow(x\in U)\}
is synthetically quasicoherent.”
However, this doesn’t work out.
This statement implies the condition in the proposition, but the converse
direction does not hold, since
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/K\cong{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/\sqrt{J}
might fail to be
synthetically quasicoherent. For instance that’s the case if
U=\emptyset
; then
K=\sqrt{(0)}
by Proposition
18.29
. The
quotient
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/\sqrt{(0)}
is not synthetically quasicoherent by
Remark
18.23
.

Remark 19.26
.

There is the notion of an open geometric morphism of toposes. For the big
Zariski toposes, this notion is not related to open morphisms or open
immersions between schemes: If
X\to S
is any morphism of schemes, the
induced geometric morphism
\mathrm{Zar}(X)\to\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is isomorphic to the canonical
morphism
\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\text{\textul{$X$}}\to\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, as detailed in
Section
16.3
. Geometric morphisms of the form
\mathcal{E}/A\to\mathcal{E}
are always open.

19.6.
Closed immersions

Definition 19.27
.

In the context of a specified local ring
R
, as for instance
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}

of the big Zariski topos of a scheme, a truth value
\varphi
is

closed
if and only if there exists an ideal
J\subseteq R
such
that
R/J
is synthetically quasicoherent
(Definition
18.18
) and such that
\varphi
holds if and only if
J=(0)
.

In other words, a truth value
Z\subseteq 1
is closed if and only if
Z
is
isomorphic to the spectrum of a synthetically quasicoherent quotient algebra
of
R
.

Example 19.28
.

Let
f\,{:}\,R
. Then “
f=0
” is a closed truth value with
witnessing ideal
J=(f)
. More generally, if
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\,{:}\,R
, the
truth value “
f_{1}=\cdots=f_{n}=0
” is closed with witnessing
ideal
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
.

Definition 19.29
.

In the context of a specified local ring, a map
Z\to X
of sets is a

synthetic closed immersion
if and only if it is injective and for
any
x\,{:}\,X
the truth value of “the fiber of
x
is inhabited” is
closed.

Example 19.30
.

The inclusion
\{0\}\hookrightarrow R
is a synthetic closed
immersion. More generally, for any functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}:R^{n}\to R
, the inclusion of the set of their common zeros in
R^{n}
is a
synthetic closed immersion.

Example 19.31
.

Let
X
be a set. Let
f:X\to R
be a function.
The inclusion
\{x\,{:}\,X\,|\,f(x)=0\}\hookrightarrow X
is a
synthetic closed immersion.

Proposition 19.32
.

Let
X
be an
S
-scheme.

(1)

Let
Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed subscheme. Then the subfunctor
\text{\textul{$Z$}}\hookrightarrow\text{\textul{$X$}}
is a synthetic closed immersion from the internal point
of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

(2)

Assume that
X
is locally representable by an object of the site used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. Let
Z\hookrightarrow\text{\textul{$X$}}
be a synthetic closed
immersion. If the site is one of the parsimonious sites or if the witnessing
ideals for the immersion are finitely generated from the internal point of
view, then
Z\hookrightarrow\text{\textul{$X$}}
is
isomorphic to the subfunctor associated to a closed subscheme of
X
.

To verify the first claim, let a
quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra
\mathcal{J}_{0}
be given such that the closed subscheme
Z\hookrightarrow X
is the vanishing scheme of
\mathcal{J}_{0}
. Following the translation with
the Kripke–Joyal semantics, let
f:T\to S
be an object of the site used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
and let
x\in\text{\textul{$X$}}(T)
. We define
J\vcentcolon=(f^{*}\mathcal{J}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\text{\textul{$T$}}
. Then
T\models\ulcorner\text{{${\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J$ is
synthetically quasicoherent}}\urcorner
and
T\models(x\in\text{\textul{$Z$}}\Leftrightarrow J=(0))
, therefore “
x\in\text{\textul{$Z$}}
” is a closed truth value.

For the converse direction, we may assume that
X=S
since
\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\text{\textul{$X$}}\simeq\mathrm{Zar}(X)
, as discussed in Section
16.3
. We then
observe that the problem is local on
S
, since we can glue matching schemes
defined over the members of an open covering of
S

[
126
,
Tag 01JJ
]
. We
may therefore assume that we are given an
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
J
such
that
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J
is synthetically quasicoherent from the internal point of view
and such that
Z={\llbracket{J=(0)}\rrbracket}
. The assumptions ensure that
Theorem
18.19
is applicable. Therefore there is a
quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{E}
such that
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J\cong\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
. We set
\mathcal{J}\vcentcolon=\Kernel(\mathcal{O}_{S}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{E})
. Lemma
18.22
then
implies that
Z
is the functor of points of the
S
-scheme
V(\mathcal{J})
.
∎

Proposition 19.33
.

Let
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be defined using a parsimonious site and assume
that
S
is locally Noetherian. Then the witnessing ideals of closed truth
values are uniquely determined by the truth value.

We argue internally. Let
\varphi
be a closed truth value.
Let
J\subseteq{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
be an ideal such that
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J
is synthetically
quasicoherent and such that
\varphi
holds if and only if
J=(0)
.

We set
K\vcentcolon=\{f\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\,|\,\varphi\Rightarrow f=0\}
. Then,
trivially,
J\subseteq K
. For the converse containment relation, let
f\in K
. The canonical homomorphism
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J\to[{\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]
is
bijective by synthetic quasicoherence of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J
and by
Scholium
18.20
(it is here that we need that
S
is locally Noetherian
– else we can’t ensure that the
S
-scheme
\operatorname{\text{\textul{$\mathrm{Spec}$}}}_{S}(({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/J)|_{\mathrm{Sh}(S)})

is locally finitely presented). The image of
[f]
is zero, hence
f
is an
element of
J
.
∎

Remark 19.34
.

Without any assumptions on
S
or on the site used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, Proposition
19.33
holds for
those closed truth values which admit a finitely generated witnessing ideal.
For those closed truth values, any given finitely generated witnessing ideals
are equal.

Lemma 19.35
.

If
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is defined using one of the parsimonious sites, then
finite conjunctions of closed truth values are closed from the internal point
of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

We argue internally. Let
\varphi
and
\psi
be closed truth
values with witnessing ideals
I
and
J
. Then
\varphi\wedge\psi
is
equivalent to
I+J=(0)
and the module
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}/(I+J)
is synthetically
quasicoherent (as in the proof of Lemma
19.17
).
Hence
\varphi\wedge\psi
is a closed truth value.
∎

It’s in general not the case that finite disjunctions of closed truth
values are closed, and it’s instructive to see why. The external interpretation
of this failure is the following: Let
A\hookrightarrow S
and
B\hookrightarrow S
be closed subschemes. Then the functor of points of the
union
A\cup B
does
not
coincide with the union of the
subfunctors
\text{\textul{$A$}}\hookrightarrow\text{\textul{$S$}}
and
\text{\textul{$B$}}\hookrightarrow\text{\textul{$S$}}
. An explicit description of the former functor is

(X\xrightarrow{f}S)\longmapsto\{\star\,|\,\text{$f$ factors over\penalty\ $A\cup B$}\}

and of the latter is

(X\xrightarrow{f}S)\longmapsto\{\star\,|\,\text{locally on the target, $f$ factors over\penalty\ $A$ or over\penalty\ $B$}\}.

The inclusion
A\cup B\hookrightarrow S
trivially factors over
A\cup B
,
but in general there isn’t an open covering
S=\bigcup_{i}U_{i}
such that for
each
i
, the restriction
(A\cup B)\cap U_{i}\hookrightarrow U_{i}
factors
over
A\cap U_{i}
or over
B\cap U_{i}
. For instance, this isn’t the case
if
S
is the affine plane over a field and
A
and
B
are the two axes.

This phenomenon doesn’t happen for open subschemes, which explains why finite
disjunctions of open truth values are open.

Remark 19.36
.

There is the notion of a closed geometric morphism of toposes. For
an arbitrary topos
\mathcal{E}
and an object
A\in\mathcal{E}
, the canonical geometric
morphism
\mathcal{E}/A\to E
is closed if and only if

\forall U\subseteq A\mathpunct{.}\,\forall\varphi\,{:}\,\Omega\mathpunct{.}\,\hskip 10.00002ptA\subseteq(U\cup\{x\in A\,|\,\varphi\})\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt(A\subseteq U)\vee\varphi

from the internal point of view of
\mathcal{E}
. If
X\to S
is a closed morphism of
schemes, then the induced geometric morphism
\mathrm{Sh}(X)\to\mathrm{Sh}(S)
between the
little Zariski toposes is closed in this sense.

However, the induced geometric morphism
\mathrm{Zar}(X)\to\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is typically not
closed. For instance, if
X\to S
is the embedding of a closed subset
V(f)

with
f\in\Gamma(S,\mathcal{O}_{S})
, then the morphism
\mathrm{Zar}(X)\to\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is
isomorphic to
\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\text{\textul{$V(f)$}}\to\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, as discussed in
Section
16.3
. In the special case
U\vcentcolon=\emptyset
and

\varphi\vcentcolon={\llbracket{f=0}\rrbracket}
, the displayed closedness condition simplifies
to
\ulcorner\text{{$f$ inv.{}}}\urcorner\vee(f=0)
. This is typically not true in the internal
language of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. A specific counterexample is given in
Example
6.39
.

19.7.
Surjective morphisms

Proposition 19.37
.

Let
f:X\to S
be an arbitrary
S
-scheme. Consider the following
statements:

(1)

The morphism
f
is surjective.

(2)

From the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, it’s not the case that
X
is
empty, that is

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\neg\neg(\ulcorner\text{{$\text{\textul{$X$}}$ is inhabited}}\urcorner).

If
X
is locally contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
(for instance,
if
X
is contained in the universe used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
or if one of the
parsimonious sites is used and
X
is locally of finite presentation over
S
),
then (1) implies (2). The converse holds if the site is closed under
taking spectra of residue fields or if one of the parsimonious sites is used
and
f
is quasicompact and quasiseparated.

The translation of the internal statement using the Kripke–Joyal
semantics is:
For any
S
-scheme
T
of the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
,
if
\text{\textul{$X\times_{S}T$}}=\text{\textul{$\emptyset$}}
(as functors of points
of
T
-schemes), then
T=\emptyset
.

In the case that the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
is closed under taking
spectra of residue fields, this implies that
f
is surjective as follows.
Let
s\in S
be an arbitrary point. The
S
-scheme
T\vcentcolon=\Spec(k(s))
is
not empty. Therefore the fiber
X_{s}=X\times_{S}T
of
f
over
s
is not empty.

If one of the parsimonious sites is used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, we can’t apply
the assumption to the
S
-scheme
T=\Spec(k(s))
since it might not be
locally of finite presentation over
S
. We therefore argue as follows. Without
loss of generality, we may assume that
S
is affine. Writing
k(s)
as the
canonical filtered colimit of all finitely presented
\Gamma(S,\mathcal{O}_{S})
-algebras
mapping to
k(s)
(and then rewriting this filtered colimit as a directed
colimit
[
3
, Theorem 1.5]
), we see
that
\Spec(k(s))
is the directed limit of an inverse system of finitely
presented affine
S
-schemes
T_{i}
with affine transition maps.
In particular, the structure morphisms
T_{i}\to S
are quasicompact and
quasiseparated. By the assumption that the morphism
X\to S
is quasicompact and
quasiseparated as well, the schemes
X\times_{S}T_{i}
are quasicompact and
quasiseparated (as absolute schemes). Therefore, if
X_{s}=X\times_{S}T=\lim_{i}(X\times_{S}T_{i})
is empty, then so is
X\times_{S}T_{i}
for
some
i

[
126
,
Tag 01ZC
]
. Thus
T_{i}=\emptyset
and
hence
\Spec(k(s))=\emptyset
; this is a contradiction.

For the converse direction, let an
S
-scheme
T
contained in the site used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be given such that
\text{\textul{$X\times_{S}T$}}=\text{\textul{$\emptyset$}}
as
functors of points of
T
-schemes. The assumption that
X
is locally contained
in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
implies that
X\times_{S}T=\emptyset
as
schemes. Since the base change
X\times_{S}T\to T
of
f
is surjective, this
implies that
T
is empty.
∎

Corollary 19.38
.

Let
p:X\to Y
be a morphism of
S
-schemes. Assume that
Y
is locally
contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.
Further assume that the site used to define the big Zariski toposes are closed
under taking spectra of residue fields or that the parsimonious sites are used
and that
p
is quasicompact and quasiseparated.
Consider the following statements:

(1)

The morphism
p
is surjective.

(2)

From the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
all fibers of
p
are
nonempty, that is

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\forall y\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$Y$}}\mathpunct{.}\,\neg\neg\exists x\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$X$}}\mathpunct{.}\,\text{\textul{$p$}}(x)=y.

If
X
is locally contained in the
site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(Y)
, then (1) implies (2). The converse holds if the
sites used to define the big Zariski toposes are closed under taking spectra of
residue fields or that the parsimonious sites are used and that
p
is
quasicompact and quasiseparated.

Immediate using Proposition
19.37

and the equivalence
\mathrm{Zar}(Y)\simeq\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\text{\textul{$Y$}}
,
as explained in Section
16.3
.
∎

Remark 19.39
.

Combining Proposition
17.1
and
Proposition
19.37
yields a proof of the fact
that a quasicompact, quasiseparated, and locally finitely presented morphism
X\to S
, where
S
is locally of
finite type over a field, is surjective if it is surjective on closed points.

Remark 19.40
.

In the case that the parsimonious sites are used, the assumption in
Proposition
19.37
that the morphism
f
is
quasicompact can’t be dropped. For instance, let
k
be an
algebraically closed field. Then the canonical morphism

X\vcentcolon=\coprod_{a\in k}\Spec(k[X]/(X-a))\longrightarrow\Spec(k[X])=\vcentcolon S

is surjective on closed points. By Proposition
17.1
,
it’s not the case that
X
is empty from the internal point of view
of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. However, the morphism is not surjective.

19.8.
Universally injective morphisms

Proposition 19.41
.

Let
f:X\to S
be an
S
-scheme which is locally contained in the site
used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. In the case that the parsimonious sites are used to
define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, further assume that
f
is quasicompact and quasiseparated.
Then the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The morphism
f
is universally injective.

(2)

The diagonal morphism
X\to X\times_{S}X
is surjective.

(3)

From the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, any given elements of
X

are
not not
equal, that is

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\forall x\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$X$}}\mathpunct{.}\,\forall x^{\prime}\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$X$}}\mathpunct{.}\,\neg\neg(x=x^{\prime}).

The equivalence “(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2)” is
well-known
[
126
,
Tag 01S4
]
. The equivalence “(2)
\Leftrightarrow
(3)”
is by Corollary
19.38
and the fact that,
internally, there is
not not
a preimage for any element of
\text{\textul{$X$}}\times\text{\textul{$X$}}
under the diagonal map
\text{\textul{$X$}}\to\text{\textul{$X$}}\times\text{\textul{$X$}}
if and only if
any given elements of
X
are
not not
equal.
∎

Corollary 19.42
.

Let
p:X\to Y
be a morphisms of
S
-schemes which are locally
contained in the site used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. In the case that the
parsimonious sites are used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, further assume that
f
is
quasicompact and quasiseparated. Then the following statements
are equivalent:

(1)

The morphism
p
is universally injective.

(2)

From the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, any given elements of any
fiber of
p
are
not not
equal.

Immediate using Proposition
19.41

and the equivalence
\mathrm{Zar}(Y)\simeq\mathrm{Zar}(S)/\text{\textul{$Y$}}
,
as explained in Section
16.3
.
∎

19.9.
Universally closed morphisms

Definition 19.43
.

In the context of a specified local ring, a set
X
is

synthetically closed
if and only if, for any synthetic closed
immersion
Z\hookrightarrow X
, there is a closed truth value
\varphi
such
that
Z
is
not not
inhabited if and only if
\neg\neg\varphi
.

Example 19.44
.

Any singleton set is synthetically closed.

Example 19.45
.

The specified local ring
R
is typically not synthetically closed.
For let
f\,{:}\,R
be an element. Then the inclusion
Z\vcentcolon=\{g\,{:}\,R\,|\,fg-1=0\}\hookrightarrow R
is a synthetic closed immersion. The set
Z
is
not not
inhabited if and
only if
f
is
not not
invertible if and only if
f
is invertible (by
Proposition
18.27
); typically, there is no closed truth
value
\varphi
such that
\neg\neg\varphi
is equivalent to the open truth
value “
f
is invertible”.

Proposition 19.46
.

Assume that
S
is locally Noetherian. Let
f:X\to S
be a
finitely presented morphism. In the situation that one the parsimonious
sites is used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The morphism
f
has closed image.

(2)

The morphism
f
has universally closed image, that is for
any
S
-scheme
T
the image of the induced morphism
X\times_{S}T\to T
is
closed.

(3)

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\exists\varphi\,{:}\,\Omega\mathpunct{.}\,\ulcorner\text{{$\varphi$ is a closed truth value}}\urcorner\wedge(\neg\neg(\ulcorner\text{{$\text{\textul{$X$}}$ inhabited}}\urcorner)\Leftrightarrow\neg\neg\varphi)
.

The direction “(2)
\Rightarrow
(1)” is trivial, and the
direction “(1)
\Rightarrow
(2)” is immediate, since the image of
X\times_{S}T\to T
is the preimage of the image of
f
under the structure
morphism
T\to S
.

For the “(1)
\Rightarrow
(3)” direction, we may pick the subfunctor
of
S
induced by the closed immersion
\im(f)\hookrightarrow S
as the
sought truth value. This truth value is closed by
Proposition
19.32
and its double negation is
equivalent to
\ulcorner\text{{$\text{\textul{$X$}}$ is inhabited}}\urcorner
by
Lemma
17.3
.

For the converse direction, we see that, after passing to an open covering
of
S
which we won’t reflect in the notation, there is a closed subfunctor
Z\hookrightarrow 1
such that
\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\neg\neg(\ulcorner\text{{$\text{\textul{$X$}}$
inhabited}}\urcorner)\Leftrightarrow\neg\neg(\ulcorner\text{{$Z$ is inhabited}}\urcorner)
. By
Proposition
19.32
, this subfunctor is the functor of
points of a closed subscheme of
S
. Since
S
is locally Noetherian, this
subscheme is locally of finite presentation over
S
. Therefore
Lemma
17.3
is applicable and yields that the image of
X\to S
coincides with the found closed subscheme. This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 19.47
.

Assume that
S
is locally Noetherian. Let
f:X\to S
be a
finitely presented morphism. In the situation that one the parsimonious
sites is used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The morphism
f
is universally closed.

(2)

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\ulcorner\text{{$\text{\textul{$X$}}$ is synthetically closed}}\urcorner
.

Immediate using Proposition
19.32
and
Proposition
19.46
.
∎

In view of Corollary
19.47
,
Example
19.44
and Example
19.45

have a geometric interpretation. The first example reflects the fact that the
identity morphism
S\to S
is universally closed. The second example reflects
the fact that the projection morphism
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\to S
is typically not
universally closed.

19.10.
Quasicompact and quasiseparated morphisms

Definition 19.48
.

In the context of a specified local ring
R
,
a
synthetic affine scheme
is a set which is isomorphic (as a set)
to the synthetic spectrum of a synthetically
quasicoherent
R
-algebra.

Definition 19.49
.

In the context of a specified local ring
R
:

(1)

A
quasicompact synthetic scheme
is a set
X
which admits a finite
open covering
X=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
by synthetic affine schemes
U_{i}
.

(2)

A
locally finitely presented quasicompact synthetic scheme
is a
set
X
which admits a finite open covering
X=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
such that
the sets
U_{i}
are isomorphic to spectra of finitely presented
R
-algebras.

(3)

A
finitely presented synthetic scheme
is a set
X
which admits a
finite open covering
X=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
such that the sets
U_{i}
are
isomorphic to spectra of finitely presented
R
-algebras and such that the
intersections
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
can be covered by finitely many open subsets which
are isomorphic to spectra of finitely presented
R
-algebras.

Proposition 19.50
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be a Zariski sheaf. Consider the following
statements:

(1)

X
is the functor of points of a quasicompact
S
-scheme.

(2)

X
is the functor of points of a locally finitely presented quasicompact
S
-scheme.

(3)

X
is the functor of points of a finitely presented
S
-scheme (locally
finitely presented, quasicompact, and quasiseparated over
S
).

(1’)

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\ulcorner\text{{$X$ is a quasicompact synthetic scheme}}\urcorner
.

(2’)

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\ulcorner\text{{$X$ is a locally finitely presented quasicompact synthetic scheme}}\urcorner
.

(3’)

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\ulcorner\text{{$X$ is a finitely presented synthetic scheme}}\urcorner
.

Then:

•

(1)
\Rightarrow
(1’), (2)
\Rightarrow
(2’), and (3)
\Rightarrow
(3’).

•

If the parsimonious sites are used to define
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, then all three
converses hold.

•

If the given synthetic open immersions are even quasicompact open
immersions, then (2’)
\Rightarrow
(2) and (3’)
\Rightarrow
(3).

For proving (1)
\Rightarrow
(1’), (2)
\Rightarrow
(2’),
and (3)
\Rightarrow
(3’), we may assume that
S
is affine, since the
internal language is local. Let
X_{0}
be an
S
-scheme representing the functor
X
. Since
the structure morphism
X_{0}\to S
is quasicompact and
S
is quasicompact,
there exist finitely many open affine subschemes
U_{i}\subseteq X_{0}
which
cover
X_{0}
. By Proposition
19.7
, the
subfunctors
\text{\textul{$U_{i}$}}\hookrightarrow X
are synthetic open immersions from the
internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
. The internal union
\bigcup_{i}\text{\textul{$U_{i}$}}\hookrightarrow X
is the functor

T/S\longmapsto\{f:T\to X_{0}\,|\,\text{locally, the morphism $f$ factors over one of the opens\penalty\ $U_{i}$}\}

and therefore coincides with
X
.

Since each scheme
U_{i}
can be realized as a relative spectrum of a
quasicoherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra, both
U_{i}
and
S
being affine, the sets
U_{i}

are synthetic affine schemes from the internal point of view.
Hence (1)
\Rightarrow
(1’). If (2) holds, then the
U_{i}
are spectra of
finitely presented
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebras, so (2’) holds. If (3) holds, then the
intersections
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
can be covered by finitely many open subschemes
which are spectra of finitely presented
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebras, so (3’) holds.

For the converse directions, we first note that the problem is local on
S
,
since we can glue matching schemes defined over the members of an open covering
of
S

[
126
,
Tag 01JJ
]
. We may therefore assume that we are given
subfunctors
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}\hookrightarrow X
such that, from the internal
point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, the subsets
U_{i}\subseteq X
are synthetic affine
schemes and the inclusions
U_{i}\hookrightarrow X
are synthetic open
immersions. The extra assumptions ensure that
Proposition
19.13
and
Proposition
18.9
are applicable. These imply that the
functors
U_{i}
are representable by affine
S
-schemes. These can be glued to
yield an
S
-scheme which represents the functor
X

[
126
,
Tag 01JJ
]
.

In case that internally further finiteness conditions are satisfied, the
resulting scheme
X
will satisfy the corresponding external finiteness
conditions.
∎

One can reasonably wonder why we didn’t include the following notion
in Definition
19.49
: A
synthetic scheme
is a
set
X
which admits an arbitrary open covering by synthetic affine schemes.
The reason is that, with this definition, any subset
X
of the singleton
set
1=\{\star\}
is a synthetic scheme, since it admits the open affine
covering
X=\bigcup\{1\,|\,\star\in X\}
. But not any subfunctor of the
terminal functor is representable by a scheme.

This phenomenon is well-known in synthetic topology; one has to put some
restrictions on the kind of allowed open coverings. Being finite is the
simplest such condition.

Proposition 19.51
.

Let
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be defined using the parsimonious sites. Let
f:X\to S
be a locally finitely presented morphism. Then:

(1)

The morphism
f
is quasicompact if and only if
X
is a
quasicompact synthetic scheme from the internal point of view of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

(2)

The morphism
f
is quasiseparated if and only if, internally, for any
elements
x,y\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$X$}}
the set
{\llbracket{x=y}\rrbracket}
is a quasicompact synthetic
scheme.

(3)

The morphism
f
is separated if and only if, internally, for any
elements
x,y\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$X$}}
the truth value
{\llbracket{x=y}\rrbracket}
is closed. If this is
the case, the witnessing ideal can be chosen to be finitely generated.

The first statement is by Proposition
19.50

and by the fact that the scheme representing a representable functor is unique.

The second statement follows from the first by applying it to the diagonal
morphism
\Delta:X\to X\times_{S}X
. More precisely, we have the following
chain of equivalences:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{\Longleftrightarrow}}\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\forall x,y\,{:}\,\text{\textul{$X$}}\mathpunct{.}\,\ulcorner\text{{${\llbracket{x=y}\rrbracket}$ is a quasicompact synthetic scheme}}\urcorner

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\mathrm{Zar}(X\times_{S}X)\models\ulcorner\text{{$\text{\textul{$X$}}$ is a quasicompact synthetic scheme}}\urcorner

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\text{$\Delta:X\to X\times_{S}X$ is quasicompact}

The first step is by the discussion in Section
16.3
and
the second by applying the first statement; the diagonal morphism is locally of
finite presentation
[
126
,
Tag 0818
]
, as required.

The third statement follows in a similar way, but employing
Proposition
19.32
instead of the first statement.
∎

Lemma 19.52
.

Internally in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, a set
X
is separated if and only if all of
its points are closed. More formally, the following two statements are
equivalent:

(1)

For all
x\,{:}\,X
, the inclusion
\{x\}\hookrightarrow X
is a synthetic
closed immersion.

(2)

The diagonal morphism
X\hookrightarrow X\times X
is a synthetic
closed immersion.

We argue internally. Let
x\,{:}\,X
be fixed. The inclusion
\{x\}\hookrightarrow X
is a synthetic closed immersion if and only if, for
all
y\,{:}\,X
, the truth value “the fiber over
y
is inhabited” is closed. This
truth value is equal to the truth value of “
x=y
”.

With these observations, the equivalence of the two statements is immediate.
∎

19.11.
Proper morphisms

Proposition 19.53
.

Let
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be defined using the parsimonious sites. Assume
that
S
is locally Noetherian. Let
X\in\mathrm{Zar}(S)
be a Zariski sheaf. Then
the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The sheaf
X
is the functor of points of a proper
S
-scheme.

(2)

\mathrm{Zar}(S)\models\ulcorner\text{{$X$ is a finitely presented synthetic scheme and
synthetically closed}}\urcorner
.

Follows immediately from Proposition
19.50
,
Proposition
19.51
, and Corollary
19.47
.
∎

20.
Case studies

20.1.
Punctured plane

Definition 20.1
.

The
synthetic punctured plane
is the set
P\vcentcolon=({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{2}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proposition 20.2
.

The synthetic punctured plane, as constructed by the internal
language of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
, is the functor of points of the ordinary punctured plane
over
S
, that is the open subscheme
D(X)\cup D(Y)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}
.

The set
P
can be written as
\{(x,y)\,{:}\,({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{2}\,|\,\text{$x$ inv.{}}\vee\text{$y$ inv.{}}\}
.
∎

Proposition 20.3
.

The evaluation morphism
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[X,Y]\to[P,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]
is bijective.

The synthetic punctured plane can be expressed as the pushout

P\cong D(X)\amalg_{D(X)\cap D(Y)}D(Y).

Therefore we have the chain of isomorphisms

\displaystyle[P,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]

\displaystyle\cong[D(X)\amalg_{D(X)\cap D(Y)}D(Y),{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]

\displaystyle\cong[D(X),{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]\times_{[D(X)\cap D(Y),{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]}[D(Y),{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]

\displaystyle\cong{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[X,X^{-1}]\times_{{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[XY,(XY)^{-1}]}{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[Y,Y^{-1}]

\displaystyle\cong{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[X,Y].

The penultimate isomorphism exploits the synthetic quasicoherence of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
,
which ensures that the canonical map

{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[X,X^{-1}]\longrightarrow[\Spec({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[X,X^{-1}]),{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]\cong[D(X),{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]

is bijective. The ultimate isomorphism rests on the purely algebraic argument
that elements of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[X,X^{-1}]
and
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[Y,Y^{-1}]
which agree as
elements of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[(XY),(XY)^{-1}]
are both given by an element
of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[X,Y]
and in fact by the same element.
∎

Corollary 20.4
.

The punctured plane is not affine.

The canonical map
P\to\Spec([P,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}])
is isomorphic to the
strict inclusion
P\hookrightarrow({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{2}
and therefore not bijective.
∎

20.2.
Cohomology of projective space

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a finite locally free
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module. In this section, we give
internal descriptions of the projectivization of
\mathcal{V}
and of Serre’s twisting
bundles on
\mathbb{P}(V)
, and show how their cohomology groups can be computed
internally. Let
V\vcentcolon=\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
.

Definition 20.5
.

The
synthetic projective space
is the set

\mathbb{P}(V)\vcentcolon=\{\ell\subseteq V\,|\,\ulcorner\text{{$\ell$ is a one-dimensional
subspace}}\urcorner\}.

The twisting bundles are modules over the affine line of
\mathbb{P}(V)
. As such they
are
\mathbb{P}(V)
-indexed families of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-modules, as in
Section
19.4
. We set
X\vcentcolon=\mathbb{P}(V)
.

Definition 20.6
.

For
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, the
twisting bundle

\mathcal{O}(m)
is
the
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{X}}
-module
(\ell^{\otimes(-m)})_{\ell{:}X}
. In particular, the

tautological bundle

\mathcal{O}(-1)
is
(\ell)_{\ell{:}X}
and its dual
is
\mathcal{O}(1)=(\ell^{\vee})_{\ell{:}X}
.

We stress that Definition
20.6
formalizes the common
geometric intuition about the twisting bundles. In algebraic geometry, one
often pictures a bundle as the collection of its fibers, well knowing that this
picture is formally incomplete and that a bundle is not really determined by
the collection of its fibers. In our account of synthetic algebraic geometry,
the statement that a bundle is given by its collection of fibers is
just
true
, and in fact is so by definition.

Proposition 20.7
.

(1)

The canonical map
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}(0))=[X,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]
is an isomorphism.

(2)

The canonical map
V^{\vee}\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}(1)),\,\vartheta\mapsto(\vartheta|_{\ell})_{\ell{:}X}
is an isomorphism.

(3)

The module
\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}(-1))
is zero.

For notational convenience, we assume that
V
is isomorphic
to
({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{2}
. In this case
X=\{[x\mathbin{:}y]\,|\,x\neq 0\vee y\neq 0\}
. Let
X=U_{0}\cup U_{1}
be the standard open covering with

U_{0}=\{[x\mathbin{:}y]\,|\,x\neq 0\}=\{[1:y]\,|\,y\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\}\cong{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}

and similarly with
U_{1}
.

For the first claim, let
f:X\to{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
be an arbitrary function.
Since
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is synthetically quasicoherent, the canonical morphism
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T]\longrightarrow[U_{0},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]
is bijective. Hence there is a unique polynomial
p_{0}\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T]
such that
f([x\mathbin{:}y])=p_{0}(y/x)
for all
[x\mathbin{:}y]\in U_{0}
.
Analogously, there is a unique polynomial
p_{1}\,{:}\,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T]
such that
f([x\mathbin{:}y])=p_{1}(x/y)
for all
[x\mathbin{:}y]\in U_{1}
.

Since
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is synthetically quasicoherent, the canonical morphism

{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T,T^{-1}]\longrightarrow[U_{0}\cap U_{1},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]

is bijective. Because
p_{0}(y/x)=p_{1}(x/y)
as functions on
U_{0}\cap U_{1}
, the
polynomials
p_{0}(T)
and
p_{1}(1/T)
agree as elements of
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T,T^{-1}]
.
Hence
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
, and thus
f
, are constant.

This shows that the canonical morphism
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}\to[X,{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]
is surjective.
This morphism is trivially injective since
X
is inhabited.

The other claims are verified similarly.
∎

In the same spirit as in the proof of Proposition
20.7
,
we can compute the cohomology of the twisting bundles, if we define it as
the cohomology of the Čech complex associated to the standard open covering.
We want to indicate how the general idea of the usual proof can be carried out
in the synthetic account.

For notational convenience, we assume that
V
is isomorphic to
({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{2}
.
The Čech complex for computing
H^{\bullet}(X,\mathcal{O}(-2))
is

0\longrightarrow\Gamma(U_{0},\mathcal{O}(-2))\times\Gamma(U_{1},\mathcal{O}(-2))\longrightarrow\Gamma(U_{0}\cap U_{1},\mathcal{O}(-2))\longrightarrow 0.

The differential maps
(f,g)
to
g|_{U_{0}\cap U_{1}}-f|_{U_{0}\cap U_{1}}
.

The verification that
H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(-2))
is zero proceeds similarly to the proof of
Proposition
20.7
. To verify that
H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}(-2))\cong{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
, we explicitly describe the isomorphism. Given a function
h\,{:}\,\Gamma(U_{0}\cap U_{1},\mathcal{O}(-2))
, there is for any number
y\,{:}\,({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{\times}
a
unique value
h_{0}(y)
such that

h([1\mathbin{:}y])=h_{0}(y)\cdot\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
y\end{pmatrix}\otimes\begin{pmatrix}1\\
y\end{pmatrix}\right)\,{:}\,\operatorname{span}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
y\end{pmatrix}\right)^{\otimes 2}.

By the principle of unique choice, the mapping
y\mapsto h_{0}(y)

defines a well-defined function on
({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{\times}
as the notation suggests.
Because
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
is synthetically quasicoherent, the canonical map

{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T,T^{-1}]\longrightarrow[U_{0}\cap U_{1},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}]

is bijective. Hence there is a unique Laurent polynomial
p_{0}\in{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[T,T^{-1}]
such that
h_{0}(y)=p_{0}(y)
for all
y\in({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{\times}
. The sought isomorphism maps
h
to the coefficient of
T^{-1}

in
p_{0}
.

20.3.
Grassmannian

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a finite locally free
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module.
We want to illustrate the synthetic approach by verifying the basic fact that the Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}(\mathcal{V},r)
of
rank-
r
locally free quotients of
\mathcal{V}
, defined as a certain functor of points,
is representable by a locally finitely presented
S
-scheme using the internal
language of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
.

Definition 20.8
.

The
Grassmannian

\mathrm{Gr}(\mathcal{V},r)
is the functor which associates
to an
S
-scheme
f:T\to S
the set

\mathrm{Gr}(\mathcal{V},r)(T)\vcentcolon=\{\text{$U\subseteq f^{*}\mathcal{V}$ sub-$\mathcal{O}_{T}$-module}\,|\,\text{$(f^{*}\mathcal{V})/U$ is locally free of rank\penalty\ $r$}\}.

Definition 20.9
.

The
synthetic Grassmannian
of rank-
r
quotients of a
module
V
is the set

\mathrm{Gr}(V,r)\vcentcolon=\{\text{$U\subseteq V$ submodule}\,|\,\text{$V/U$ is free of rank\penalty\ $r$}\}.

We could just as well define the synthetic Grassmannian somewhat more directly
as the set of free rank
r
-quotients (up to isomorphism). This set is canonically
isomorphic to the Grassmannian as we chose to define it, by mapping a
quotient
\pi:V\twoheadrightarrow Q
to the kernel of
\pi
.

Proposition 20.10
.

The synthetic Grassmannian of
\mathcal{V}
, as constructed by the internal
language of
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
where
\mathcal{V}
looks like an ordinary free module, coincides
with the functorially defined Grassmannian.

Immediate from
Definition
2.8
and
Proposition
18.1
.
∎

Having established that the internally constructed synthetic Grassmannian
actually describes the external Grassmannian which we’re interested in, we can
switch to a fully internal perspective. We’ll reflect this switch notationally
by referring to the
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-module
V\vcentcolon=\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
instead of
\mathcal{V}
.

We define for any free submodule
W\subseteq V
of rank
r
the subset

G_{W}\vcentcolon=\{U\in\mathrm{Gr}(V,r)\,|\,\text{$W\to V\to V/U$ is bijective}\}.

This sets admits a more concrete description, since it is in canonical bijection
to the set

G_{W}^{\prime}\vcentcolon=\{\pi:V\to W\,|\,\pi\circ\iota=\mathrm{id}\}

of all splittings of the inclusion
\iota:W\hookrightarrow V
: An element
U\in G_{W}
corresponds to the splitting
V\twoheadrightarrow V/U\xrightarrow{({\cong})^{-1}}W
. Conversely, a splitting
\pi
corresponds to
U\vcentcolon=\ker(\pi)\in G_{W}
.

Proposition 20.11
.

The union of the subsets
G_{W}
is
\mathrm{Gr}(V,r)
.

Let
U\in\mathrm{Gr}(V,r)
. Then there exists a
basis
([v_{1}],\ldots,[v_{r}])
of
V/U
. The family
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})
is
linearly independent in
V
, therefore the submodule
W\vcentcolon=\operatorname{span}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})\subseteq V
is free of rank
r
. The
canonical linear map
W\hookrightarrow V\twoheadrightarrow V/U
maps
the basis
(v_{i})_{i}
to the basis
([v_{i}])_{i}
and is therefore bijective. Thus
U\in G_{W}
.∎

Proposition 20.12
.

The sets
G_{W}
are (quasicompact-)open subsets of
\mathrm{Gr}(V,r)
.

Let
U\in\mathrm{Gr}(V,r)
. Then
U\in G_{W}
if and only if
the canonical linear map
W\hookrightarrow V\twoheadrightarrow V/U

is bijective. Since
W
and
V/U
are both free modules of rank
r
, this map is
given by an
(r\times r)
-matrix
M
over
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
; therefore it’s bijective if
and only if the determinant of
M
is invertible.

Thus we’ve found a number which is invertible if and only if
U\in G_{W}
. By
Corollary
19.14
, the truth value of “
U\in G_{W}
” is open.∎

Proposition 20.13
.

The sets
G_{W}
are synthetic affine schemes. Moreover, the algebras which the
G_{W}
are
spectra of are finitely presented.

The set of all linear maps
V\to W
is the spectrum of
the
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebra
A\vcentcolon=\mathrm{Sym}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}(V,W)^{\vee})
, since

\displaystyle\Spec(A)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Alg}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}(\mathrm{Sym}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Mod}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}(V,W)^{\vee}),{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Mod}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Mod}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}(V,W)^{\vee},{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Mod}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}(V,W)^{\vee\vee}

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Mod}({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})}(V,W).

In the last step the assumption that not only
W
, but also
V
is a free module
of finite rank enters. (This is the first time in this development that we need
this assumption.)

The set
G_{W}^{\prime}
is a closed subset of this spectrum, namely the locus where the
generic linear map
V\to W
is a splitting of the inclusion
\iota:W\hookrightarrow V
. If we choose bases of
V
and
W
,
whereby
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}(V,W)^{\vee})
is isomorphic
to
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}[M_{11},\ldots,M_{rn}]
, we can be more explicit: The set
G_{W}^{\prime}
is
isomorphic to

\Spec(k[M_{11},\ldots,M_{rn}]/(MN-I)),

where
I
is the
(r\times r)
identity matrix,
M
is the generic matrix
M=(M_{ij})_{ij}
, and
N
is the matrix of
\iota
with respect to the chosen
bases. The notation “
(MN-I)
” denotes the ideal generated by the entries
of
MN-I
.
∎

Corollary 20.14
.

The Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}(V,r)
is a locally finitely presented
quasicompact synthetic scheme.

We need to verify that
\mathrm{Gr}(V,r)
admits a finite covering by
spectra of finitely presented
{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}
-algebras. We already know that
\mathrm{Gr}(V,r)

can be covered by the open subsets
G_{W}
and that these sets are spectra of
finitely presented algebras. Therefore it remains to prove that finitely many of
these subsets suffice to cover
\mathrm{Gr}(V,r)
.

In fact, if we choose an isomorphism
V\cong({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{n}
, we see
that
\binom{n}{r}
of these subsets suffice: namely those where
W
is one of
the standard submodules of
({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{n}
(generated by the standard basis
vectors). For if
U\in\mathrm{Gr}(({\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}})^{n},r)
, the surjection
V\to V/U
maps
the basis of at least one of these standard submodules to a basis and is
therefore bijective. This is because from any surjective
(r\times n)
-matrix
over a local ring one can select
r
columns which form an linearly independent
family.
∎

Proposition 20.15
.

Let
U\in\mathrm{Gr}(V,r)
. Then the tangent space at
U
is given by

T_{U}\mathrm{Gr}(V,r)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}}(U,V/U)
.

For notational simplicity, we verify the claim in the case
r=1
, in
which case
\mathrm{Gr}(V,r)
is the projectivization of
V
. Let
\gamma:\Delta\to\mathbb{P}(V)
be a tangent vector with base point
\ell\vcentcolon=\gamma(0)\,{:}\,\mathbb{P}(V)
.
By Corollary
19.21
and
Corollary
20.14
, there’s a
lift
\overline{\gamma}:\Delta\to V
such that
\gamma(\varepsilon)=\operatorname{span}(\overline{\gamma}(\varepsilon))
for all
\varepsilon\,{:}\,\Delta
.
We define a linear map
\alpha:\ell\to V/\ell
by setting

x\longmapsto\alpha(x)=[x/\overline{\gamma}(0)\cdot\overline{\gamma}^{\prime}(0)].

The expression “
x/\overline{\gamma}(0)
” should be read as follows: The
vector
x
, being an element of
\ell
, is some multiple
\lambda

of
\overline{\gamma}(0)
. The expression “
x/\overline{\gamma}(0)
” denotes
this unique number
\lambda
. It can be checked that the vector
\alpha(x)\,{:}\,V/\ell
does not depend on the choice of the lifting
\overline{\gamma}
. The
element
\alpha
is therefore a well-defined element
of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}(\ell,V/\ell)
.

Conversely, let an element
\alpha\,{:}\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\text{\textul{$\mathbb{A}$}}^{1}_{S}}(\ell,V/\ell)
be given. We
choose vectors
x_{0}\,{:}\,V
and
z\,{:}\,V
such that
\ell=\operatorname{span}(x_{0})
and
\alpha(x_{0})=[z]
and define
\gamma:\Delta\to\mathbb{P}(V)
by setting

\varepsilon\longmapsto\gamma(\varepsilon)=\operatorname{span}(x_{0}+\varepsilon z).

The definition of
\gamma(\varepsilon)
is invariant under scaling of
x_{0}
and
also under changing
z
to some other vector
z+\lambda x_{0}
in its
equivalence class:

\displaystyle\operatorname{span}(x_{0}+\varepsilon(z+\lambda x_{0}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{span}((1+\varepsilon\lambda)x_{0}+\varepsilon z)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{span}(x_{0}+\varepsilon/(1+\varepsilon\lambda)z)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{span}(x_{0}+\varepsilon z),

since
\varepsilon/(1+\varepsilon\lambda)=\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon\lambda)=\varepsilon
. Therefore
\gamma
is a well-defined element
of
T_{\ell}\mathbb{P}(V)
which only depends on
\alpha
and not on
the arbitrary choices of
x_{0}
and
z
.

One can check that the two described constructions are mutually inverse.
∎

Remark 20.16
.

The arguments given in this section are intended to be
applied internally to the big Zariski topos of a base scheme. However, one can
also apply them internally to well-adapted models for synthetic differential
geometry. In this way, one almost obtains that the differential-geometric
Grassmannian can be represented as a manifold; only a verification of
smoothness is missing.

21.
Beyond the Zariski topology

The Zariski topology is of course not the only interesting topology
on
\mathrm{Sch}/S
. For any finer topology
\tau
, such as the Nisnevich, étale, or fppf
topology (a valuable hyperlinked chart of the various topologies is located
at
[
21
]
), the big
\tau
-topos of
S
, that is the topos
of sheaves on
\mathrm{Sch}/S
with respect to
\tau
, is a subtopos
of the big Zariski topos. Therefore there is a modal operator
\Box_{\tau}

in
\mathrm{Zar}(S)
reflecting the topology
\tau
. Explicitly, for an
S
-scheme
T

and a formula
\varphi
over
T
, the meaning of

T\models\Box_{\tau}\varphi

is that there exists a
\tau
-covering
(T_{i}\to T)_{i}
of
T
such that
T_{i}\models\varphi
for all
i
(where parameters appearing in
\varphi
have to
be pulled back along
T_{i}\to T
). Succinctly, the formula “
\Box_{\tau}\varphi
” means that
\varphi
holds
\tau
-locally. Generalizing
Theorem
6.31
from sheaves on locales to
